🎭 Backstage — Complete Learning Guide

Developer Portal Platform with anihpj Django Project (jobpost app)

From complete beginner to CBA-certified Backstage practitioner. Learn the Software Catalog, Templates, TechDocs, Plugins, Kubernetes integration, authentication, and production deployment — all through hands-on examples with a real Django application.

🟢 Beginner Friendly 🎭 Backstage v1.30+ 🐍 Django / Python 📚 32 Chapters ⏱️ ~40 Hours 🎓 CBA Exam — 100% Coverage 📋 9 Appendices

🚀 Your Backstage Learning Journey

🎯 Welcome! This guide takes you from complete beginner to CBA-certified Backstage practitioner. Every concept is explained with real examples using the anihpj/jobpost Django project — you'll learn by doing, not just reading.

🗺️ Master Summary & Roadmap — Your Backstage Journey

🎭 What is Backstage?

Backstage is an open-source platform for building developer portals, created by Spotify and now a CNCF Graduated project. Think of it as the "operating system" for your engineering organization — one unified interface where developers discover services, create new projects, read documentation, and monitor their entire software ecosystem.

🧠 Remember

Backstage = Catalog (find services) + Templates (create projects) + TechDocs (read docs) + Plugins (extend infinitely). All accessible from ONE interface.

🏛️ The 4 Pillars of Backstage

📦
Software Catalog

"What do we have?" — Track every service, API, team, and resource in one auto-updating registry.

🚀
Software Templates

"How do we create?" — Scaffold new projects in minutes with standardized, production-ready templates.

📖
TechDocs

"How do we use it?" — Documentation that lives with your code, auto-published on every push.

🔌
Plugins

"How do we extend?" — 200+ plugins for CI/CD, monitoring, incidents — build your own for anything.

🛫 The Airport Analogy — How Backstage Organizes Your Engineering Org

Backstage = A Modern Airport Hub
              🛫 THE AIRPORT (Backstage)

  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
  │ 📋 Flight   │  │ 🧳 Baggage  │  │ 🛍️ Duty-Free│
  │ Information │  │   Claim     │  │   Shops     │
  │ (Catalog)   │  │ (Templates) │  │  (Plugins)  │
  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        🛂 SECURITY CHECKPOINT (Auth/RBAC)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  🛫 AIRPORT SERVICES                  BACKSTAGE FEATURES
  ─────────────────────────────────    ────────────────────
  Flight Info Board (Arrivals/Dep)  →  Software Catalog
  Luggage Handling System           →  Software Templates
  Duty-Free Shops & Restaurants     →  Plugin Ecosystem
  Security Checkpoint               →  Authentication/RBAC
  Information Kiosks                →  TechDocs/Search
  Air Traffic Control Tower         →  Backstage Backend
  Terminal Gates                    →  Entity Pages

  💡 Just like an airport connects passengers to flights,
     Backstage connects developers to their tools & services

🏬 The Shopping Mall Analogy

Backstage = A Massive Shopping Mall
           🏬 THE BACKSTAGE MALL

  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐
  │ Nike   │ │ Apple  │ │  Zara  │ │  H&M   │
  │(GitHub)│ │(Jenkins)│ │(Sentry)│ │(Jira)  │
  └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     🗺️ MALL DIRECTORY (Software Catalog)    │
  │    "Find any store/service in seconds"      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Each "store" = A tool integrated into Backstage
  Mall directory = Software Catalog
  New store builder = Software Templates
  Mall map kiosk = Search
  Security guards = RBAC/Auth

  💡 Just like a mall brings ALL stores under ONE roof,
     Backstage brings ALL developer tools under ONE UI

              ┌──────────────────────────┐

              │   (Software Templates)    │
              └────────────┬─────────────┘

    ┌──────────┐          │          ┌──────────┐

  └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘

🔄 Developer Experience — Before vs After Backstage

❌ WITHOUT Backstage

Developer must juggle 10-15 different tools daily:

  • GitHub → find repo (10 min)
  • Jenkins → check CI (15 min)
  • Confluence → find docs (20 min)
  • Jira → find tickets (10 min)
  • PagerDuty → on-call (5 min)
  • Slack → ask questions (15 min)
  • Grafana → check metrics (10 min)
  • Sentry → check errors (10 min)
  • kubectl → check K8s (10 min)

⏱️ ~2 hours/day lost to context switching

✅ WITH Backstage

Developer opens ONE interface:

  • 📦 Catalog → find any service (30s)
  • 🔄 CI/CD card → build status (10s)
  • 📖 TechDocs → read docs (10s)
  • 📋 Jira card → tickets visible (5s)
  • 📞 PagerDuty card → on-call (5s)
  • 📊 Grafana card → metrics (5s)
  • 🐛 Sentry card → errors (5s)
  • ☸️ K8s tab → deployment status (5s)

⏱️ ~10 min/day — 80% reduction in context switching!

🏗️ Architecture at a Glance

🖥️
Frontend
React + TypeScript + Material UI — Plugin-based UI with customizable theming
⚙️
Backend
Node.js + Express — Plugin services, Catalog ingestion, Auth, Search indexing
💾
Database
PostgreSQL (production) or SQLite (dev) — Entities, settings, plugin state

🐍 anihpj Project Showcase — Your Hands-On Demo

Throughout this guide, we'll use the anihpj Django project with the jobpost application as our hands-on demonstration. anihpj is a job posting platform with these models:

👤
User

Job seekers and employers

✍️
Author

Content creators and posters

📍
Location

Job locations and regions

💼
JobPost

Job listings with skills & filters

🛠️
Skills

Required and optional skills

We'll build a complete Backstage portal for anihpj, including Catalog entities, Software Templates for new Django apps, TechDocs for documentation, and Kubernetes deployment monitoring.

Backstage Infrastructure 22%

📋
Backstage Catalog 22%

  • Populate Backstage Catalog with entities

📅 8-Week CBA Study Plan

Week 1-2: 🟢 Foundations

Ch 1-6: What is Backstage, Architecture, Setup, CLI, Configuration

Week 3-4: 🟡 Software Catalog

Ch 7-10: Catalog, Entities, YAML, K8s Integration

Week 5: 🟡 Templates

📚 Part 1: Foundation — Understanding Backstage 🟢 Beginner

Chapter 1: What is Backstage? (The Big Picture)

Before diving into creating portals and writing YAML, let's understand why Backstage exists, what problems it solves for engineering organizations, and why the CBA certification focuses on making you a Backstage practitioner. We'll use our anihpj/jobpost Django project to make every concept concrete.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📖 Conceptual 🎯 Big Picture

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The chaos before Backstage — scattered tools, lost docs, 2-week onboarding
  • ✅ What Backstage actually is — the simple, clear definition
  • ✅ The 8 key benefits: Discovery, Self-Service, Docs-as-Code, Single Pane, Plugins, K8s Native, GitOps, Enterprise
  • ✅ The 4 Pillars of Backstage — Catalog, Templates, TechDocs, Plugins
  • ✅ How Backstage compares to other developer portals
  • ✅ Real companies using Backstage at massive scale
  • ✅ The CNCF journey — from Spotify internal tool to graduated project
🎓 CBA Relevance
Development Workflow 24%
Understanding "Why" Matters on the CBA

The CBA tests your understanding of why Backstage exists and what problems it solves, not just how to configure it. You'll be asked about the value proposition, key use cases, and the problems Backstage addresses. Key concepts to internalize:

  • Developer Experience (DX) — Backstage is fundamentally about improving developer productivity by reducing cognitive load and context switching.
  • Single Pane of Glass — The concept that all your tools, services, and docs should be accessible from ONE interface.
  • Self-Service — Backstage enables developers to create, deploy, and manage services without opening tickets or waiting for ops teams.

1.1 The Problem Backstage Solves

Imagine you've built anihpj — a Django app with a jobpost module where employers list jobs filtered by Skills, Location, and Author. It's part of a growing microservices ecosystem. But as your engineering org grows from 5 to 50 to 500 developers, chaos emerges.

📖 Key Term: Developer Portal

A Developer Portal is a centralized web application that serves as the front door to your engineering ecosystem. It provides discovery (find services, APIs, docs), self-service (create new projects), and observability (see build status, deployments, errors) — all in one place. Backstage is the leading open-source developer portal platform.

The Developer Experience Crisis

Before Backstage, large engineering organizations faced a common set of problems that collectively created a terrible developer experience:

😰 A Developer's Day WITHOUT Backstage
  • 🔴 "Where is the anihpj repo?" → Search GitHub... 10 min lost
  • 🔴 "What's the CI/CD pipeline?" → Check Jenkins... 15 min lost
  • 🔴 "Who owns jobpost service?" → Ask in Slack... 20 min waiting
  • 🔴 "Where are the API docs?" → Search Confluence... 30 min lost
  • 🔴 "How do I create a new service?" → Find template... 45 min lost
  • 🔴 "What's the PagerDuty for this?" → Ask ops team... 15 min lost
  • 🔴 "What K8s namespace is jobpost in?" → kubectl search... 10 min
  • ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  • 📊 TOTAL TIME LOST: ~2.5 HOURS just finding information!
🎉 A Developer's Day WITH Backstage
  • ✅ "Search for anihpj" → Software Catalog... 30 seconds
  • ✅ "CI/CD status" → Built-in plugin widget... 10 seconds
  • ✅ "Who owns jobpost?" → Entity page → Ownership card... 5 seconds
  • ✅ "API docs" → TechDocs tab → auto-published... 10 seconds
  • ✅ "New microservice" → Create → Template → scaffold... 2 minutes
  • ✅ "On-call info" → PagerDuty plugin card... 5 seconds
  • ✅ "K8s deployment" → K8s tab on entity page... 5 seconds
  • ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  • 📊 TOTAL TIME: ~3 MINUTES — NOT 2.5 HOURS!

The Cognitive Load Problem

Every new tool a developer must use adds cognitive load — the mental effort of remembering URLs, credentials, UIs, and workflows. Before Backstage, developers juggled 10-15 different tools daily. Backstage reduces this to ONE interface.

10-15

1
Interface After Backstage
80%
Context Switching Reduction
2hrs→2min
Onboarding Time Improvement

Spotify's Story — The Origin

Backstage was born at Spotify around 2016. With 100+ autonomous squads and 1,000+ microservices, Spotify's developers were drowning in complexity. Every squad used different tools, documentation was scattered across wikis, and new developers took weeks to become productive. Spotify's Platform team built an internal developer portal called "Backstage" (named after the area behind the stage at concerts) to solve this. In March 2020, Spotify open-sourced it and donated it to the CNCF.

Spotify's Problem → Backstage Solution
  SPOTIFY BEFORE BACKSTAGE (2016)          SPOTIFY AFTER BACKSTAGE (2020+)
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │  100+ Squads · 1000+ Services│        │  🎭 BACKSTAGE PORTAL          │
  │                              │        │                              │
  │  Squad A: GitHub + Jenkins   │        │  ┌────────────────────────┐  │
  │  Squad B: GitLab + Travis CI │        │  │ One place to:          │  │
  │  Squad C: Bitbucket + Circle │   ──▶  │  │ • Find any service     │  │
  │  Docs: 15 different wikis    │        │  │ • Create new services  │  │
  │  Dashboards: Grafana links   │        │  │ • Read documentation   │  │
  │  scattered across Slack      │        │  │ • See build status     │  │
  │  Onboarding: 2-3 weeks       │        │  │ • Check K8s deployments │  │
  │  "Where's the X service?"    │        │  │ • Know who owns what    │  │
  │  (asked 100x/day in Slack)   │        │  └────────────────────────┘  │
  │                              │        │  Onboarding: 2-3 DAYS        │
  └──────────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────────────┘

1.2 Backstage Defined — The Simple Answer

📖 Official Definition

"An open platform for building developer portals."

In plain English: Backstage is a centralized hub where developers can find, create, and manage everything related to their software — services, documentation, CI/CD pipelines, Kubernetes deployments, and more — all from a single, unified interface.

Quick Facts

🎭
Name Origin

"Backstage" refers to the area behind the stage at concerts. Like a concert's backstage, it's where everything is orchestrated behind the scenes.

🎵
Born at Spotify

Created internally around 2016 to solve Spotify's microservices chaos. Open-sourced in March 2020.

🔤
Written in TypeScript

Built with TypeScript + React (frontend) and Node.js (backend). Plugin-based architecture.

📜
Apache 2.0 License

Fully open-source. Free to use, modify, and distribute commercially.

Backstage — The Elevator Pitch
  "Backstage is the ANDROID/iOS OF DEVELOPER TOOLS"

  📱 Android/iOS gives you:
     • App Store (discover apps)       → 📦 Software Catalog (discover services)
     • SDK for building apps           → 🔌 Plugin framework
     • Home screen widgets             → 🖥️ Dashboard cards
     • System settings                 → ⚙️ Configuration

  🎭 Backstage gives your engineering org:
     • Discover all services, APIs, docs in ONE place
     • Scaffold new projects with Software Templates
     • Document everything with TechDocs (docs-as-code)
     • Extend infinitely with 200+ plugins

  💡 Backstage = Operating System for your Engineering Organization

1.3 Key Benefits of Backstage

🔍
Centralized Discovery

Find any service, API, team, or resource in seconds. The Software Catalog is a searchable, auto-updating registry of everything your org builds and runs.

🚀
Developer Self-Service

Scaffold new projects in minutes with Software Templates. No more waiting for ops tickets — developers click "Create" and get a production-ready repo with CI/CD, K8s manifests, and docs.

📖
Documentation-as-Code

Docs live in your repo next to your code. When you push to Git, TechDocs auto-publishes beautifully rendered documentation. Docs that stay with the code stay up-to-date.

🖥️
Single Pane of Glass

GitHub, Jenkins, PagerDuty, Sentry, Grafana, Jira, Slack — all visible in ONE interface. No more juggling 15 browser tabs just to understand one service.

🔌
Plugin Ecosystem (200+)

Extend Backstage with 200+ community plugins. Need CI/CD visibility? Install the Jenkins plugin. Need incident management? Add PagerDuty. Build custom plugins for YOUR needs.

☸️
Kubernetes Native

Auto-discover K8s resources. See pod health, replica counts, and service endpoints directly on the entity page. Backstage runs on K8s and understands K8s.

🔄
GitOps Friendly

Everything is declared in YAML files stored in Git. Configuration changes go through PR reviews. The Catalog auto-syncs from GitHub. True infrastructure-as-code.

🏢
Enterprise Ready

SSO with Okta/Azure AD/GitHub, RBAC permissions, audit logging. Backstage scales to thousands of developers and hundreds of teams.

How does Backstage compare to other approaches for building a developer portal?

💡 When Backstage Makes the Most Sense

Backstage shines when you have: (1) 50+ developers, (2) 20+ microservices, (3) Multiple teams needing to discover each other's services, (4) A desire to standardize how new projects are created.

When Backstage Might NOT Be the Right Choice

🟡 Should you use Backstage? Ask yourself:
Do you have 50+ developers?
❌ NO → A wiki or README files may suffice
Small team, simple needs
✅ YES → Continue to next question
Scale justifies a portal
Do you have 20+ services/APIs?
❌ NO → GitHub + README docs are enough
Few services, easy to track
✅ YES → Continue to next question
Need service discovery
Are your developers spending >1 hour/day context switching?
❌ NO → Current tools are sufficient
Low cognitive load
✅ YES → Backstage is a GREAT fit!
High ROI from unification
⚠️ Common Adoption Pitfalls

Don't try to integrate EVERY tool at once. Start with the Catalog (auto-discover services from GitHub), then add Templates, then TechDocs, then plugins. A phased rollout prevents overwhelming your team. Also, Backstage requires at least 1-2 dedicated platform engineers to maintain — it's not a "set up and forget" tool.

1.9 Developer Lifecycle with Backstage

🚀 Day 1: Onboarding

New developer signs into Backstage via SSO. Immediately sees their team's services in the Catalog. Reads TechDocs to understand the architecture. Has full context in hours, not weeks.

💻 Day 2: First Contribution

Finds the jobpost repo in the Catalog. Clones it. Sees CI/CD status on the entity page. Makes a PR. Watches the build pass in Backstage's CI/CD card. First contribution done!

🏗️ Week 2: Creating a New Service

Clicks "Create" in Backstage. Chooses the Django Microservice template. Fills in the service name and owner. Clicks "Create". In 2 minutes: GitHub repo created, CI/CD configured, K8s manifests ready, Catalog entity registered.

📊 Ongoing: Daily Work

Starts each day on the Backstage dashboard. Checks CI/CD status, K8s pod health, Sentry errors, Jira tickets, and PagerDuty schedule — all in ONE tab. No more context switching.

Spotify builds internal "Backstage" prototype to solve microservices chaos across 100+ squads.

📝 Chapter 1 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the primary problem that Backstage solves for engineering organizations?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage solves the developer experience (DX) crisis in large engineering organizations. Before Backstage, developers had to juggle 10-15 different tools (GitHub, Jenkins, Confluence, PagerDuty, Sentry, Grafana, Jira, Slack, kubectl, etc.) just to understand and work with a single service. Backstage provides a single pane of glass — one unified interface where developers can discover services, read documentation, check CI/CD status, and manage deployments without context switching.

Explanation

This is the core value proposition of Backstage. The CBA exam tests your understanding of WHY Backstage exists, not just HOW to configure it. Key phrase: "single pane of glass" — all tools visible in one interface, reducing cognitive load and context switching by ~80%.

Q2
What are the four pillars of Backstage? Briefly describe each one.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Software Catalog — The single source of truth for all software. Answers "What do we have?" Tracks services, APIs, websites, libraries, databases, and teams. Auto-discovers from GitHub, GitLab, K8s.

2. Software Templates — Scaffolding engine. Answers "How do we create?" Developers click "Create", fill a form, and get a production-ready repo with CI/CD, K8s manifests, and docs in minutes.

3. TechDocs — Documentation-as-code. Answers "How do we use it?" Docs written in Markdown, stored in the same repo as code, auto-published on git push. Powered by MkDocs.

4. Plugins — Extensibility framework. Answers "How do we extend?" 200+ community plugins for CI/CD, monitoring, incident management, project management. Build custom plugins for your organization.

Explanation

These four pillars are the foundation of Backstage. Every feature falls into one of these categories. The CBA exam expects you to understand what each pillar does, which problems it solves, and how they work together to create a complete developer portal.

Q3
Which company originally created Backstage, and why was it built?
Show Answer
Answer

Spotify created Backstage internally around 2016. It was built to solve the chaos of managing 100+ autonomous squads and 1,000+ microservices. Each squad used different tools, documentation was scattered across 15 different wikis, and new developers took 2-3 weeks to become productive. Spotify's platform team built "Backstage" (named after the backstage area at concerts) as a unified developer portal to bring order to this chaos. In March 2020, Spotify open-sourced it and donated it to the CNCF.

Explanation

Understanding Backstage's origin story is important for the CBA exam. Spotify (not Google, not Netflix) created it. The name "Backstage" comes from concert terminology — the area behind the stage where everything is orchestrated. Key fact: it was open-sourced in March 2020.

Q4
What is the CNCF, and what is Backstage's status within it?
Show Answer
Answer

The Cloud Native Computing Foundation (CNCF) is a Linux Foundation project that hosts critical cloud-native open-source projects like Kubernetes, Prometheus, Envoy, and Helm. Backstage was donated to the CNCF in 2020 as a Sandbox project. It progressed to Incubating in 2022 and Graduated in 2024 — the highest maturity level in CNCF, shared by projects like Kubernetes, Prometheus, and Envoy.

Explanation

CNCF graduation is a big deal — it signals that Backstage is production-ready, has a healthy community, and follows best practices for governance and security. The CBA exam may ask about Backstage's CNCF status and what graduation means.

Q5
What is meant by "Developer Self-Service" in the context of Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Developer Self-Service means that developers can create, deploy, and manage services and infrastructure on their own — without filing tickets, waiting for ops approvals, or depending on other teams. In Backstage, this is primarily enabled through Software Templates, which allow developers to click "Create", fill in a few parameters (service name, owner, description), and automatically get a production-ready GitHub repository with CI/CD pipeline configured, Kubernetes manifests generated, documentation scaffolded, and the new service registered in the Software Catalog — all in about 2 minutes.

Explanation

Self-service is a core Backstage concept. Instead of a developer waiting 2-3 days for ops to create a repo and configure CI/CD, they do it themselves in 2 minutes. This dramatically accelerates development velocity and reduces friction. The CBA exam tests this concept.

Q6
How does Backstage reduce cognitive load for developers?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage reduces cognitive load by providing a single unified interface for all developer tools. Instead of remembering 10-15 different URLs, UIs, and workflows (GitHub, Jenkins, Confluence, PagerDuty, Sentry, Grafana, Jira, Slack, kubectl, etc.), developers only need to know ONE URL: their Backstage portal. The Catalog auto-discovers and organizes all services, APIs, and teams. TechDocs provides documentation alongside code. Plugins surface CI/CD status, K8s health, errors, and incidents directly on the relevant entity pages. This reduces context switching by approximately 80%.

Explanation

Cognitive load is the mental effort of juggling multiple tools, remembering where things are, and context switching. Backstage addresses this head-on. The CBA exam may ask how Backstage improves developer productivity, and "reducing cognitive load through a single pane of glass" is the key answer.

Q7
What are Software Templates in Backstage and what problem do they solve?
Show Answer
Answer

Software Templates are blueprints that define how to scaffold new projects. A template specifies: (1) what parameters the user fills in (name, owner, description), (2) what actions to execute (fetch skeleton code, create GitHub repo, register in Catalog), and (3) what output is produced (a fully configured, production-ready repository). Templates solve the problem of inconsistent project creation — before templates, every developer created services differently, leading to security gaps, missing CI/CD, and undocumented services. Templates enforce "golden path" standards.

Explanation

Software Templates are one of the 4 pillars. They standardize how new projects are created, enforce best practices, and dramatically reduce the time from idea to production. The CBA exam will ask about template.yaml structure, template actions, and the scaffolder workflow.

Q8
What technology stack is Backstage built on?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage is built on a three-layer architecture:
Frontend: TypeScript + React + Material UI (MUI v5). Handles all UI rendering, plugin components, and user interactions.
Backend: Node.js + Express. Handles API requests, plugin backend logic, catalog ingestion, and authentication.
Database: PostgreSQL (production) or SQLite (development). Stores catalog entities, user preferences, and plugin state.

Explanation

Knowing the technology stack is important for the CBA. Backstage is NOT built on Java, Python, or Go — it's TypeScript/React/Node.js. The backend uses Express.js. Understanding this helps when you need to customize, debug, or extend Backstage with plugins.

Q9
What is the relationship between Backstage and Kubernetes?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage has a dual relationship with Kubernetes:
1. Backstage runs ON Kubernetes: The Backstage application itself is typically deployed on Kubernetes in production, using Deployments, Services, Ingress, ConfigMaps, and Secrets.
2. Backstage understands Kubernetes: The Kubernetes plugin auto-discovers K8s resources (Deployments, Pods, Services, Ingresses) and displays them on the relevant Catalog entity pages, showing pod health, replica counts, and service endpoints.

Explanation

This is a commonly tested CBA concept. Backstage is "K8s-native" — it runs on K8s and understands K8s resources. The K8s plugin uses the backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation to link K8s resources to Catalog entities.

Q10
A company has 200 developers, 80 microservices, and struggles with developers spending hours finding service information across multiple tools. Would Backstage be a good fit? Explain why.
Show Answer
Answer

Yes, Backstage would be an excellent fit. Here's why:
1. 200 developers — Backstage is designed for orgs with 50+ developers. At this scale, the cognitive load of scattered tools is significant.
2. 80 microservices — The Software Catalog would auto-discover and organize all 80 services, making them searchable with ownership, documentation, and dependency information.
3. Hours spent finding information — This is the exact problem Backstage solves. The Catalog provides instant discovery, TechDocs auto-publishes documentation, and plugins surface CI/CD, monitoring, and incident data all in one place.
4. Additional benefits: Software Templates would standardize how new services are created, ensuring all 80+ services follow consistent patterns.

Explanation

This is a classic CBA scenario question. The key indicators that Backstage is a good fit: (1) 50+ developers, (2) 20+ services, (3) multiple teams, (4) scattered tooling causing productivity loss. The answer should reference specific Backstage features (Catalog, TechDocs, Templates, Plugins) that address each pain point.

Chapter 2: Backstage Architecture Deep Dive

Now that you understand why Backstage exists, let's open the hood and see how it works. Understanding the three-layer architecture (Frontend/Backend/Database), the plugin system, and the configuration model is essential for customizing, deploying, and troubleshooting Backstage — all key skills for the CBA exam.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🏗️ Architecture 🎯 CBA 30%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The three-layer architecture: Frontend (React), Backend (Node.js), Database (PostgreSQL)
  • ✅ How plugins mount into the frontend and register routes
  • ✅ The plugin lifecycle: install → configure → register → run
  • ✅ The 5 core Backstage services: Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search, Auth
  • ✅ How a request flows from browser to database and back
  • ✅ The event bus system for inter-plugin communication
  • ✅ The app-config.yaml configuration system and merge hierarchy
🎓 CBA Relevance
Infrastructure 22% Architecture KEY TOPIC
Architecture Questions Are Common on the CBA

The CBA exam expects you to understand the three-layer architecture and how plugins work. You'll be asked about the frontend/backend split, how plugins communicate, and the role of the database. Know the difference between frontend-only, backend-only, and full-stack plugins.

2.1 The Three-Layer Architecture

Backstage follows a clean three-layer architecture that separates concerns between presentation, business logic, and data storage.

🖥️
Frontend (React + MUI)
Plugin UI components, Catalog browser, Template wizard, TechDocs reader, Dashboard cards
⚙️
Backend (Node.js + Express)
Plugin services, Catalog ingestion, Scaffolder engine, Search indexing, Auth & permissions
💾
Database (PostgreSQL / SQLite)
Catalog entities, User preferences, Template execution history, Plugin state storage
📖 Key Concept: Separation of Concerns

The frontend NEVER talks directly to the database. All data flows through the backend API. This keeps the frontend stateless (any browser can connect to any backend instance) and allows the backend to handle authentication, authorization, and data validation before it reaches the database.

2.2 Frontend Architecture — React App Deep Dive

The Backstage frontend is a React single-page application using Material UI (MUI v5) for components. Every feature — the Catalog page, the Create wizard, the Docs reader — is built as a plugin that mounts into the main app.

Frontend Component Tree
  <App>
  ├── <AppRouter>
  │   ├── <Sidebar>           ← Navigation (left side)
  │   ├── <Header>            ← Top bar with search
  │   └── <Routes>
  │       ├── <CatalogPage>   ← /catalog (browse entities)
  │       ├── <CreatePage>    ← /create (scaffold projects)
  │       ├── <DocsPage>      ← /docs (read documentation)
  │       ├── <SettingsPage>  ← /settings (user preferences)
  │       └── <PluginPages>   ← Plugin-registered routes
  └── <PluginProvider>        ← Plugin context and API

Plugin Mounting: Plugins register their routes, sidebar items, and entity page tabs through the Backstage plugin API. When a user navigates to a plugin's route, React renders the plugin's components. Plugins can also extend the entity page with additional tabs and cards.

💡 MUI Theming

Backstage uses Material UI v5 with a customizable theme. You can change colors, fonts, and branding by creating a custom theme in packages/app/src/App.tsx. This is how companies white-label Backstage with their own branding.

2.3 Backend Architecture — The Plugin System

The backend is where the real work happens. It's built on Node.js + Express and follows a plugin-based architecture just like the frontend.

Plugin Lifecycle
  📦 Install ──▶  ⚙️ Configure ──▶  🔌 Register ──▶  ▶️ Run
  (npm/yarn)     (app-config)     (createPlugin)   (active)

  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
  │ @backstage/  │  │ app-config  │  │ createPlugin│
  │ plugin-xxx   │  │   .yaml     │  │   ( {       │
  │              │  │             │  │     id,      │
  │ npm install  │  │ catalog:    │  │     routes,  │
  │              │  │   locations │  │     apis     │
  │              │  │             │  │   } )        │
  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘

The backend plugin system provides:

  • Router: Each plugin can expose REST API endpoints via Express Router
  • Database Access: Plugins access the database through PluginDatabaseManager
  • URL Reader: Plugins can read files from GitHub, GitLab, or any URL via UrlReader
  • Event System: Plugins communicate via a pub/sub event bus
  • Scheduler: Plugins can schedule recurring tasks (like catalog ingestion)

2.4 Database Schema Overview

Backstage uses knex.js as its database query builder, which supports PostgreSQL, SQLite, and MySQL. In development, SQLite is used for simplicity. In production, PostgreSQL is required.

TablePurposePlugin
entitiesStores all catalog entities (services, APIs, users, etc.)Catalog
locationsWhere to find entity YAML files (GitHub URLs, file paths)Catalog
final_entitiesProcessed/stitched entity data for fast queryingCatalog
search_indexFull-text search index for entities and docsSearch
scaffolder_tasksTemplate execution history and statusScaffolder
user_settingsPer-user preferences and settingsCore

2.5 Configuration System — app-config.yaml

The app-config.yaml file is the central configuration for your entire Backstage instance. It controls everything: the app title, database connection, auth providers, catalog locations, and plugin settings.

📋 app-config.yaml Key Sections

app
Title, baseUrl, navigation structure, branding
backend
Database connection (client: pg or sqlite3), CORS, cache
catalog
Entity locations (GitHub URLs, file paths), processing rules
scaffolder
Template file locations for the create wizard
techdocs
Builder (local/docker), publisher (local/GCS/S3/Azure), generator
auth
Provider configuration: GitHub, Okta, Azure AD, Google, etc.
integrations
GitHub/GitLab/Bitbucket tokens and API endpoints
⚠️ Configuration Merge Order

Backstage merges multiple config files in this order (later overrides earlier):
1. app-config.yaml (base/default)
2. app-config.local.yaml (local dev overrides)
3. app-config.production.yaml (production overrides)
4. Environment variables (${ENV_VAR} substitution). Never put secrets in YAML files — use environment variables!

2.6 The 5 Core Services

Backstage Core Services

🎭 Backstage
Hub
📦
Catalog Service

Entity ingestion, storage, querying, and stitching. The single source of truth.

🚀
Scaffolder Service

Template execution engine. Runs fetch, publish, and register actions.

📖
TechDocs Service

Documentation generation (MkDocs) and publishing to storage.

🔍
Search Service

Full-text search across entities, docs, and plugin data.

🔐
Auth Service

Authentication (SSO), JWT token management, and permission checks.

2.7 Request Flow Lifecycle — Browser to Database

Full Request Lifecycle
  1️⃣  🌐 Browser                  https://backstage.anihpj.com
      │
      ▼  GET /catalog/default/component/jobpost
  2️⃣  🔀 React Router            Matches route to Catalog Plugin
      │
      ▼
  3️⃣  🖥️  Catalog Plugin UI       Renders EntityPage component
      │                           Calls useApi(catalogApiRef)
      ▼
  4️⃣  🌐 API Client (fetch)       POST /api/catalog/entities/by-name
      │
      ▼
  5️⃣  🔐 Auth Middleware          Validates JWT token
      │                           Checks permissions
      ▼
  6️⃣  ⚙️ Catalog Backend Plugin   Processes request
      │                           Queries database
      ▼
  7️⃣  💾 PostgreSQL DB            SELECT * FROM entities
      │                           WHERE name = 'jobpost'
      ▼
  8️⃣  ⬆️ Response flows back:    DB → Backend → API → Frontend → React → Browser

  ⏱️ Total time: ~50ms (with proper caching)

2.8 Plugin Communication — The Event Bus

Plugin Event Bus Architecture
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │   🔔 Event Bus   │
                    │   (Pub/Sub)      │
                    └──────┬───────────┘
                           │
       ┌───────────────────┼───────────────────┐
       │                   │                   │
       ▼                   ▼                   ▼
  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐
  │ Catalog  │      │ Scaffold │       │  Search  │
  │ Plugin   │      │ Plugin   │       │  Plugin  │
  └──────────┘      └──────────┘       └──────────┘
       │                   │                   │
       │  "entity-created" │  "task-completed" │
       │  ─────────────────▶                   │
       │                   │  "catalog-updated"│
       │                   │  ─────────────────▶
       ▼                   ▼                   ▼
  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐
  │ Re-index │      │ Register │       │ Update   │
  │ Search   │      │ in Cat.  │       │ Index    │
  └──────────┘      └──────────┘       └──────────┘

  💡 Plugins communicate via events, NOT direct calls.
     This keeps them loosely coupled and independently deployable.

Chapter 2 CBA Quick Wins: Remember the three layers: React/MUI → frontend, Node.js/Express → backend, PostgreSQL → database. Plugins use the event bus (pub/sub) to communicate — they never directly import each other. Configuration merges: app-config.yaml → app-config.local.yaml → app-config.production.yaml → env vars.

📊 Chapter 2 — What You Should Remember

🖥️
3-Layer ArchitectureFrontend (React+MUI) → Backend (Node.js+Express) → Database (PostgreSQL/SQLite)
🔌
Plugin LifecycleInstall → Configure → Register → Run. Plugins are the building blocks.
📋
app-config.yamlCentral config file. Merge order: base → local → production → env vars
📡
Event BusPub/sub system for plugin communication. Keeps plugins loosely coupled.
💾
DatabasePostgreSQL for production (concurrent). SQLite only for local development.
🔀
Request FlowBrowser → React Router → Plugin → API Client → Auth → Backend → DB

📝 Chapter 2 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the three layers of the Backstage architecture?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Frontend: TypeScript + React + Material UI. Handles UI rendering, plugin components, routing.
2. Backend: Node.js + Express. Handles API requests, plugin logic, catalog ingestion, auth.
3. Database: PostgreSQL (production) or SQLite (development). Stores entities, settings, plugin state.

Explanation

The three-layer architecture is fundamental to Backstage. The frontend NEVER talks directly to the database — all communication flows through the backend API. This is a common CBA question.

Q2
What is the event bus in Backstage, and why is it important for plugin communication?
Show Answer
Answer

The event bus is a publish/subscribe system that allows plugins to communicate without directly depending on each other. For example, when a new entity is registered via the Scaffolder, the Scaffolder plugin publishes an entity-created event. The Search plugin subscribes to this event and automatically re-indexes the search index. This keeps plugins loosely coupled — they can be developed, tested, and deployed independently.

Explanation

Loose coupling via events is a key architectural pattern in Backstage. Plugins don't import each other's code — they communicate through shared events on the bus. This is what makes the plugin ecosystem work at scale.

Q3
What is the purpose of app-config.yaml, and how does the configuration merge hierarchy work?
Show Answer
Answer

app-config.yaml is the central configuration file for Backstage. The merge hierarchy (later overrides earlier) is:
1. app-config.yaml — Base/default configuration
2. app-config.local.yaml — Local development overrides
3. app-config.production.yaml — Production overrides
4. Environment variables (${ENV_VAR}) — Secrets and deployment-specific values

Explanation

Understanding the config merge order is essential for the CBA. Secrets should ALWAYS be passed via environment variables, never hardcoded in YAML files. The ${} syntax allows referencing env vars in config files.

Q4
Name the 5 core Backstage services and their primary responsibilities.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Catalog Service: Entity ingestion, storage, querying, and relationship stitching.
2. Scaffolder Service: Template execution — runs fetch, publish, and register actions.
3. TechDocs Service: Documentation generation (MkDocs) and publishing to cloud storage.
4. Search Service: Full-text search indexing across entities, docs, and plugin data.
5. Auth Service: Authentication providers, JWT token management, permission enforcement.

Explanation

These 5 services form the backbone of Backstage. Each one is implemented as a backend plugin. Understanding what each service does helps you troubleshoot issues and understand Backstage's capabilities.

Q5
What is knex.js and what role does it play in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

knex.js is a SQL query builder for Node.js. Backstage uses it as its database abstraction layer. It allows Backstage to work with multiple database backends (PostgreSQL, SQLite, MySQL) without changing the application code. Plugins access the database through PluginDatabaseManager, which uses knex under the hood to execute queries and manage migrations.

Explanation

knex.js provides database portability. In development, you use SQLite (zero config). In production, you use PostgreSQL (scalable, reliable). The same code works with both thanks to knex.js. Database migrations are also managed through knex.

Q6
How do frontend plugins register their routes in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Frontend plugins use createRoutableExtension() to register routes. This creates a lazy-loaded route that Backstage's React Router picks up. The plugin also exports a plugin.ts file that defines the plugin's ID and registers its components with the plugin framework. When a user navigates to the plugin's URL, React renders the plugin's main component.

Explanation

Route registration is fundamental to how plugins integrate. createRoutableExtension() enables code-splitting — the plugin's code is only loaded when the user navigates to it, keeping the initial page load fast.

Q7
What role does the UrlReader play in Backstage's backend?
Show Answer
Answer

The UrlReader is a backend service that allows plugins to read files from various sources — GitHub repositories, GitLab, Bitbucket, Azure DevOps, or plain URLs. It handles authentication (using configured tokens), rate limiting, and caching. Plugins like Catalog (to read catalog-info.yaml) and TechDocs (to read Markdown files) rely heavily on UrlReader.

Explanation

UrlReader abstracts away the complexity of accessing files from different Git providers. Without it, every plugin would need to implement its own GitHub/GitLab API client. This is a good example of how Backstage's backend services simplify plugin development.

Q8
What is the difference between SQLite and PostgreSQL for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

SQLite: File-based database, zero configuration. Used for local development only. Not suitable for production because it doesn't support concurrent writes from multiple Backstage instances.
PostgreSQL: Full-featured relational database. Required for production. Supports concurrent connections, replication, and high availability. Multiple Backstage pods can share the same PostgreSQL instance.

Explanation

The CBA exam may ask about production requirements. PostgreSQL is mandatory for production deployments, especially when running multiple Backstage replicas on Kubernetes. SQLite is convenient for local development but cannot handle concurrent access.

Q9
Describe the full lifecycle of a request when a user opens an entity page in Backstage.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Browser sends GET request to Backstage URL.
2. React Router matches the URL to the Catalog plugin's route.
3. Catalog plugin's React component renders the EntityPage.
4. The component calls useApi(catalogApiRef) which makes an API call to /api/catalog/entities/by-name.
5. Auth middleware validates the user's JWT token and checks permissions.
6. Catalog backend plugin processes the request and queries the PostgreSQL database.
7. Database returns the entity data.
8. Response flows back: DB → Backend → API → Frontend → React renders the page.

Explanation

This end-to-end understanding is important for troubleshooting. If something breaks, knowing which layer is involved helps you isolate the problem. For example, if entities aren't showing up, check: (1) Is the catalog backend plugin running? (2) Is the database connected? (3) Is the JWT token valid?

Q10
You're deploying Backstage to production on Kubernetes with 3 replicas. Which database should you use and why?
Show Answer
Answer

You must use PostgreSQL, not SQLite. With 3 replicas, all three Backstage pods will try to read and write to the database simultaneously. SQLite doesn't support concurrent writes — it would cause data corruption and locking issues. PostgreSQL is designed for concurrent access, supports connection pooling, and can be deployed as a separate StatefulSet or managed service (like AWS RDS or Google Cloud SQL).

Explanation

This is a practical CBA scenario. Multi-replica deployments REQUIRE PostgreSQL. The database must be external to the Backstage pods — either a managed cloud database or a separate StatefulSet with PersistentVolume. SQLite is ONLY for local single-instance development.

Chapter 3: Core Concepts & Terminology

Before you can build with Backstage, you need to speak its language. This chapter covers the essential terminology: entities, kinds, the YAML format, annotations, and the entity lifecycle. These concepts are the foundation for everything that follows.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 Terminology 🎯 CBA 30%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Key terms: Entity, Kind, Spec, Location, Annotation, Plugin, Template, Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group
  • ✅ All 9 entity kinds and when to use each one
  • ✅ The entity lifecycle: Registration → Processing → Stitching → Discovery
  • ✅ The catalog-info.yaml format: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec
  • ✅ Annotations: How to link entities to external systems (GitHub, K8s, PagerDuty, etc.)
🎓 CBA Relevance
Development Workflow 24% Backstage Catalog 22%
Entity Kinds Are Heavily Tested on the CBA

The CBA exam will ask you to identify the correct entity kind for a given scenario, understand the YAML structure, and know which annotations enable which integrations. Memorize the 9 entity kinds and their purposes — this is foundational knowledge.

3.1 Key Backstage Terminology

TermDefinition
EntityAny thing tracked in the Catalog — a service, API, person, team, database, etc. Every entity has a kind and is defined in a YAML file.
KindThe type of entity. Backstage has 9 built-in kinds: Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group, Template, Location.
SpecType-specific properties of an entity. A Component spec has type, lifecycle, owner. An API spec has type (openapi, asyncapi, etc.) and definition.
MetadataCommon properties: name, description, labels, annotations, tags, links. Every entity has metadata.
AnnotationA key: value pair in metadata that links an entity to an external system. Example: github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost.
LocationA pointer to where entity YAML files can be found — a GitHub URL, a local file path, or an integration source.
PluginAn extensible module that adds features to Backstage. Can be frontend-only, backend-only, or full-stack.
TemplateA Scaffolder blueprint that defines how to create new projects. Specifies parameters (user inputs) and steps (actions to execute).

3.2 Entity Kinds — Complete Reference

📦
Component

A piece of software — a service, website, library, or CLI tool. The most common entity kind.

Example: jobpost (Django service), anihpj-frontend (React website)

📡
API

An API definition — OpenAPI (REST), AsyncAPI (async), GraphQL, or gRPC. Describes the contract between services.

Example: jobpost-api (OpenAPI spec for jobpost's REST endpoints)

🖥️
System

A collection of Components and Resources that work together. Like a "product" or "application suite."

Example: anihpj-recruitment-system (contains jobpost, frontend, postgres-db)

🏢
Domain

A grouping of Systems by business domain. Like a department or business unit.

Example: hr-domain (contains recruitment system, payroll system)

💾
Resource

Infrastructure that Components depend on — databases, caches, queues, S3 buckets.

Example: postgres-db (PostgreSQL database), redis-cache (Redis cache)

👤
User

A person in the organization. Typically auto-discovered from your auth provider (GitHub, Okta).

Example: jane.doe (developer on team-platform)

👥
Group

A team, squad, tribe, or organizational unit. Can have parent/child hierarchy.

Example: team-platform (parent of team-fe, team-data)

🚀
Template

A Scaffolder blueprint for creating new projects. Defines parameters and actions.

Example: django-service (template for creating new Django microservices)

📍
Location

A pointer to where entity YAML files can be found. Can be a GitHub URL, file path, or integration.

Example: URL pointing to anihpj's catalog-info.yaml on GitHub

3.3 The Entity Lifecycle

Entity Lifecycle — From YAML to Searchable
  1. LOCATION        2. INGESTION       3. PROCESSING
  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐
  │ Git URL  │─────▶│ Fetch    │─────▶│ Validate │
  │ File path│      │ YAML     │      │ & Parse  │
  │ URL      │      │ from src │      │ entity   │
  └──────────┘      └──────────┘      └──────────┘
                                            │
                                            ▼
  4. STORAGE          5. STITCHING       6. SEARCH
  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐
  │ Save to  │◀─────│ Link     │◀─────│ Index    │
  │ Database │      │ relations│      │ for      │
  │ (PG/SQL) │      │ (owner,  │      │ search   │
  └──────────┘      │  APIs)   │      └──────────┘
                    └──────────┘
1
Registration

Entity YAML is discovered at a Location (GitHub URL, file path)

2
Ingestion

YAML is fetched and parsed from the source

3
Processing

Entity is validated, enriched with additional data

4
Storage

Entity is saved to the PostgreSQL database

5
Stitching

Relationships between entities are resolved

6
Search

Entity is indexed for full-text search

3.4 Annotations Reference

Annotations are key: value pairs in an entity's metadata that link it to external systems. They're how Backstage knows which GitHub repo, PagerDuty service, or Kubernetes deployment belongs to each entity.

AnnotationPurposeExample Value
github.com/project-slugLink to GitHub repositoryanihpj/jobpost
backstage.io/techdocs-refWhere TechDocs files livedir:. or url:https://...
backstage.io/kubernetes-idLink to K8s Deploymentjobpost
backstage.io/kubernetes-namespaceK8s namespace for this entityanihpj-prod
jenkins.io/job-full-nameLink to Jenkins jobanihpj/jobpost-build
pagerduty.com/service-idLink to PagerDuty serviceP12345
sentry.io/project-slugLink to Sentry projectanihpj/jobpost
sonarqube.org/project-keyLink to SonarQube projectanihpj-jobpost

3.5 The YAML Format — catalog-info.yaml

📋 catalog-info.yaml — Complete Anatomy

apiVersion
backstage.io/v1alpha1 — Always this value for now
kind
Component | API | System | Domain | Resource | User | Group | Template | Location
metadata.name
Unique identifier. Must be unique per kind and namespace. jobpost
metadata.annotations
Key-value pairs linking to external systems (GitHub, K8s, PagerDuty, etc.)
metadata.labels
Key-value pairs for filtering and grouping. language: python, tier: backend
metadata.tags
Simple string tags. [django, postgresql, rest-api]
spec
Type-specific properties. Varies by kind. Component has type, lifecycle, owner.
Example: catalog-info.yaml for anihpj/jobpost
apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
kind: Component
metadata:
  name: jobpost
  description: Job posting and management application
  annotations:
    github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
    backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
  tags: [python, django, postgresql]
  links:
    - url: https://jobpost.anihpj.com
      title: Production
spec:
  type: service
  lifecycle: production
  owner: team-platform
  system: anihpj-recruitment-system
  providesApis:
    - jobpost-api
  dependsOn:
    - resource:postgres-db
    - resource:redis-cache

Chapter 3 CBA Quick Wins: Memorize the 9 entity kinds: Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group, Template, Location. Software you build = Component. Infrastructure you use = Resource. Labels = filtering. Annotations = external system links. Every catalog-info.yaml needs: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec.

📝 Chapter 3 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 9 entity kinds in Backstage? List them all.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Component — A piece of software (service, website, library, CLI)
2. API — An API definition (openapi, asyncapi, graphql, grpc)
3. System — A collection of Components + Resources
4. Domain — A grouping of Systems by business domain
5. Resource — Infrastructure (database, cache, queue, bucket)
6. User — A person in the organization
7. Group — A team, squad, or organizational unit
8. Template — A Scaffolder blueprint
9. Location — A pointer to where entity YAML files live

Explanation

Memorizing all 9 entity kinds is essential for the CBA. You'll be asked to identify which kind to use for a given scenario. Component is the most common; Location is the most unique (it's a pointer, not a thing).

Q2
You have a PostgreSQL database that multiple services depend on. Which entity kind should you use to represent it in the Catalog?
Show Answer
Answer

Use the Resource kind with spec.type: database. Resources represent infrastructure that Components depend on. Other Components would reference it via spec.dependsOn: [resource:postgres-db].

Explanation

A common mistake is using Component for databases. Components are for software YOU build. Resources are for infrastructure you USE. Key discriminator: "Did we write the code for this?" Yes → Component. No → Resource.

Q3
What is the difference between labels and annotations in entity metadata?
Show Answer
Answer

Labels: Key-value pairs used for filtering and grouping entities in the Catalog UI. Example: language: python, tier: backend. Users filter by labels in the Catalog browser.
Annotations: Key-value pairs used to link entities to external systems. Example: github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost. Annotations are consumed by plugins, not users.

Explanation

Think of labels as "user-facing tags for filtering" and annotations as "machine-facing pointers to external systems." Labels help humans browse the Catalog. Annotations help plugins integrate with GitHub, K8s, PagerDuty, etc.

Q4
What are the 4 required top-level fields in every catalog-info.yaml file?
Show Answer
Answer

1. apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1 (always this value)
2. kind: One of the 9 entity kinds
3. metadata: At minimum, must include name (unique identifier)
4. spec: Type-specific properties (varies by kind)

Explanation

Every catalog-info.yaml MUST have these 4 fields. If any is missing, the entity will fail validation during catalog ingestion. name in metadata is the only required metadata field, though description is highly recommended.

Q5
What annotation links a Backstage entity to a GitHub repository?
Show Answer
Answer

github.com/project-slug with the value in the format owner/repo. Example: github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost. This annotation enables the GitHub plugin to show repository information, README, and CI/CD status on the entity page.

Explanation

This is one of the most commonly used annotations. Without it, Backstage can't connect the entity to its GitHub repo, and the GitHub plugin cards won't appear on the entity page. The format is always owner/repository-name.

Q6
Describe the 6 stages of the entity lifecycle in Backstage.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Registration: Entity YAML is discovered at a Location (GitHub URL, file path)
2. Ingestion: YAML is fetched from the source
3. Processing: Entity is validated and enriched with additional data
4. Storage: Entity is saved to the PostgreSQL database
5. Stitching: Relationships between entities are resolved (owner, system, APIs)
6. Search: Entity is indexed for full-text search

Explanation

Understanding the entity lifecycle helps you troubleshoot catalog issues. If an entity isn't showing up, check: (1) Is the location configured? (2) Is the YAML valid? (3) Did processing succeed? (4) Is it in the database? (5) Are relationships resolved? (6) Is the search index updated?

Q7
What's the difference between a System and a Domain in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

System: A collection of Components and Resources that work together as a logical unit. Like a "product" or "application." Example: anihpj-recruitment-system contains jobpost, frontend, postgres-db.
Domain: A grouping of Systems by business area. Higher level than System. Example: hr-domain contains recruitment-system, payroll-system, benefits-system.
Hierarchy: Domain → System → Component/Resource

Explanation

The hierarchy goes: Domain (business area) → System (product/app) → Component (service/library) / Resource (infrastructure). A Domain can contain multiple Systems. A System can contain multiple Components and Resources.

Q8
How do you define that a Component depends on a Resource in catalog-info.yaml?
Show Answer
Answer

Use the spec.dependsOn field with the format resource:resource-name. Example:
dependsOn:
- resource:postgres-db
- resource:redis-cache
This creates a dependency relationship visible in the Catalog's dependency graph.

Explanation

The dependsOn field accepts references in the format kind:name. For Resources, it's resource:name. For other Components, it's component:name. This builds the dependency graph that helps developers understand service relationships.

Q9
What is the purpose of the backstage.io/techdocs-ref annotation?
Show Answer
Answer

It tells Backstage's TechDocs system where to find the documentation files for this entity. Common values:
dir:. — Docs are in the root of the repository (in a /docs folder)
url:https://github.com/anihpj/docs — Docs are at a specific URL
Without this annotation, TechDocs won't know where to look for an entity's documentation.

Explanation

This annotation is essential for TechDocs to work. Every entity that wants documentation in Backstage needs this annotation. dir:. is the most common value — it tells TechDocs to look for an mkdocs.yml and /docs folder in the same repo.

Q10
You need to represent the \"anihpj\" platform which consists of a Django backend (jobpost), a React frontend, and a PostgreSQL database. Show the entity kinds and relationships you would use.
Show Answer
Answer

Domain: hr-domain (business domain)
└── System: anihpj-recruitment-system (the platform)
  ├── Component: jobpost (type: service, Django backend)
  ├── Component: anihpj-frontend (type: website, React frontend)
  ├── Resource: postgres-db (type: database)
  └── API: jobpost-api (type: openapi)
Relationships: jobpost provides jobpost-api, frontend consumes jobpost-api, both components depend on postgres-db.

Explanation

This is a classic CBA scenario question. The key is choosing the right kind for each piece: software you build → Component, infrastructure → Resource, API definitions → API, logical grouping → System, business grouping → Domain. The hierarchy and relationships between them create the full picture.

4.1 Prerequisites — What You Need

📦
Node.js 18+ (LTS)

Backstage requires Node.js 18 or later. Download from nodejs.org. Verify: node --version should show v18.x or v20.x.

🧶
Yarn (v1.22+ or v3+)

Backstage uses Yarn as its package manager. Install: npm install -g yarn. Verify: yarn --version.

🔀
Git

Required for version control and GitHub integration. Download from git-scm.com. Verify: git --version.

🐳
Docker (Optional)

Recommended for PostgreSQL in development and for building production images. Download from docker.com.

💻
8 GB+ RAM

Backstage dev server needs memory for TypeScript compilation, React hot-reload, and the backend server running simultaneously.

🖥️
Windows / macOS / Linux

Backstage runs on all major operating systems. Windows users should use WSL2 for best experience.

4.2 Creating Your First Backstage App

Real Step-by-Step: Creating the anihpj Portal
  $ mkdir anihpj-portal && cd anihpj-portal
  $ npx @backstage/create-app@latest

  ? Enter a name for the app [required] anihpj-portal
  ? Select database for the backend [required]
    ❯ SQLite (recommended for local dev)
      PostgreSQL (for production)

  Creating the app... this may take 2-3 minutes
  ✔  Created 147 files in ./anihpj-portal
  ✔  Installed 1,200+ dependencies via yarn

  $ cd anihpj-portal
  $ yarn install --frozen-lockfile     # ~2 min
  $ yarn tsc                            # TypeScript check
  $ yarn dev                            # Start dev server!

  [0]  → Server running on http://localhost:3000
  [0]  → Backend running on http://localhost:7007

  Open http://localhost:3000 in your browser! 🎉
💡 What Just Happened?

The create-app command scaffolded a complete Backstage project with frontend (React), backend (Node.js/Express), and database (SQLite). yarn dev starts BOTH the frontend (port 3000) and backend (port 7007) simultaneously. The frontend communicates with the backend via REST API calls.

4.3 Directory Structure — Every File Explained

Anatomy of a Backstage Project
  anihpj-portal/
  ├── .github/              ← CI/CD workflows
  ├── packages/
  │   ├── app/              ← 🖥️ FRONTEND (React UI)

  │   │   ├── src/
  │   │   │   ├── index.ts         ← Backend entry point
  │   │   │   ├── plugins/         ← Backend plugins
  │   │   │   └── types.ts         ← TypeScript types
  │   │   └── package.json
  │   └── README.md
  ├── plugins/              ← 🔌 CUSTOM PLUGINS
  ├── app-config.yaml       ← ⚙️ CENTRAL CONFIG
  ├── app-config.local.yaml ← Local overrides
  ├── catalog-info.yaml     ← 📦 Portal's own entity
  ├── package.json          ← Root dependencies
  └── yarn.lock

packages/backend/src/index.tsBackend entry, registers pluginsAdding new backend plugins

4.4 Running Backstage — Dev vs Production

ModeCommandWhat HappensDatabase
Developmentyarn devHot reload on code changes; frontend :3000, backend :7007SQLite (./dev.db)
Production Buildyarn buildCompiles TypeScript, bundles React appConfigured in app-config
Production Serveyarn startServes built app on :7007 (single port)PostgreSQL (required)
Docker Builddocker build -t backstage .Multi-stage: install → tsc → copy dist → run nodeExternal (env var)
⚠️ NEVER use SQLite in production!

SQLite only supports a single writer at a time. With multiple Backstage replicas on Kubernetes, you need PostgreSQL for concurrent access. The CBA exam WILL test this distinction.

4.5 First Customization — Changing the Theme & Branding

Customizing app-config.yaml for anihpj
  # app-config.yaml — Change these FIRST!
  app:
    title: anihpj Developer Portal  # ← Your portal name
    baseUrl: http://localhost:3000  # ← Your Backstage URL

  organization:
    name: Anihpj Corp               # ← Your company name

  backend:
    baseUrl: http://localhost:7007
    cors:
      origin: http://localhost:3000
    database:
      client: better-sqlite3        # ← Dev: SQLite
      connection: ./dev.db

  # After editing: Ctrl+C to stop, then yarn dev to restart
  # You should see "anihpj Developer Portal" in the header!

Custom MUI Theme — Go Beyond the Basics

Backstage uses Material UI (MUI) v5 for its component library. You can override the default theme to match your company's branding — colors, fonts, logo, and even dark/light mode defaults.

Creating a Custom Theme in packages/app/src/App.tsx
  // packages/app/src/App.tsx
  import { createApp } from '@backstage/app-defaults';
  import { LightTheme, DarkTheme } from '@backstage/theme';
  import { createTheme } from '@material-ui/core/styles';

  // Create a custom theme extending Backstage's built-in theme
  const customTheme = createTheme({
    palette: {
      type: 'dark',  // Force dark mode by default
      primary: {
        main: '#326ce5',   // anihpj brand blue
      },
      secondary: {
        main: '#ff6b6b',   // anihpj accent color
      },
      background: {
        default: '#0d1117',
        paper: '#161b22',
      },
    },
    typography: {
      fontFamily: '"Inter", "Roboto", "Helvetica", "Arial", sans-serif',
      h1: { fontWeight: 700 },
      h2: { fontWeight: 600 },
    },
    overrides: {
      // Customize Backstage-specific components
      BackstageHeader: {
        header: {
          background: 'linear-gradient(135deg, #1e3a5f, #0a1929)',
        },
      },
    },
  });

  const app = createApp({
    apis: ...,
    themes: [
      {
        id: 'anihpj-dark',
        title: 'anihpj Dark Theme',
        variant: 'dark',
        Provider: ({ children }) => (
          <ThemeProvider theme={customTheme}>{children}</ThemeProvider>
        ),
      },
    ],
  });
💡 Theme Customization Pro Tips

• The custom logo goes in packages/app/public/ and is referenced in app-config.yaml with app.logo
• MUI v5 uses emotion/styled for component styling — you can override ANY Backstage component
• Backstage has built-in dark/light themes — you can extend them instead of starting from scratch
• The theme toggle icon is in packages/app/src/components/Root/LogoFull.tsx

Dark Mode / Light Mode Toggle

Backstage ships with a built-in theme toggle. Users can switch between dark and light mode from the sidebar. You can configure which theme is the default and which themes are available.

Configuring Available Themes in App.tsx
  // Make BOTH dark and light available, default to dark:
  const app = createApp({
    apis: ...,
    themes: [
      {
        id: 'dark',
        title: 'Dark Mode',
        variant: 'dark',
        Provider: ({ children }) => (
          <UnifiedThemeProvider theme={builtInThemes.dark} children={children} />
        ),
      },
      {
        id: 'light',
        title: 'Light Mode',
        variant: 'light',
        Provider: ({ children }) => (
          <UnifiedThemeProvider theme={builtInThemes.light} children={children} />
        ),
      },
    ],
  });

  // User sees a sun/moon icon in the sidebar to toggle!
  // The selected theme persists in localStorage automatically.
CustomizationWhere to ChangeRequires Restart?
App Titleapp-config.yaml → app.titleYes (Ctrl+C → yarn dev)
Company Nameapp-config.yaml → organization.nameYes
Logo (Full)app-config.yaml → app.logo.full (URL or path)Yes
Logo (Icon)app-config.yaml → app.logo.icon (sidebar collapsed)Yes
Theme ColorsApp.tsx — MUI theme overridesNo (hot reload)
Font FamilyApp.tsx — typography configNo (hot reload)
FaviconReplace packages/app/public/favicon.icoYes

📝 Chapter 4 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What command creates a new Backstage application?
Show Answer
Answer

npx @backstage/create-app@latest. This scaffolds the entire project with frontend (React), backend (Node.js/Express), and database configuration. You'll be prompted for the app name and database choice.

Explanation

This is the first command every Backstage developer runs. npx runs the package without installing it globally. The @latest tag ensures you get the newest version. You can also specify --path ./ to create in the current directory.

Q2
What are the three prerequisites for running Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Node.js 18+ (LTS recommended) — the JavaScript runtime
2. Yarn (v1.22+ or v3+) — the package manager Backstage uses
3. Git — for version control and GitHub integration

Explanation

Docker is optional but recommended for production builds. 8 GB+ RAM is recommended for smooth development. Backstage works on Windows, macOS, and Linux, but Windows users should prefer WSL2.

Q3
Which database should you use for local development, and which for production?
Show Answer
Answer

Development: SQLite (file-based, zero configuration, built into Backstage)
Production: PostgreSQL (full-featured, supports concurrent connections, required for multi-replica deployments)

Explanation

SQLite cannot handle concurrent writes — with 3 Backstage replicas on K8s, you'd get data corruption. PostgreSQL is designed for concurrent access and is the only supported production database. The CBA exam explicitly tests this distinction.

Q4
What two ports does Backstage use in development mode, and what runs on each?
Show Answer
Answer

Port 3000: Frontend (React app with Material UI)
Port 7007: Backend (Node.js/Express API server)
The frontend communicates with the backend via REST API calls to http://localhost:7007.

Explanation

In development, these run as separate processes. In production (yarn build && yarn start), only port 7007 is used — the backend serves both the API and the built frontend assets.

Q5
Where do frontend plugin files live, and where do backend plugin files live?
Show Answer
Answer

Frontend plugins: packages/app/src/ — React components, routes, and UI
Backend plugins: packages/backend/src/plugins/ — Express routes, API logic, database access
Custom shared plugins: plugins/ at the project root

Explanation

This separation is a key architectural concept. The frontend NEVER directly accesses the database — all data flows through the backend API. This keeps the frontend stateless and allows horizontal scaling.

Q6
What is the purpose of app-config.yaml?
Show Answer
Answer

app-config.yaml is the central configuration file for Backstage. It controls: the app title and URL, database connection, auth providers, catalog locations, TechDocs settings, and plugin configuration. Every aspect of Backstage can be configured here using ${ENV_VAR} substitution for secrets.

Explanation

The CBA exam will ask about specific app-config.yaml sections. Know the key top-level sections: app, backend, catalog, scaffolder, techdocs, auth, integrations, proxy, kubernetes. Secrets should NEVER be hardcoded — always use ${ENV_VAR}.

Q7
You run yarn dev and get "Port 3000 already in use". What do you do?
Show Answer
Answer

Option 1: Kill the process using port 3000 — lsof -i :3000 (macOS/Linux) or netstat -ano | findstr :3000 (Windows), then kill -9 <PID>.
Option 2: Use a different port — yarn dev --port 3001.

Explanation

Port conflicts are common when you have multiple Backstage instances or other React apps running. This is a practical troubleshooting question the CBA might ask in a hands-on scenario.

Q8
What is the difference between yarn dev and yarn start?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn dev — Development mode with hot reload. Frontend on :3000, backend on :7007. Code changes are reflected instantly. Uses SQLite.
yarn start — Production mode. Serves the built app on a single port (:7007). Must run yarn build first. Requires PostgreSQL.

Explanation

In dev mode, TypeScript is compiled on-the-fly. In production, you must run yarn build first to compile everything. Dev mode is for development only — never run yarn dev in production.

Q9
What command would you run to add a new frontend plugin to your Backstage instance?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn backstage-cli create-plugin scaffolds a new plugin. Then: (1) Import the plugin in packages/app/src/App.tsx, (2) Add its route in packages/app/src/components/Root.tsx, (3) If it has a backend, register it in packages/backend/src/index.ts.

Explanation

Creating a plugin is just the first step. You must also wire it into the app and backend. The CBA may ask about the steps required after creating a plugin to make it visible.

Q10
You've created a Backstage app. After running yarn dev, the browser shows a blank page. What are the first three things you check?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Check the terminal — are there TypeScript compilation errors? Run yarn tsc separately to see all errors.
2. Check the browser console (F12 → Console) — are there JavaScript errors or failed API calls?
3. Check the backend — is the backend running on :7007? Try curl http://localhost:7007. If the backend is down, the frontend can't load any data.

Explanation

This is a practical debugging scenario. TypeScript errors prevent compilation. Backend errors prevent data loading. Browser console errors indicate React rendering issues. Systematically checking these three layers (compile → backend → frontend) helps isolate the problem.

  • ✅ Understand every section of app-config.yaml
  • ✅ Use environment variables for secrets — ${ENV_VAR} syntax
  • ✅ Understand the config merge hierarchy: base → local → production → env vars
  • ✅ Troubleshoot common dev issues
