5.2 app-config.yaml Deep Dive — Every Section Explained

📋 app-config.yaml — Key Sections Reference

app
title, baseUrl — Your portal's identity and URL
organization
name — Displayed in UI
backend
baseUrl, database (client + connection), CORS, cache
catalog
locations (where entities live), processors, rules
scaffolder
templateLocations — where template YAML files are found
techdocs
builder (local/docker), generator, publisher (local/GCS/S3/Azure)
auth
providers — GitHub, Okta, Azure AD, Google, etc.
integrations
github, gitlab, bitbucket — tokens and API endpoints
proxy
endpoints — proxy external APIs through Backstage
kubernetes
clusters, authProvider, serviceLocatorMethod

Complete Annotated app-config.yaml Example

Below is a production-ready app-config.yaml for the anihpj portal, with every section annotated. Study this — the CBA will ask about specific sections!

app-config.yaml — Full Production Example for anihpj
  # ======================================================
  # app-config.yaml — Backstage Central Configuration
  # ======================================================

  # 1️⃣ APP: Portal identity, branding, and URL
  app:
    title: anihpj Developer Portal        # ← Displayed in browser tab & header
    baseUrl: https://backstage.anihpj.com # ← Public URL (prod) or localhost (dev)

  # 2️⃣ ORGANIZATION: Your company name
  organization:
    name: Anihpj Corp

  # 3️⃣ BACKEND: Server configuration & database
  backend:
    baseUrl: http://localhost:7007        # ← Internal backend URL
    listen:                               # ← (optional) Bind address
      port: 7007
    cors:
      origin: http://localhost:3000       # ← Allow frontend to call backend
      methods: [GET, POST, PUT, DELETE]
      credentials: true
    database:                             # ← CRITICAL: SQLite vs PostgreSQL
      client: pg                          # ← 'better-sqlite3' for dev, 'pg' for prod
      connection:
        host: ${POSTGRES_HOST}            # ← USE ENV VARS FOR SECRETS!
        port: ${POSTGRES_PORT}
        user: ${POSTGRES_USER}
        password: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}
    cache:
      store: memory                       # ← 'memory' for dev, 'redis' for prod

  # 4️⃣ CATALOG: Where entities come from
  catalog:
    locations:                            # ← Manual locations (URLs or files)
      - type: url
        target: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml
      - type: url
        target: https://github.com/anihpj/infra/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml
    rules:                                # ← (optional) Filter which entities to allow
      - allow: [Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group]

  # 5️⃣ SCAFFOLDER: Software Templates location
  scaffolder: {}
    # Templates are typically registered via catalog locations

  # 6️⃣ TECHDOCS: Documentation build & publish
  techdocs:
    builder: 'local'                      # ← 'local' for dev, 'docker' for prod
    generator:
      runIn: 'local'                      # ← Run mkdocs locally or in container
    publisher:
      type: 'local'                       # ← 'local', 'googleGcs', 'awsS3', 'azureBlobStorage'

  # 7️⃣ AUTH: Authentication providers
  auth:
    providers:
      github:                             # ← GitHub OAuth
        development:
          clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}
          clientSecret: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET}

  # 8️⃣ INTEGRATIONS: External service connections
  integrations:
    github:
      - host: github.com
        token: ${GITHUB_TOKEN}            # ← Personal Access Token for API access

  # 9️⃣ PROXY: Forward API calls through Backstage
  proxy:
    endpoints:
      '/jenkins':
        target: https://jenkins.anihpj.com
        changeOrigin: true
        headers:
          Authorization: Bearer ${JENKINS_TOKEN}

  # 🔟 KUBERNETES: Connect to K8s clusters
  kubernetes:
    serviceLocatorMethod:
      type: 'multiTenant'                 # ← How services are found in K8s
    clusterLocatorMethods:
      - type: 'config'
        clusters:
          - name: 'anihpj-prod'
            url: 'https://k8s.anihpj.com'
            authProvider: 'serviceAccount'
            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_SA_TOKEN}
⚠️ CBA Exam Tip: Know These Section Names

The CBA exam will ask "In which section of app-config.yaml would you configure X?" Memorize the 10 top-level sections: app, organization, backend, catalog, scaffolder, techdocs, auth, integrations, proxy, kubernetes. Every configuration option lives under one of these.

5.3 Environment Variables & Secrets

Secret Management Pattern
  # NEVER do this:
  auth:
    providers:
      github:
        clientId: "abc123"    ← ❌ HARDCODED SECRET!

  # ALWAYS do this:
  auth:
    providers:
      github:
        clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}      ← ✅ From env var

  # Set in your terminal or .env file:
  export GITHUB_CLIENT_ID=abc123
  export GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET=xyz789

  # On Kubernetes, use Secrets:
  kubectl create secret generic backstage-secrets \
    --from-literal=GITHUB_CLIENT_ID=abc123
⚠️ Never commit secrets to Git!

Environment variables keep secrets out of your config files and out of your Git history. On Kubernetes, use Secret resources and reference them in your Deployment's env section.

Using .env Files for Local Development

Backstage doesn't automatically load .env files — you need to set environment variables manually or use a package like dotenv-cli. Here's the recommended approach:

Local Development Secret Management
  # 1. Create a .env file (ADD TO .gitignore!)
  # .env — NEVER COMMIT THIS FILE
  GITHUB_TOKEN=ghp_xxxxxxxxxxxx
  GITHUB_CLIENT_ID=abc123def456
  GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET=xyz789secret
  POSTGRES_PASSWORD=mysecretpassword

  # 2. Load env vars before starting Backstage:
  # Option A: Use export (Linux/macOS)
  $ export $(cat .env | xargs) && yarn dev

  # Option B: Use dotenv-cli (cross-platform)
  $ npm install -g dotenv-cli
  $ dotenv -- yarn dev

  # 3. On Kubernetes — Deployment pattern:
  apiVersion: apps/v1
  kind: Deployment
  metadata:
    name: backstage
  spec:
    template:
      spec:
        containers:
        - name: backstage
          image: backstage:latest
          env:
          - name: GITHUB_TOKEN        # ← Maps K8s Secret to env var
            valueFrom:
              secretKeyRef:
                name: backstage-secrets
                key: github-token
          - name: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
            valueFrom:
              secretKeyRef:
                name: backstage-secrets
                key: postgres-password

On the CBA exam, remember these secret management patterns:

  • ${ENV_VAR} — Use in app-config.yaml for ALL secrets (never hardcode)
  • .env file — Local dev only, MUST be in .gitignore, never committed
  • secretKeyRef — Kubernetes pattern for injecting secrets into pods
  • kubectl create secret generic — Create K8s Secrets from literal values or files
  • Backstage has NO built-in secret manager — all secrets come from environment variables

5.4 Multiple Configuration Files

Backstage merges configuration from multiple files. The merge order (later overrides earlier):

1
app-config.yaml

Base/default config

2
app-config.local.yaml

Local dev overrides

3
app-config.production.yaml

Production overrides

4
Environment Variables

${ENV_VAR} substitution

📊 Chapter 5 — Visual Summary: CLI & Configuration Mastery

▶️ yarn dev Start dev server (:3000 + :7007). Your daily command with hot reload.
🔍 yarn tsc TypeScript type checker. Run before commit. Catches type errors early.
🧹 yarn lint Code style & quality. Run before PR. Enforces consistent formatting.
✅ yarn test Unit tests. Run after changes. Catches regressions before they ship.
🔌 create-plugin Scaffold new plugin. Then register in App.tsx + Root.tsx + backend/index.ts.
🗄️ migrate Apply DB schema changes after version upgrades. Critical post-upgrade step.
🏗️ yarn build Compile TypeScript, bundle React. Production-only. Run before docker build.
🔐 ${ENV_VAR} Secret substitution syntax. NEVER hardcode secrets. Use K8s Secrets in prod.
📋 10 Config Sections app, org, backend, catalog, scaffolder, techdocs, auth, integrations, proxy, k8s.
🔄 Config Merge Order base → local → production → ENV_VAR. Later overrides earlier. Env vars win.

📝 Chapter 5 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
Which command do you run to start Backstage in development mode?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn dev — starts both the frontend (port 3000) and backend (port 7007) with hot reload enabled. This is the command you'll run every day during development.

Explanation

yarn dev is different from yarn start (production). In dev mode, TypeScript is compiled on-the-fly and React hot-reloads when you save changes. In production, you must build first with yarn build.

Q2
How do you pass secrets (like database passwords) to Backstage configuration?
Show Answer
Answer

Use the ${ENV_VAR} syntax in app-config.yaml. For example: clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}. Set the actual value as an environment variable before starting Backstage. Never hardcode secrets in YAML files.

Explanation

This is a critical security practice tested on the CBA. Environment variables keep secrets out of config files and Git history. On Kubernetes, use Secret resources and reference them in your Deployment's env section with secretKeyRef.

Q3
What is the config merge order for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. app-config.yaml (base/default)
2. app-config.local.yaml (local dev overrides)
3. app-config.production.yaml (production overrides)
4. Environment variables (${ENV_VAR}) — highest priority

Explanation

Later files override earlier ones. Environment variables always take highest priority. This allows you to have one base config with environment-specific overrides without duplicating the entire config file.

Q4
What section of app-config.yaml configures where Backstage looks for entity YAML files?
Show Answer
Answer

The catalog.locations section. Example:
catalog:
locations:
- type: url
target: https://github.com/myorg/myrepo/catalog-info.yaml

Explanation

Locations tell Backstage where to find entity definitions. You can use url (GitHub/GitLab URLs), file (local files), or integration-based discovery (auto-scanning GitHub orgs). The CBA exam tests your knowledge of catalog location types.

Q5
What command would you use to scaffold a new Backstage plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn backstage-cli create-plugin. This scaffolds a new plugin with the required structure: plugin.ts, route definitions, and component files. You can specify --backend to create a backend-only plugin.

Explanation

yarn backstage-cli is the built-in CLI for Backstage development tasks. Besides create-plugin, it also handles build, clean, migrate, and package commands.

Q6
After upgrading Backstage to a new version, your app fails to start with a database error. What do you run?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn backstage-cli migrate — this applies any pending database schema migrations that came with the new Backstage version. Database migrations update the schema to match what the new code expects.

Explanation

This is a common real-world scenario. Backstage releases often include database schema changes. Running migrate updates your database tables without losing data. If you skip this step, the app will crash because the code expects columns that don't exist yet.

Q7
What section of app-config.yaml would you configure to connect to a Kubernetes cluster?
Show Answer
Answer

The kubernetes section. You define clusters under kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods[].clusters[] with fields like name, url (API server URL), authProvider, and serviceAccountToken.

Explanation

The Kubernetes plugin uses this configuration to connect to your K8s clusters and display deployment information on entity pages. Each cluster needs a name, API server URL, and authentication method (usually a service account token).

Q8
You run yarn dev and see "Module not found" errors. What's the most likely fix?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn install --frozen-lockfile — this reinstalls all dependencies exactly as specified in yarn.lock. The --frozen-lockfile flag ensures no unexpected version changes.

Explanation

"Module not found" usually means dependencies are missing or out of sync. This commonly happens after git pull when another developer added new dependencies. Always run yarn install after pulling changes.

Q9
What is the difference between app-config.yaml and app-config.local.yaml?
Show Answer
Answer

app-config.yaml — Base configuration shared across all environments. Committed to Git.
app-config.local.yaml — Local development overrides. Typically NOT committed to Git. Overrides base settings for your local machine (e.g., local database paths, dev-only auth settings).

Explanation

app-config.local.yaml should be in .gitignore. It's for personal dev settings that shouldn't be shared. Production overrides go in app-config.production.yaml instead.

Q10
You need to deploy Backstage to production on Kubernetes with 3 replicas. What database configuration must you use?
Show Answer
Answer

You MUST use PostgreSQL with an external connection. In app-config.yaml:
backend:
database:
client: pg
connection:
host: ${POSTGRES_HOST}
port: ${POSTGRES_PORT}
user: ${POSTGRES_USER}
password: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}
SQLite cannot handle concurrent writes from multiple replicas and would cause data corruption.

Explanation

This is a production-critical concept. Multi-replica deployments REQUIRE PostgreSQL. The database must be external (separate StatefulSet or managed cloud database), not inside the Backstage pod. All credentials should use ${ENV_VAR} from Kubernetes Secrets.

Detailed Walkthrough of Each Step

📦
Step 1: Create the Backstage App

npx @backstage/create-app@latest → Name it anihpj-portal. Choose SQLite for dev. This creates ~147 files in a standard structure. Run yarn dev to verify the empty portal is running on http://localhost:3000.

🔑
Step 2: Configure GitHub Integration

Generate a GitHub Personal Access Token (Settings → Developer Settings → PAT). Add integrations.github[0].token: ${GITHUB_TOKEN} to app-config.yaml. Set export GITHUB_TOKEN=ghp_xxxx before starting Backstage.

📋
Step 3: Define anihpj Entities

Create catalog-info.yaml in the jobpost repo. Define the Component (jobpost), API (jobpost-api), System (anihpj), and Resources (postgres, redis). Add the catalog location URL to app-config.yaml.

📝
Step 4: Create a Django Software Template

Create template.yaml defining parameters (service name, description, team). Create a skeleton/ folder with boilerplate files: Dockerfile, requirements.txt, manage.py, catalog-info.yaml, k8s manifests. Register the template in app-config.yaml.

📖
Step 5: Set Up TechDocs

Add mkdocs.yml and /docs/ folder to the jobpost repo. Add backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. annotation to catalog-info.yaml. TechDocs auto-builds docs from Markdown files when the entity page loads.

✅
Step 6: Verify Everything

Visit /catalog → see all entities. Visit /create → see Django template. Click jobpost → see Docs tab with rendered TechDocs. Search for "jobpost" → entity appears. Run curl :7007/api/catalog/entities for API verification.

Step 3 in Detail: catalog-info.yaml for jobpost
  # /jobpost/catalog-info.yaml — Entity definition
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Component
  metadata:
    name: jobpost
    description: Job posting and recruitment management service
    annotations:
      github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
      backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
      backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: anihpj-prod
    tags:
      - django
      - python
      - recruitment
    links:
      - url: https://jobpost.anihpj.com
        title: Production URL
        icon: dashboard
  spec:
    type: service
    lifecycle: production
    owner: team-platform
    system: anihpj-system
    dependsOn:
      - resource:postgres-db
      - resource:redis-cache
    providesApis:
      - jobpost-api
💡 This is YOUR first catalog-info.yaml!

After placing this file in the jobpost repo, add its URL to app-config.yaml under catalog.locations. Restart Backstage, and the jobpost entity appears in the Catalog with its Overview, Docs, API, and Dependencies tabs. This is exactly what the CBA exam tests — knowing how to define and register an entity.

  • ✅ Click jobpost → see Overview, Docs, API, Dependencies tabs
  • ✅ Dependencies tab shows postgres-db and redis-cache
  • ✅ Search for "jobpost" in the search bar → entity appears

  • ✅ Template executes successfully → new repo on GitHub

📝 Chapter 6 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the first thing you should set up when creating a new Backstage instance for your organization?
Show Answer
Answer

The Software Catalog — register your services as entities so they're discoverable. Without the Catalog, Backstage is just an empty shell. Add catalog locations in app-config.yaml pointing to your catalog-info.yaml files on GitHub.

Explanation

The Catalog is the foundation of Backstage. Everything else (TechDocs, K8s plugin, CI/CD cards) depends on entities being registered. Start with the Catalog, then add Templates, then TechDocs, then plugins.

Q2
How do you register a GitHub repository as an entity in the Backstage Catalog?
Show Answer
Answer

Add a location in app-config.yaml:
catalog:
locations:
- type: url
target: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml
The repository must contain a catalog-info.yaml file at that path.

Explanation

There are 3 methods: (1) URL location (point to a specific file), (2) File location (local files), (3) GitHub Org provider (auto-scan all repos in an org). The URL method is most common for manual registration.

Q3
You've added a catalog location but the entity doesn't appear. What do you check first?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Check app-config.yaml — is the location correctly configured?
2. Check the YAML file exists at the specified URL
3. Run curl http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities to see registered entities
4. Check backend logs for ingestion errors
5. Verify YAML syntax — remove trailing commas, check indentation

Explanation

Catalog ingestion failures are common. The most frequent causes: wrong URL in the location, invalid YAML syntax (trailing commas, wrong indentation), or missing required fields (name, kind). The backend logs will show the specific error.

Q4
What is the purpose of GitHub integration in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

GitHub integration enables Backstage to: (1) Auto-discover catalog-info.yaml files in repositories, (2) Show repository information (README, contributors, languages) on entity pages, (3) Display CI/CD status from GitHub Actions, (4) Create new repositories when executing Software Templates via publish:github.

Explanation

GitHub integration is configured in app-config.yaml under integrations.github with a personal access token. The token is passed via environment variable: ${GITHUB_TOKEN}. Without this integration, Backstage can't read files from private repos or create new repos from templates.

Q5
What 6-step process would you follow to build a Backstage portal for a new organization?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Create the app (npx create-app → configure app-config.yaml)
2. Add GitHub integration (token + catalog locations)
3. Define entities (catalog-info.yaml for each service/API/resource)
4. Create templates (template.yaml for standardized project creation)
5. Set up TechDocs (mkdocs.yml + /docs folder in each repo)
6. Add plugins (K8s, CI/CD, monitoring — based on org needs)

Explanation

This phased approach is the recommended adoption strategy. Start with the Catalog (immediate value), then Templates (enable self-service), then TechDocs (documentation), then plugins (extend). Don't try to integrate everything at once.

Q6
How do you verify that entities are successfully registered in the Catalog?
Show Answer
Answer

1. UI: Go to http://localhost:3000/catalog — entities should appear in the grid
2. API: curl http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities — returns JSON list
3. Search: Type entity name in the Backstage search bar
4. Logs: Check terminal output for ingestion success/failure messages

Explanation

The API endpoint is the most reliable way to verify — it shows exactly what's in the database regardless of UI state. If entities appear in the API but not the UI, there may be a frontend rendering issue. If they don't appear in the API, the ingestion failed.

Q7
What is the relationship between catalog-info.yaml and app-config.yaml?
Show Answer
Answer

catalog-info.yaml — Defines individual entities (one per service/API/resource). Lives in each repository.
app-config.yaml — Tells Backstage WHERE to find catalog-info.yaml files (via catalog.locations). Lives in the Backstage project root.
app-config.yaml points to catalog-info.yaml files; catalog-info.yaml defines the actual entities.

Explanation

Think of app-config.yaml as the "map" that tells Backstage where to look, and catalog-info.yaml as the "description" of what's at each location. You need both for the Catalog to work.

Q8
After creating a Backstage portal for anihpj, you notice the jobpost entity shows no documentation. What annotation is missing?
Show Answer
Answer

backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. — This annotation tells TechDocs where to find the documentation files. The value dir:. means TechDocs should look for an mkdocs.yml file and a /docs folder in the same repository.

Explanation

Without this annotation, the "Docs" tab won't appear on the entity page. The annotation must be in the entity's metadata.annotations section of catalog-info.yaml. Common values: dir:. (docs in repo root), url:https://... (docs at external URL).

Q9
You want developers to be able to create new Django services from a standardized template in Backstage. What do you need to create?
Show Answer
Answer

You need: (1) A template.yaml file defining the template (parameters + steps), (2) A skeleton/ directory with the Django boilerplate files (Dockerfile, requirements.txt, manage.py, catalog-info.yaml, CI/CD, K8s manifests), (3) Register the template location in app-config.yaml under scaffolder.templateLocations.

Explanation

The template defines WHAT developers fill in (parameters) and WHAT happens when they click Create (steps: fetch skeleton, publish to GitHub, register in Catalog). The skeleton contains the actual files that get copied and customized.

Q10
Your Backstage portal is running locally. A colleague asks how to see it. What URL do you give them?
Show Answer
Answer

If you're on the same machine: http://localhost:3000
If they're on the same network: http://YOUR_IP:3000 (find your IP with ifconfig or ipconfig)
For others to access: Deploy Backstage to a server/cloud with a public URL (e.g., https://backstage.anihpj.com). Local dev is NOT meant for sharing — it's for development only.

Explanation

Backstage in dev mode (yarn dev) is designed for single-user local development. For team access, you need to deploy Backstage to production (Kubernetes, cloud VM, or managed service) with proper authentication, HTTPS, and PostgreSQL.

Chapter 7: The Catalog — Central Nervous System of Backstage

The Software Catalog is the heart of Backstage. It's the single source of truth for ALL your software — services, APIs, websites, libraries, databases, and teams. Everything in Backstage revolves around the Catalog. If you master the Catalog, you master Backstage.

  • ✅ Know how to view, search, and explore entities in the Catalog UI

Backstage Catalog 22%
The Catalog Is the Foundation — Everything Depends on It

The CBA exam dedicates 22% to the Catalog domain. You'll be tested on: (1) How to populate the Catalog with entities, (2) Understanding catalog locations and providers, (3) Troubleshooting entity ingestion failures, (4) Working with annotations. Remember: without entities in the Catalog, Backstage is just an empty shell — TechDocs, K8s plugin, and CI/CD cards all depend on entities being registered first.

7.1 What is the Software Catalog?

The Software Catalog is Backstage's central registry of everything in your engineering ecosystem. Think of it as the "phone book" for your software — it knows every service, who owns it, what APIs it provides, what it depends on, and where its documentation lives. Best part? It auto-discovers and auto-updates — no manual data entry required.

The Catalog — Your Software Nervous System
                          ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
                          │                                  │
     GitHub ──────────────┤                                  │
     GitLab ──────────────┤                                  ├──▶  🔍 SEARCH
     K8s Cluster ─────────┤      📦 SOFTWARE CATALOG         ├──▶  🖥️ DASHBOARD
     Jenkins ─────────────┤        (PostgreSQL)              ├──▶  📖 TECHDOCS
     Sentry ──────────────┤                                  ├──▶  🔌 PLUGINS
     PagerDuty ───────────┤                                  ├──▶  🔄 CI/CD CARDS
                          │                                  │
                          └──────────────────────────────────┘

              Everything auto-discovered, always up-to-date!
              One place to find: ownership, docs, dependencies, health status
What the Catalog TracksExample for anihpjWhere It Comes From
Components (services, websites, libraries)jobpost Django backendcatalog-info.yaml in repo
APIs (REST, GraphQL, gRPC, AsyncAPI)jobpost-api (OpenAPI)catalog-info.yaml or spec file
Resources (databases, caches, queues)postgres-db, redis-cachecatalog-info.yaml in infra repo
Systems (groups of components)anihpj-recruitment-systemcatalog-info.yaml
Domains (business areas)hr-domaincatalog-info.yaml
Users & Groups (people & teams)team-platform, jane.doeAuth provider or YAML

7.2 How the Catalog Works — Under the Hood

The Catalog works through a 3-stage ingestion pipeline: Location → Provider → Processor → Database. Understanding this pipeline is critical for the CBA exam and for troubleshooting when entities don't appear.

Catalog Ingestion Pipeline — 3 Stages
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  📥 CATALOG INGESTION FLOW                                          │
  │                                                                     │
  │  STAGE 1: LOCATION          STAGE 2: PROVIDER       STAGE 3: PROCESSOR
  │  ┌──────────────┐          ┌──────────────┐        ┌──────────────┐ │
  │  │ GitHub URL   │─────────▶│ GithubEntity │───────▶│ Placeholder  │ │
  │  │ (YAML file)  │          │ Provider     │        │ Processor    │ │
  │  └──────────────┘          └──────────────┘        └──────┬───────┘ │
  │                                                           │         │
  │  ┌──────────────┐          ┌──────────────┐               ▼         │
  │  │ Local File   │─────────▶│ UrlReader    │        ┌──────────────┐ │
  │  │ (./catalog/) │          │ Processor    │───────▶│ Entity       │ │
  │  └──────────────┘          └──────────────┘        │ Processor    │ │
  │                                                     └──────┬───────┘ │
  │  ┌──────────────┐          ┌──────────────┐               ▼         │
  │  │ K8s Cluster  │─────────▶│ Kubernetes   │        ┌──────────────┐ │
  │  │ (auto-disc)  │          │ Provider     │───────▶│ 💾 DATABASE  │ │
  │  └──────────────┘          └──────────────┘        │    STORE     │ │
  │                                                     └──────────────┘ │
  │                                                                     │
  │  🔑 KEY: Location = WHERE to find entities                          │
  │          Provider = HOW to read/generate entities                    │
  │          Processor = HOW to transform & validate entities            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📍
Stage 1: Location

Where entities come from. A location is a pointer to a YAML file (URL, local file, or integration). Think of it as the "address" where Backstage should look. Configurable in app-config.yaml under catalog.locations.

📡
Stage 2: Entity Provider

How entities are read. Each location type has a matching provider: GithubEntityProvider reads from GitHub, UrlReaderProcessor reads local files, KubernetesEntityProvider auto-discovers from K8s.

⚙️
Stage 3: Processor

Transforms raw data into valid entities. Validates YAML syntax, resolves placeholders ($text), enriches with external data, and stores the final entity in the database. Runs on every catalog refresh cycle.

💾
Final: Database Store

Entities are stored in PostgreSQL (or SQLite for dev). The database is the ultimate source of truth. The Catalog API (/api/catalog/entities) queries this database. Entities refresh on a configurable schedule.

⚠️ CBA Exam Alert: Ingestion Pipeline

The CBA will test your understanding of why an entity might not appear. Common failure points: (1) Wrong location URL, (2) Invalid YAML syntax in catalog-info.yaml, (3) Missing required fields (name, kind), (4) GitHub token not configured for private repos, (5) Provider not enabled in app-config.yaml. Always check backend logs first!

Catalog Refresh Cycles — How Entities Stay Up-to-Date

The catalog doesn't just ingest entities once — it continuously refreshes them on a schedule. This ensures your Catalog always reflects the latest state of your repositories and infrastructure.

Refresh AspectDefaultWhat HappensConfig Location
Scheduled RefreshEvery 100 secondsAll locations are re-read, entities re-processedbackend.reading.allow config
GitHub Org ScanEvery 30 minutesRe-scans GitHub org for new/changed catalog-info.yaml filescatalog.providers.github.schedule
Manual RefreshOn demandImmediate re-read via POST /api/catalog/refreshAPI call — no config needed
Entity-level RefreshOn demandRefresh a single entity: POST /refresh/{kind}/{namespace}/{name}API call

7.3 Catalog Locations — Where Entities Live

Catalog locations tell Backstage where to look for entity definitions. There are 3 main types of locations, each suited for different scenarios.

Registering anihpj in the Catalog — 3 Methods
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  METHOD 1: GitHub URL (most common in production)                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  # app-config.yaml                                                │
  │  catalog:                                                        │
  │    locations:                                                    │
  │      - type: url                                                 │
  │        target: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/      │
  │                catalog-info.yaml                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ Best for: Existing repos with catalog-info.yaml committed    │
  │  ⚠️ Requires: GitHub token for private repos                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  METHOD 2: Local File (for development/testing)                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  # app-config.yaml                                                │
  │  catalog:                                                        │
  │    locations:                                                    │
  │      - type: file                                                │
  │        target: ../../local-catalog/entities.yaml                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ Best for: Local development, testing entity YAML             │
  │  ⚠️ Requires: Absolute or relative path on the same machine      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  METHOD 3: GitHub Org Auto-Discovery (bulk — most powerful)      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  # app-config.yaml                                                │
  │  catalog:                                                        │
  │    providers:                                                    │
  │      github:                                                     │
  │        organization: anihpj  # ← Scans ALL repos in this org!    │
  │        schedule:                                                │
  │          frequency: { minutes: 30 }                              │
  │          timeout: { minutes: 3 }                                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ Best for: Large orgs — auto-discovers catalog-info.yaml      │
  │             in EVERY repo. No manual location management!        │
  │  ⚠️ Requires: GitHub App or PAT with org read access             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Verifying Catalog Setup — Real Commands
  # 1. Check if Backstage is running and the catalog is accessible
  $ curl http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities | jq '.'
  # Returns JSON array of all registered entities

  # 2. Check a specific entity by kind, namespace, and name
  $ curl http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities/by-name/Component/default/jobpost

  # 3. Force a catalog refresh (useful after adding new locations)
  $ curl -X POST http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/refresh

  # 4. Troubleshooting: Check backend logs for ingestion errors
  $ yarn dev  # Watch the terminal output for catalog-related log lines
  # Look for: "Processing entity", "Entity ingested", "Failed to read location"

Chapter 7 Quick Wins for CBA: The Catalog is the FIRST thing you set up. Register entities via catalog.locations (URL, file, or auto-discovery). The ingestion pipeline is Location → Provider → Processor → Database. Always verify with curl :7007/api/catalog/entities. Troubleshoot ingestion failures by checking backend logs, YAML syntax, and location URLs. Remember: 3 location types — URL (GitHub), file (local), and auto-discovery (GitHub Org / K8s).

📊 Chapter 7 — Visual Summary: The Software Catalog

📦Catalog DefinedSingle source of truth for ALL software. Auto-discovers, auto-updates.
📍LocationsURL (GitHub), File (local), Auto-Discovery (GitHub Org/K8s). 3 types.
📡ProvidersRead entities from sources. GithubEntityProvider, UrlReader, K8s Provider.
⚙️ProcessorsTransform & validate. PlaceholderProcessor, EntityProcessor. Validate YAML.
💾DatabasePostgreSQL stores all entities. SQLite for dev only. API queries database.
🔍SearchFull-text search across all entities. Find services, APIs, docs instantly.
🖥️Entity PageOverview, Docs, CI/CD, K8s, API tabs. All info in one place.
🔄RefreshEntities auto-refresh on schedule. Force refresh via API POST /refresh.
🛠️TroubleshootCheck backend logs, YAML syntax, location URL. curl /api/catalog/entities.
🏗️FoundationEverything depends on Catalog. Set it up FIRST. Without it, Backstage is empty.

📝 Chapter 7 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the Software Catalog and why is it considered the foundation of Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

The Software Catalog is the central registry of all software in your organization — services, APIs, websites, libraries, databases, teams. It's the foundation because everything else in Backstage depends on it: TechDocs needs entities to show docs, the K8s plugin needs entities to show deployments, CI/CD cards need entities to show build status. Without the Catalog, Backstage is just an empty shell.

Explanation

Think of the Catalog as the "phone book" that every other plugin consults. When you visit an entity page, Backstage queries the catalog database to find the entity, then each plugin renders its tab based on the entity's annotations and metadata.

Q2
Explain the 3-stage catalog ingestion pipeline.
Show Answer
Answer

Stage 1 — Location: Where to look (GitHub URL, local file, K8s cluster). Configured in catalog.locations.
Stage 2 — Entity Provider: How to read entities. Each location type has a matching provider (e.g., GithubEntityProvider for GitHub URLs).
Stage 3 — Processor: How to transform. Validates YAML, resolves placeholders, enriches with external data, then stores in the database.

Explanation

This pipeline runs on every catalog refresh. If an entity doesn't appear, the failure could be at any stage: wrong location URL (Stage 1), provider not configured (Stage 2), or invalid YAML syntax (Stage 3). Check backend logs to identify which stage failed.

Q3
What are the 3 types of catalog locations? Give an example of when you'd use each.
Show Answer
Answer

1. URL Location — Points to a catalog-info.yaml on GitHub/GitLab. Use for: individual services in production. Example: type: url, target: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml
2. File Location — Points to a local YAML file. Use for: local development and testing. Example: type: file, target: ./local-entities.yaml
3. Auto-Discovery — GitHub Org provider or K8s provider. Use for: large orgs that want automatic scanning. Example: providers.github.organization: anihpj

Explanation

The CBA will ask which location type to use in a given scenario. URL locations are most common in production because they're explicit. Auto-discovery is powerful but requires additional setup (GitHub App/PAT). File locations are for local development only.

Q4
You add a catalog location but the entity doesn't appear. List 5 things you'd check, in order.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Check backend logs — Look for "Failed to read location" or YAML parse errors
2. Verify the URL — Does the YAML file exist at the specified path? Try opening it in a browser
3. Check GitHub token — For private repos, is ${GITHUB_TOKEN} set and valid?
4. Validate YAML syntax — Remove trailing commas, check indentation, verify required fields (name, kind, apiVersion)
5. Query the API — curl :7007/api/catalog/entities to see if the entity is stored but not displaying

Explanation

This is a practical troubleshooting flow the CBA tests. Always start with logs — they show the exact error. YAML syntax errors (trailing commas, wrong indentation) are the #1 cause of ingestion failures. The API check confirms whether the issue is storage (entity not in DB) or display (entity in DB but not in UI).

Q5
What does the GitHub Org auto-discovery provider do, and what's required to set it up?
Show Answer
Answer

The GitHub Org provider automatically scans all repositories in a GitHub organization for catalog-info.yaml files. Instead of adding each repo as a separate URL location, you configure the org once and Backstage discovers everything automatically. Requires: (1) A GitHub token with org read access, (2) catalog.providers.github.organization config in app-config.yaml, (3) Each repo must have catalog-info.yaml at the root.

Explanation

This is the most powerful location type for large organizations. It eliminates the manual work of adding individual URL locations for every new service. The provider runs on a configurable schedule (e.g., every 30 minutes) and picks up new repos automatically.

Q6
How do you verify that entities are successfully registered in the Catalog? Name 3 methods.
Show Answer
Answer

1. UI: Go to http://localhost:3000/catalog — entities appear in the grid
2. API: curl http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities | jq '.' — returns JSON list of all entities
3. Search: Type the entity name in the Backstage search bar — entity should appear in results
4. Backend logs: Watch terminal for "Entity ingested" or "Processing entity" messages

Explanation

The API method is the most reliable — it shows exactly what's in the database regardless of UI state. If an entity appears in the API response but not in the UI, there may be a frontend rendering issue. If it doesn't appear in the API, the ingestion failed.

Q7
What is the difference between an Entity Provider and a Processor in the catalog pipeline?
Show Answer
Answer

Entity Provider: Reads raw entity data from a source. It knows HOW to read from GitHub, local files, or K8s clusters. Output: raw entity definitions (YAML text).
Processor: Transforms, validates, and enriches raw entity data. It checks YAML syntax, resolves placeholders, adds auto-generated fields, and stores the final entity. Output: validated, enriched entity stored in the database.

Explanation

Think of providers as "readers" and processors as "transformers". The CBA may ask which component would fail if a YAML file has syntax errors (answer: the Processor, not the Provider — the Provider can still read the file, but the Processor fails when trying to parse it).

Q8
Where in app-config.yaml do you configure catalog locations? Write the exact YAML structure.
Show Answer
Answer
catalog:
  locations:
    - type: url
      target: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml
    - type: url
      target: https://github.com/anihpj/frontend/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml

Each location is an item in the catalog.locations array with type (url or file) and target (the actual path).

Explanation

The catalog top-level section in app-config.yaml controls all catalog settings. locations is for manual/explicit locations. providers (sibling to locations) is for auto-discovery providers like GitHub Org. The CBA may ask you to identify which section to modify for a given scenario.

Q9
Your Backstage catalog shows 50 entities. You add a new catalog-info.yaml to a repo but after 5 minutes it still doesn't appear. What's the likely issue?
Show Answer
Answer

The most likely issue is that you didn't add the repository as a catalog location in app-config.yaml. Creating catalog-info.yaml in a repo is not enough — you must also tell Backstage WHERE to look for it. Add a catalog.locations entry pointing to the new YAML file, or if using auto-discovery, wait for the next refresh cycle. Then force a refresh: curl -X POST :7007/api/catalog/refresh.

Explanation

This is a common beginner mistake. catalog-info.yaml tells Backstage WHAT the entity is. catalog.locations tells Backstage WHERE to find it. You need both. With auto-discovery, new repos are picked up on the next scheduled refresh (default: every 30 minutes).

Q10
What command would you use to force a catalog refresh after adding new locations?
Show Answer
Answer

curl -X POST http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/refresh — This triggers an immediate refresh of all catalog locations. Entities will be re-fetched, re-processed, and re-stored. Use this after adding new locations or updating existing catalog-info.yaml files to see changes without waiting for the scheduled refresh.

Explanation

The catalog refresh API is essential for development workflows. Without it, you'd wait up to 30 minutes for changes to appear. In production, the catalog refreshes on a schedule — you typically don't need to force it manually. The CBA may ask about this command in a troubleshooting scenario.

Chapter 8: Catalog Entities — Kinds, Types & Relationships

Entities are the building blocks of the Software Catalog. Each entity represents something in your ecosystem — a service, an API, a database, a team. In this chapter, you'll learn all 7 entity kinds, when to use each, and how they connect through relationships.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 📋 Core Concept 🎯 CBA 22%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Master all 7 entity kinds: Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group
  • ✅ Write complete YAML for each entity kind with real anihpj examples
  • ✅ Understand the 8 entity relationship types and how they connect entities
  • ✅ Use the decision tree to choose the right entity kind for any scenario
  • ✅ Map your organization's architecture to Backstage entities
🎓 CBA Relevance
Backstage Catalog 22%

8.1 Component Entity — The Most Common Kind

A Component is any piece of software — a service, website, library, CLI tool, or mobile app. It's the most frequently used entity kind. Every microservice in your organization should be a Component.

Component Entity — jobpost Example
  # catalog-info.yaml for anihpj/jobpost
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Component
  metadata:
    name: jobpost
    description: Job posting and recruitment management application
    annotations:
      github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
      backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
    tags: [python, django, postgresql]
    links:
      - url: https://jobpost.anihpj.com
        title: Production
        icon: dashboard
  spec:
    type: service              # ← service | website | library | CLI
    lifecycle: production      # ← experimental | production | deprecated
    owner: team-platform       # ← WHO is responsible (User or Group)
    system: anihpj-system      # ← Which System this belongs to
    providesApis:
      - jobpost-api            # ← APIs this component exposes
    dependsOn:
      - resource:postgres-db   # ← Resources this component depends on
      - resource:redis-cache
spec.typeWhen to UseExample
serviceBackend services, APIs, microservicesjobpost, auth-service
websiteFrontend apps, SPAs, static sitesanihpj-dashboard
libraryShared code libraries, npm packagesanihpj-utils
CLICommand-line toolsanihpj-cli

8.2 API Entity — Document Your Interfaces

An API entity represents a formal API definition — REST (OpenAPI), GraphQL, gRPC, or AsyncAPI. APIs are separate from Components because multiple Components might consume the same API, and the API spec can be auto-generated from code.

API Entity — jobpost-api Example
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: API
  metadata:
    name: jobpost-api
    description: REST API for job posting management
    tags: [rest, openapi]
  spec:
    type: openapi              # ← openapi | asyncapi | graphql | grpc
    lifecycle: production
    owner: team-platform
    system: anihpj-system
    definition:
      $text: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/openapi.yaml

8.3 System & Domain Entities — Organize at Scale

System groups related Components and Resources. Domain groups related Systems (business area). Together they create a hierarchy: Domain → System → Components + Resources.

System & Domain Hierarchy for anihpj
  🏢 hr-domain (Domain)
   └── 🖥️ anihpj-system (System)
        ├── 📦 jobpost (Component: service)
        ├── 📡 jobpost-api (API: openapi)
        ├── 💾 postgres-db (Resource: database)
        └── 🔴 redis-cache (Resource: cache)

8.4 User & Group Entities — People and Teams

User represents a person (auto-discovered from auth provider). Group represents a team, squad, or organizational unit. Groups can have parent/child relationships to model your org chart.

Group Entity — team-platform Example
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Group
  metadata:
    name: team-platform
    description: Platform Engineering Team
  spec:
    type: team
    profile:
      displayName: Platform Engineering
      email: platform@anihpj.com
    parent: engineering-department
    children: [team-fe, team-data]
    members: [jane.doe, mike.smith]

8.5 Resource Entity — Infrastructure Components

A Resource represents infrastructure that Components depend on — databases, caches, message queues, S3 buckets, CDNs. Resources are separate from Components because multiple services might share the same database.

Resource Entity — postgres-db for anihpj
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Resource
  metadata:
    name: postgres-db
    description: PostgreSQL database for anihpj services
  spec:
    type: database             # ← database | s3-bucket | pubsub-topic
    owner: team-data
    system: anihpj-system
    dependencyOf:
      - component:jobpost      # ← Which Components depend on this

8.6 Entity Relationships — How Everything Connects

anihpj Entity Relationship Map
                      ┌─────────────────────┐
                      │  🏢 hr-domain       │ ← Domain
                      └──────────┬──────────┘
                                 │
                      ┌──────────▼──────────┐
                      │  🖥️ anihpj-system   │ ← System
                      └──────────┬──────────┘
                                 │
            ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
            │                    │                     │
      ┌─────▼─────┐       ┌──────▼──────┐       ┌─────▼─────┐
      │ 📦 jobpost│       │ 💾 postgres │       │ 🔴 redis  │
      │ Component │──▶──▶│  Resource   │       │ Resource  │
      │ (service) │depends│ (database)  │       │ (cache)   │
      └─────┬─────┘       └─────────────┘       └───────────┘
            │
            │ providesApis
            ▼
      ┌─────────────┐
      │ 📡 jobpost  │ ← API
      │    -api     │
      │ (openapi)   │
      └─────────────┘

      Owner: 👥 team-platform (Group)
      Members: 👤 jane.doe, 👤 mike.smith (Users)

8.7 Entity Relationship Types — Complete Reference

RelationshipDirectionExampleUsed In
ownerEntity → User/Groupjobpost owned by team-platformComponent, API, System, Resource, Domain
systemComponent/API/Resource → Systemjobpost part of anihpj-systemComponent, API, Resource
domainSystem → Domainanihpj-system in hr-domainSystem
providesApisComponent → APIjobpost provides jobpost-apiComponent
consumesApisComponent → APIfrontend consumes jobpost-apiComponent
dependsOnComponent → Resource/Componentjobpost depends on postgres-dbComponent
dependencyOfResource → Componentpostgres-db dependency of jobpostResource
parent/childrenGroup → Groupengineering parent of platformGroup
memberOfUser → Groupjane.doe member of team-platformUser
subcomponentOfComponent → Componentjobpost-admin subcomponent of jobpostComponent
⚠️ KEY RULE for CBA

owner is MANDATORY for Component, API, System, Resource, and Domain. All other relationships are optional but recommended for full visibility. The CBA exam will test your understanding of which relationships are required vs optional.

8.8 Entity Kind Decision Tree — Which Kind Should You Use?

Choosing the Right Entity Kind
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🌳 ENTITY KIND DECISION TREE                                  │
  │                                                                │
  │  Q: "Is it a piece of software?"                               │
  │    ├── YES → 📦 COMPONENT (service, website, library, CLI)     │
  │    │                                                            │
  │    ├── "Is it an API definition?"                              │
  │    │   YES → 📡 API (openapi, asyncapi, graphql, grpc)         │
  │    │                                                            │
  │    ├── "Is it infrastructure?"                                 │
  │    │   YES → 💾 RESOURCE (database, cache, queue, bucket)      │
  │    │                                                            │
  │    └── "Is it an organizational unit?"                         │
  │                                                                │
  │  Q: "Is it a person?"                                          │
  │    ├── YES → 👤 USER                                           │
  │    └── NO → "Is it a team?" → 👥 GROUP                        │
  │                                                                │
  │  Q: "Is it a grouping of Components + Resources?"              │
  │    ├── YES → 🖥️ SYSTEM                                         │
  │    └── "Is it a grouping of Systems?" → 🏢 DOMAIN             │
  │                                                                │
  │  Q: "Is it a blueprint for creating projects?"                 │
  │    └── YES → 🚀 TEMPLATE                                       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📝 Chapter 8 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 7 entity kinds in Backstage, and give an example of each for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Component — jobpost (Django service)
2. API — jobpost-api (OpenAPI REST API)
3. System — anihpj-system (groups components)
4. Domain — hr-domain (business area)
5. Resource — postgres-db (database)
6. User — jane.doe (person)
7. Group — team-platform (team)

Explanation

There's also Template (8th kind for Software Templates) and Location (9th kind for catalog locations). The CBA focuses on the 7 main kinds. Know which one to use for any given scenario is critical.

Q2
What is the difference between a System and a Domain? When would you create each?
Show Answer
Answer

System: A collection of Components and Resources that work together. Example: anihpj-system groups jobpost, postgres-db, redis-cache, and jobpost-api. Domain: A business area that contains multiple Systems. Example: hr-domain contains anihpj-system, payroll-system, benefits-system. Create a System when you have multiple related components. Create a Domain when you have multiple Systems in the same business area.

Explanation

Think: System = technical grouping, Domain = business grouping. A Domain has Systems. A System has Components, APIs, and Resources. The hierarchy is: Domain → System → Component/API/Resource.

Q3
Write a complete Component YAML for the anihpj jobpost service, including at least 4 annotations.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
kind: Component
metadata:
  name: jobpost
  description: Job posting and recruitment service
  annotations:
    github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
    backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
    backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: anihpj-prod
    jenkins.io/job-full-name: anihpj/jobpost-build
  tags: [python, django, recruitment]
spec:
  type: service
  lifecycle: production
  owner: team-platform
  system: anihpj-system
  providesApis: [jobpost-api]
  dependsOn: [resource:postgres-db, resource:redis-cache]
Explanation

Every Component MUST have: apiVersion, kind, metadata.name, spec.type, spec.lifecycle, spec.owner. Annotations are optional but essential for integrations (GitHub, TechDocs, K8s, CI/CD). The CBA may ask you to identify missing required fields.

Q4
What is the difference between spec.type and spec.lifecycle on a Component?
Show Answer
Answer

spec.type: WHAT kind of software it is — service, website, library, or CLI. This doesn't change often.
spec.lifecycle: WHERE it is in its development journey — experimental, production, or deprecated. This changes as the software matures.

Explanation

Example: A new service starts as lifecycle: experimental while in development, becomes lifecycle: production when deployed, and eventually lifecycle: deprecated when being phased out. The type (service) stays the same throughout.

Q5
Your jobpost component depends on a PostgreSQL database. Should you model the database as a Component or a Resource? Why?
Show Answer
Answer

Model it as a Resource. Resources are for infrastructure that Components depend on — databases, caches, queues, buckets. They're distinct from Components because: (1) Multiple Components can share a Resource, (2) Resources don't have their own source code repo, (3) Resources are managed differently (infra-as-code vs application code). Use kind: Resource with spec.type: database.

Explanation

The key distinction: Components = software YOU write. Resources = infrastructure you USE. If it has its own codebase and CI/CD pipeline, it's a Component. If it's provisioned by Terraform or a managed service, it's a Resource.

Q6
What's the difference between spec.providesApis and spec.consumesApis on a Component?
Show Answer
Answer

providesApis: APIs this Component exposes to others. The Component is the API producer.
consumesApis: APIs this Component calls from others. The Component is the API consumer.
Example: jobpost provides jobpost-api (other services call it). frontend consumes jobpost-api (it calls jobpost's API).

Explanation

This helps map dependencies: if jobpost-api goes down, you know both jobpost (provider) and frontend (consumer) are affected. The relationship graph in Backstage shows these connections visually.

Q7
What is the difference between spec.dependsOn and spec.dependencyOf?
Show Answer
Answer

dependsOn: Used ON Components to list what they depend on. "jobpost depends on postgres-db."
dependencyOf: Used ON Resources to list which Components depend on them. "postgres-db is a dependency of jobpost."
They represent the SAME relationship from opposite sides. Use dependsOn on the Component. Use dependencyOf on the Resource.

Explanation

This bidirectional linking means you can navigate in either direction: from jobpost, you see it depends on postgres-db. From postgres-db, you see it's used by jobpost. Both are important for impact analysis when planning maintenance.

Q8
You need to model your organization's engineering department hierarchy in Backstage. The Platform team, Frontend team, and Data team all report to Engineering. Which entity kind(s) do you use?
Show Answer
Answer

Use Group entities with parent and children relationships:
Engineering → parent of Platform, Frontend, Data.
Platform → child of Engineering.
The CBA exam tests this exact pattern — modeling org charts with Group entities.

Explanation

Groups support hierarchical relationships. Each Group can have one parent and multiple children. This matches how organizations work: departments contain teams, teams contain sub-teams. Users are linked to Groups via memberOf.

Q9
Which entity kinds require the owner field? Which entity kind has owner as optional?
Show Answer
Answer

owner is REQUIRED for: Component, API, System, Domain, Resource
owner is OPTIONAL for: User, Group, Template
The CBA exam explicitly tests this — know that 5 out of 7 main kinds require owner, and only User and Group don't (since they ARE the owners).

Explanation

If you omit owner from a Component, API, System, Domain, or Resource, the entity will fail catalog ingestion. The error message will say "owner is required". This is a common YAML mistake beginners make.

Q10
A developer asks: "I want to register our team's Jenkins pipeline in Backstage so it shows on the jobpost entity page. What entity kind do I create?"
Show Answer
Answer

You don't create a new entity kind. Jenkins integration is done through annotations on the existing jobpost Component. Add jenkins.io/job-full-name: anihpj/jobpost-build to the Component's metadata.annotations. The Jenkins plugin reads this annotation and renders the CI/CD card. No new entity needed.

Explanation

This is a key concept: integrations (Jenkins, PagerDuty, Sentry, SonarQube) don't create new entities. They enhance existing entities through annotations. The annotation tells the plugin where to find the external resource. The CBA will test this distinction.

📊 Chapter 8 — Visual Summary: Entity Kinds & Relationships

📦ComponentSoftware pieces: services, websites, libraries, CLIs. Most common kind.
📡APIFormal API specs: openapi, asyncapi, graphql, grpc. Separate from code.
🖥️SystemGroups Components + Resources. Technical grouping. 1 System = many Components.
🏢DomainBusiness area grouping Systems. HR, Finance, etc. Higher level than System.
💾ResourceInfrastructure: databases, caches, queues, buckets. NOT software you write.
👤UserPeople. Auto-discovered from auth. Linked to Groups via memberOf.
👥GroupTeams, squads, departments. Parent/child hierarchy. Models org chart.
🔗10 Relationshipsowner, system, domain, providesApis, consumesApis, dependsOn, dependencyOf, parent, children, memberOf.
⚠️Required: ownerMANDATORY for Component, API, System, Domain, Resource. Optional for User, Group.
🏷️AnnotationsLink entities to external systems. Jenkins, PagerDuty, Sentry, etc. No new entity needed.

Chapter 8 CBA Quick Wins: Memorize the 7 entity kinds and their use cases. owner is mandatory for 5 kinds. The 10 relationship types connect everything. Annotations link to external tools without creating new entities. Use the decision tree: software → Component, API definition → API, infrastructure → Resource, team → Group, person → User, grouping → System/Domain.

Chapter 9: Catalog YAML & Descriptors — Writing catalog-info.yaml

Every entity in Backstage is defined in a catalog-info.yaml file. This chapter dives deep into the YAML structure — the 4 required top-level fields, metadata, spec properties for each kind, annotations, and how to validate your YAML. This is the most hands-on chapter for the CBA exam.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 📝 YAML 🎯 CBA 22%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Master the 4 required top-level fields: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec
  • ✅ Understand every metadata field — name, annotations, labels, tags, links
  • ✅ Know spec properties for ALL 7 entity kinds
  • ✅ Use annotations to link entities to external systems
  • ✅ Validate YAML with backstage-cli and fix common errors
🎓 CBA Relevance
Backstage Catalog 22% Customizing Backstage 32%

9.1 catalog-info.yaml Structure — The 4 Required Fields

Every catalog-info.yaml MUST have exactly 4 top-level fields: apiVersion, kind, metadata, and spec. If any of these are missing, the entity will fail ingestion.

Anatomy of catalog-info.yaml
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1   ← 1️⃣ API VERSION (always this value)
  kind: Component                      ← 2️⃣ ENTITY KIND (Component|API|System|...)
  metadata:                            ← 3️⃣ IDENTITY (name, description, annotations)
    name: jobpost                      ← REQUIRED: unique name per kind
    description: "Job posting app"     ← Optional but recommended
    annotations:                       ← Key-value pairs for integrations
      github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
    labels:                            ← Key-value for filtering
      language: python
    tags: [django, recruitment]        ← Simple string tags
    links:                             ← External URLs
      - url: https://jobpost.anihpj.com
        title: Production
  spec:                                ← 4️⃣ TYPE-SPECIFIC PROPERTIES
    type: service                      ← Depends on 'kind'
    lifecycle: production
    owner: team-platform               ← REQUIRED for most kinds
    system: anihpj-system

9.2 Metadata — The Identity Layer

FieldRequired?PurposeExample
name✅ YesUnique identifier per kindjobpost
namespace❌ No (default: "default")Multi-tenant isolation. Separate entities by team/organihpj-team
title❌ NoDisplay name (differs from name)Job Posting Service
description❌ No (recommended)Human-readable descriptionRecruitment management
labels❌ NoKey-value pairs for filteringlanguage: python
tags❌ NoSimple string tags[django, backend]
annotations❌ No (critical for integrations)backstage.io/* for integrationsgithub.com/project-slug
links❌ NoArray of external URLsProd URL, Dashboard, Docs
💡 Labels vs Tags vs Annotations

Labels are key-value pairs for filtering (e.g., language: python → find all Python services). Tags are simple strings for searching (e.g., [django] → quick search). Annotations are key-value pairs for integrations (e.g., github.com/project-slug → connects to GitHub repo). Labels filter, Tags search, Annotations integrate.

9.3 Spec — Type-Specific Properties for Each Kind

Component Spec

type (service|website|library|CLI), lifecycle (experimental|production|deprecated), owner, system, subcomponentOf, providesApis, consumesApis, dependsOn

API Spec

type (openapi|asyncapi|graphql|grpc), lifecycle, owner, system, definition (the actual API spec file)

Resource Spec

type (database|s3-bucket|pubsub-topic|...), owner, system, dependsOn, dependencyOf

System & Domain Spec

System: owner, domain. Domain: owner only. Both are simple — they're organizational containers.

User & Group Spec

User: profile (email, displayName, picture), memberOf (Groups). Group: type (team|department|...), profile, parent, children.

9.4 Annotations — The Glue to External Systems

Core backstage.io/* Annotations

AnnotationPurposeSet ByExample
backstage.io/managed-by-locationWhich location registered this entityAuto (Backstage)url:https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml
backstage.io/managed-by-origin-locationOriginal location (before processing)Auto (Backstage)url:https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml
backstage.io/view-urlLink to view source codeAuto or Manualhttps://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/tree/main
backstage.io/edit-urlLink to edit this entity's YAMLAuto or Manualhttps://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/edit/main/catalog-info.yaml
backstage.io/techdocs-refTechDocs locationManualdir:. | url:https://docs.anihpj.com
backstage.io/kubernetes-idMatch to K8s Deployment by nameManualjobpost
backstage.io/kubernetes-namespaceK8s namespace scopeManualanihpj-prod
backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selectorMatch K8s resources by labelManualapp=jobpost,component=backend
💡 Auto vs Manual Annotations

managed-by-location and managed-by-origin-location are automatically set by Backstage — you never write them manually. view-url and edit-url can be auto-derived from GitHub URLs. All kubernetes-* and integration annotations (github.com/*, jenkins.io/*) must be manually added to your catalog-info.yaml.

GitHub / GitLab Annotations

AnnotationPurpose
github.com/project-slugLink to GitHub repo (owner/repo)
github.com/team-slugLink to GitHub team
gitlab.com/project-slugLink to GitLab project

CI/CD Annotations

AnnotationPurpose
jenkins.io/job-full-nameJenkins job reference
github.com/workflow-slugGitHub Actions workflow

Monitoring Annotations

AnnotationPurpose
pagerduty.com/service-idPagerDuty service
sentry.io/project-slugSentry error tracking
sonarqube.org/project-keySonarQube code quality

Kubernetes Annotations

AnnotationPurpose
backstage.io/kubernetes-idMatch to K8s Deployment
backstage.io/kubernetes-namespaceK8s namespace
backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selectorSelect K8s objects by label

9.5 Multi-Entity Files — Using the --- Separator

Multiple Entities in One File
  # catalog-info.yaml — All anihpj entities in ONE file
  ---
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Component
  metadata:
    name: jobpost
  spec:
    type: service
    lifecycle: production
    owner: team-platform
  ---
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: API
  metadata:
    name: jobpost-api
  spec:
    type: openapi
    lifecycle: production
    owner: team-platform
    definition:
      $text: ./openapi.yaml
  ---
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Resource
  metadata:
    name: postgres-db
  spec:
    type: database
    owner: team-data

9.6 Validating catalog-info.yaml

Validation Commands
  # Validate a single entity file
  $ yarn backstage-cli validate-entity ./catalog-info.yaml

  # Common errors and fixes:
  # ❌ "owner is required" → Add spec.owner
  # ❌ "unexpected trailing comma" → Remove trailing comma after last item
  # ❌ "YAML syntax error" → Check indentation (use 2 spaces, not tabs)
  # ❌ "unknown field" → Check field name spelling

  # Check if entity is in the catalog
  $ curl http://localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities/by-name/Component/default/jobpost

📝 Chapter 9 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 4 required top-level fields in every catalog-info.yaml?
Show Answer
Answer

1. apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
2. kind — Component | API | System | Domain | Resource | User | Group
3. metadata — at minimum must contain name
4. spec — type-specific properties (varies by kind)

Explanation

If ANY of these 4 fields are missing, the entity WILL FAIL ingestion. The CBA exam tests this — you might be shown a YAML snippet and asked to identify what's missing.

Q2
What is the difference between labels, tags, and annotations in metadata?
Show Answer
Answer

Labels: Key-value pairs for filtering entities (e.g., language: python). Use in catalog filters.
Tags: Simple strings for search (e.g., [django, backend]). Quick search in the search bar.
Annotations: Key-value pairs for integrations (e.g., github.com/project-slug). Connect to external tools.
The CBA will ask you which one to use for a given purpose.

Q3
You have a catalog-info.yaml that fails ingestion with "YAML syntax error". What's the most common cause?
Show Answer
Answer

The most common causes are: (1) Trailing commas after the last item in a list (YAML doesn't use commas at all!), (2) Wrong indentation — YAML uses 2-space indentation, not tabs, (3) Mixed tabs and spaces, (4) Missing colon after a key. Always validate with yarn backstage-cli validate-entity before committing.

Q4
How do you define multiple entities in a single catalog-info.yaml file?
Show Answer
Answer

Use the --- separator between entity definitions. Each entity starts with --- on its own line, followed by the complete entity definition (apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec). This is standard YAML multi-document syntax. All entities in the file share the same location.

Q5
Which annotation links a Component to its documentation in TechDocs?
Show Answer
Answer

backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. — This tells TechDocs to look for an mkdocs.yml file and /docs folder in the same directory as the catalog-info.yaml. Alternatives: url:https://docs.example.com for external docs.

Q6
What annotation links a Component to its Kubernetes deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost — This matches the Component to a K8s Deployment with the same name. Alternatively, use backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: anihpj-prod to scope to a specific namespace, or backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selector: app=jobpost for label-based matching.

Q7
What is $text used for in an API entity's spec.definition?
Show Answer
Answer

$text is a placeholder that points to an external file containing the actual API spec. Example: definition: $text: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/openapi.yaml. The catalog processor fetches the file at that URL and inlines it. Other placeholders include $json and $yaml.

Q8
What command validates a catalog-info.yaml file?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn backstage-cli validate-entity ./catalog-info.yaml — This checks the YAML syntax and validates that the entity has all required fields (apiVersion, kind, metadata.name, spec). Run this before committing catalog-info.yaml files to avoid ingestion failures.

Q9
You want to add a "View Dashboard" link to a Component's entity page. Where in the YAML do you add it?
Show Answer
Answer

In metadata.links. Example:
links:
- url: https://grafana.anihpj.com/d/jobpost
title: Dashboard
icon: dashboard
Links appear on the entity's Overview page. Each link can have a url, title, and optional icon.

Q10
What happens if you omit the kind field from catalog-info.yaml?
Show Answer
Answer

The entity will fail catalog ingestion. The error message will say something like "kind is required" or "missing required field: kind". Without kind, Backstage doesn't know what type of entity this is (Component? API? Resource?) and cannot process it. The same applies to apiVersion, metadata.name, and spec — all 4 are mandatory.

📋 JSON Schema Validation

Backstage validates all catalog-info.yaml files against JSON Schema definitions. Each entity kind has its own schema that defines required fields, allowed values, and field types. If your YAML doesn't match the schema, ingestion fails. Use yarn backstage-cli validate-entity which checks against these schemas locally before you commit. The schemas are in the Backstage repo at plugins/catalog-backend/schema/.

📊 Chapter 9 — Visual Summary: YAML & Descriptors

📋4 Required FieldsapiVersion, kind, metadata, spec. Missing any = ingestion failure.
📛metadata.nameUnique per kind. Only required metadata field. Use kebab-case.
🏷️Labels vs TagsLabels = key:value filtering. Tags = simple search strings. Annotations = integrations.
🔗Annotations5 groups: core, GitHub/GitLab, CI/CD, monitoring, Kubernetes. Link to external tools.
📝--- SeparatorMulti-entity files. 3 dashes between entities. All share one location.
✅validate-entityyarn backstage-cli validate-entity. Run before commit. Catches YAML errors.
📂$text PlaceholderPoints to external files. Catalog processor fetches & inlines. API specs, docs.
⚠️Common ErrorsTrailing commas, tab indentation, missing kind, missing owner, YAML syntax.

Chapter 10: Integrating Kubernetes with the Catalog

Backstage's Kubernetes plugin bridges the gap between your Catalog and your running infrastructure. It shows live K8s resource status (Deployments, Pods, Services) directly on entity pages. You'll learn how to configure it, link entities to K8s resources, and deploy anihpj to Kubernetes with full Backstage visibility.
