  • ✅ Understand what the Kubernetes plugin does and how it works
  • ✅ Configure the K8s plugin in app-config.yaml with cluster details
  • ✅ Link Catalog entities to K8s resources via annotations
  • ✅ View live K8s status on entity pages
  • ✅ Deploy anihpj/jobpost to Kubernetes with full Docker + K8s YAML
🎓 CBA Relevance
Backstage Catalog 22% Infrastructure 22%

10.1 The Kubernetes Plugin — What It Does

The K8s plugin connects to your Kubernetes clusters and displays live resource status on entity pages. When you view a Component in Backstage, the "Kubernetes" tab shows its Deployments, Pods, Services, and Ingresses — all in real time.

K8s Plugin Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │  ☸️ K8s Cluster      │───────▶│  Backstage               │
  │                     │        │  Kubernetes Plugin        │
  │  Deployment: jobpost│        │                          │
  │   ├── Pod 1 (Ready) │        │  jobpost Entity Page:    │
  │   ├── Pod 2 (Ready) │        │  ┌─────────────────────┐ │
  │   └── Pod 3 (Ready) │        │  │ ☸️ KUBERNETES       │ │
  │                     │        │  │ Deployment: jobpost │ │
  │  Service: jobpost   │        │  │ 3/3 Pods Running   │ │
  │  Ingress: jobpost   │        │  │ Service: :8000     │ │
  │                     │        │  │ Ingress: URL       │ │
  └─────────────────────┘        │  └─────────────────────┘ │
                                  └──────────────────────────┘

10.2 Kubernetes Plugin Configuration

Step 1: Install the Plugins

Install K8s Frontend + Backend Plugins
  # Install frontend plugin (adds the "Kubernetes" tab to entity pages)
  $ yarn add --cwd packages/app @backstage/plugin-kubernetes

  # Install backend plugin (connects to K8s API servers)
  $ yarn add --cwd packages/backend @backstage/plugin-kubernetes-backend

  # Register in packages/app/src/plugins.ts:
  export { kubernetesPlugin } from '@backstage/plugin-kubernetes';

  # Register in packages/backend/src/index.ts:
  backend.add(import('@backstage/plugin-kubernetes-backend'));

Step 2: Configure app-config.yaml

app-config.yaml — K8s Plugin Configuration
  # app-config.yaml
  kubernetes:
    serviceLocatorMethod:
      type: 'multiTenant'                # ← How services are found
    clusterLocatorMethods:
      - type: 'config'                   # ← Static cluster list
        clusters:
          - name: 'anihpj-prod'          # ← Cluster display name
            url: 'https://k8s-api.anihpj.com'  # ← API Server URL
            authProvider: 'serviceAccount'      # ← Auth method
            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_SA_TOKEN}  # ← Token from env var
            skipTLSVerify: false         # ← TLS verification
            dashboardUrl: 'https://k8s-dash.anihpj.com'  # ← K8s dashboard link

Step 3: Create K8s ServiceAccount & RBAC

Backstage needs read-only access to your K8s cluster. Create a ServiceAccount with appropriate RBAC permissions:

K8s RBAC for Backstage Service Account
  # 1. Create ServiceAccount
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: ServiceAccount
  metadata:
    name: backstage-reader
    namespace: backstage
  ---
  # 2. Create ClusterRole (read-only)
  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
  kind: ClusterRole
  metadata:
    name: backstage-reader
  rules:
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["pods", "services", "configmaps"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
  - apiGroups: ["apps"]
    resources: ["deployments", "replicasets"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
  - apiGroups: ["networking.k8s.io"]
    resources: ["ingresses"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
  ---
  # 3. Bind ClusterRole to ServiceAccount
  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
  kind: ClusterRoleBinding
  metadata:
    name: backstage-reader
  subjects:
  - kind: ServiceAccount
    name: backstage-reader
    namespace: backstage
  roleRef:
    kind: ClusterRole
    name: backstage-reader
    apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
  ---
  # 4. Get the ServiceAccount token
  $ kubectl create token backstage-reader -n backstage
  # → Copy this token to K8S_SA_TOKEN env var
⚠️ CBA Exam: Backstage K8s Permissions

Backstage needs get, list, watch on Pods, Services, Deployments, ReplicaSets, and Ingresses. It NEVER creates, updates, or deletes K8s resources — it's read-only. The CBA may ask what minimum RBAC permissions Backstage requires.

10.3 Linking K8s Resources to Catalog Entities

Backstage matches K8s resources to Catalog entities using annotations in catalog-info.yaml. There are 3 linking methods:

3 Methods to Link K8s to Entities
  # Method 1: By Name (simplest)
  metadata:
    annotations:
      backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost
  # → Matches K8s Deployment named "jobpost"

  # Method 2: By Namespace
  metadata:
    annotations:
      backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: anihpj-prod
  # → Shows ALL K8s resources in that namespace

  # Method 3: By Label Selector (most flexible)
  metadata:
    annotations:
      backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selector: app=jobpost
  # → Matches any K8s resource with label "app=jobpost"

10.4 Viewing K8s Resources in Backstage

When the K8s plugin is configured, every entity with a K8s annotation gets a "Kubernetes" tab. The data is live — it queries the K8s API directly each time you view the tab.

What You SeeDetails ShownWhere It Comes From
DeploymentsReplicas (ready/desired), strategy, selectorapps/v1 Deployment API
PodsStatus (Running/Pending/CrashLoopBackOff), restart count, agecore/v1 Pod API
Pod LogsStdout/stderr streams (last 100 lines)Pod logs sub-resource
Pod EventsSchedule, pull, start events with timestampscore/v1 Event API
ServicesType (ClusterIP/LoadBalancer), ports, selectorcore/v1 Service API
IngressesHost, path, TLS config, backend servicenetworking.k8s.io/v1 Ingress API
Resource UsageCPU/memory requests & limits (if metrics-server installed)metrics.k8s.io API

Chapter 10 CBA Quick Win: The K8s plugin connects clusters via kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods in app-config.yaml. Entities link via backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation. Authentication uses service account tokens (stored as env vars). The plugin shows live data — no caching by default. Backstage needs get/list/watch RBAC — it's read-only.

10.5 Deploying anihpj to Kubernetes — Complete Walkthrough

Dockerfile for anihpj/jobpost Django App
  FROM python:3.11-slim
  ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1
  ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
  WORKDIR /app
  RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y gcc libpq-dev && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
  COPY requirements.txt .
  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
  COPY . .
  RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
  EXPOSE 8000
  CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"]
Kubernetes Deployment for jobpost
  apiVersion: apps/v1
  kind: Deployment
  metadata:
    name: jobpost
    namespace: anihpj-prod
    labels:
      app: jobpost
  spec:
    replicas: 3
    selector:
      matchLabels:
        app: jobpost
    template:
      metadata:
        labels:
          app: jobpost
        annotations:
          backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost  # ← Links to Backstage!
      spec:
        containers:
        - name: jobpost
          image: anihpj/jobpost:latest
          ports:
          - containerPort: 8000
          env:
          - name: DATABASE_URL
            valueFrom:
              secretKeyRef:
                name: jobpost-secrets
                key: database-url
          - name: DJANGO_SECRET_KEY
            valueFrom:
              secretKeyRef:
                name: jobpost-secrets
                key: django-secret-key
          - name: DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE
            valueFrom:
              configMapKeyRef:
                name: jobpost-config
                key: DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE
          resources:
            requests:
              cpu: 250m
              memory: 256Mi
            limits:
              cpu: 500m
              memory: 512Mi
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /health/
              port: 8000
            initialDelaySeconds: 30
            periodSeconds: 10
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /ready/
              port: 8000
            initialDelaySeconds: 5
            periodSeconds: 5
  ---
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: ConfigMap
  metadata:
    name: jobpost-config
    namespace: anihpj-prod
  data:
    DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE: "anihpj.settings.production"
    ALLOWED_HOSTS: "jobpost.anihpj.com"
    LOG_LEVEL: "INFO"
  ---
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: Secret
  metadata:
    name: jobpost-secrets
    namespace: anihpj-prod
  type: Opaque
  stringData:
    database-url: "postgresql://user:pass@postgres:5432/jobpost"
    django-secret-key: "super-secret-key-change-in-production"
  ---
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: Service
  metadata:
    name: jobpost
    namespace: anihpj-prod
  spec:
    selector:
      app: jobpost
    ports:
    - port: 8000
      targetPort: 8000
    type: ClusterIP

📝 Chapter 10 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What does the Kubernetes plugin in Backstage do?
Show Answer
Answer

The K8s plugin connects Backstage to your Kubernetes clusters and displays live resource status on entity pages. It shows Deployments (replicas, status), Pods (health, logs, events), Services (endpoints, ports), and Ingresses (URLs). Data is fetched live from the K8s API each time you view the tab.

Q2
Where do you configure K8s cluster connection details for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

In app-config.yaml under the kubernetes section. Configure clusterLocatorMethods with cluster name, API server URL, authentication method (serviceAccount), and token. All secrets should use ${ENV_VAR} syntax.

Q3
What are the 3 ways to link a Catalog entity to K8s resources?
Show Answer
Answer

1. backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost — Match by Deployment name
2. backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: anihpj-prod — Show all resources in namespace
3. backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selector: app=jobpost — Match by K8s labels

Q4
What authentication method does Backstage use to connect to K8s clusters?
Show Answer
Answer

Service Account tokens are the most common method. You create a ServiceAccount in the K8s cluster with appropriate RBAC permissions, get its token, and pass it to Backstage via serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_SA_TOKEN}. OIDC is also supported as an alternative.

Q5
Write the complete app-config.yaml kubernetes section to connect a cluster named "anihpj-prod".
Show Answer
Answer
kubernetes:
  serviceLocatorMethod:
    type: 'multiTenant'
  clusterLocatorMethods:
    - type: 'config'
      clusters:
        - name: 'anihpj-prod'
          url: 'https://k8s-api.anihpj.com'
          authProvider: 'serviceAccount'
          serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_SA_TOKEN}
Q6
You deployed jobpost to K8s but the entity page shows no K8s tab. What are 3 things to check?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Is the K8s plugin installed? (@backstage/plugin-kubernetes in frontend + backend)
2. Is the annotation present? (backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost in catalog-info.yaml)
3. Is the cluster config correct? (URL, auth token, skipTLSVerify if self-signed cert)

Q7
Where does Backstage get K8s resource data — from a cache or live?
Show Answer
Answer

Live — the K8s plugin calls the K8s API server directly each time you view the entity's K8s tab. There's no built-in caching by default. This means the data is always current but also means Backstage needs network connectivity to the K8s API server.

Q8
What K8s RBAC permissions does Backstage need to view resources?
Show Answer
Answer

At minimum: get, list, and watch on Deployments, Pods, Services, Ingresses, and ReplicaSets. Create a ClusterRole with these permissions and bind it to the ServiceAccount that Backstage uses. This is read-only — Backstage never modifies K8s resources.

Q9
What does the multiTenant serviceLocatorMethod do?
Show Answer
Answer

multiTenant means Backstage looks for K8s resources in all namespaces that match the entity's annotations. The alternative is singleTenant which restricts to a single configured namespace. Multi-tenant is the most common configuration for organizations running multiple teams/services in different namespaces.

Q10
You have 2 K8s clusters (prod and staging). How do you configure Backstage to show resources from both?
Show Answer
Answer

Add both clusters to the clusters array in kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods[0].clusters. Each cluster gets its own name, URL, and auth token. Backstage queries ALL configured clusters and merges the results on the entity page. Example:
clusters:
- name: 'anihpj-prod'
url: 'https://k8s-prod.anihpj.com'
- name: 'anihpj-staging'
url: 'https://k8s-staging.anihpj.com'

📊 Chapter 10 — Visual Summary: Kubernetes + Catalog

☸️K8s PluginShows live Deployments, Pods, Services, Ingresses on entity pages.
⚙️app-configkubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods[].clusters[]. URL, auth, token.
🔗3 Link Methodskubernetes-id (name), kubernetes-namespace, kubernetes-label-selector.
🔐AuthServiceAccount token (most common) or OIDC. RBAC: get, list, watch.
📡Live DataNo caching. Queries K8s API directly on each page view. Always current.
🐳DockerfileMulti-stage: python:3.11-slim → pip install → gunicorn. 8000.
📋DeploymentK8s Deployment + Service + Ingress. Replicas, labels, env vars from Secrets.
🏢Multi-ClusterMultiple clusters in clusters array. Backstage queries all, merges results.

🚀 Part 4: Software Templates — Scaffolding Made Easy 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 11: Software Templates — Scaffolding New Projects

Software Templates are Backstage's "Create" button — they let developers scaffold new projects in minutes instead of hours. A template is a blueprint that combines user inputs, file skeletons, and automated actions to create production-ready repositories with CI/CD, K8s manifests, and Catalog registration — all from a single form.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2.5 hours 🚀 Templates 🎯 CBA 32%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the problem templates solve — "2 hours → 2 minutes"
  • ✅ Master template anatomy: apiVersion, parameters, steps, actions
  • ✅ Define user input forms with parameters, validation, and UI pickers
  • ✅ Use built-in actions: fetch, publish, register
  • ✅ Integrate CI/CD pipelines into generated projects
  • ✅ Build a complete Docker build pipeline for anihpj
🎓 CBA Relevance
Customizing Backstage 32%
Templates Are the #1 CBA Topic (32% of Exam)

The CBA exam heavily tests Software Templates — knowing template YAML structure, parameter types, action sequences, and how templates integrate with the Catalog. You'll need to identify correct vs incorrect template YAML, understand the execution flow, and know how to register templates so they appear in the "Create" menu.

11.1 What are Software Templates? — The 2-Hour vs 2-Minute Problem

Templates — Before vs After Backstage
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ WITHOUT TEMPLATES (Manual Scaffolding)                   │
  │  1. Find a similar repo to copy from... 20 min               │
  │  2. Clone it and delete what you don't need... 15 min        │
  │  3. Rename everything (files, packages, configs)... 30 min   │
  │  4. Update CI/CD configs... 20 min                            │
  │  5. Create repo in GitHub and push... 10 min                  │
  │  6. Set up Docker/K8s manifests... 25 min                     │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────                 │
  │  TOTAL: ~2 HOURS of tedious manual work                       │
  │                                                               │
  │  ✅ WITH BACKSTAGE TEMPLATES (Automated Scaffolding)          │
  │  1. Click "Create" → Choose Template → Fill Form → Done      │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────                 │
  │  TOTAL: ~2 MINUTES — everything automated!                   │
  │  • GitHub repo created automatically                         │
  │  • CI/CD pipeline pre-configured                             │
  │  • K8s manifests generated                                   │
  │  • Catalog entity registered automatically                   │
  │  • TechDocs skeleton ready to go                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 Template = Blueprint + Parameters + Actions

A template has 3 parts: Blueprint (the skeleton files — Dockerfile, CI config, K8s YAML), Parameters (user input form — service name, owner, description), and Actions (what happens — fetch skeleton, publish to GitHub, register in Catalog). Together they automate the entire project creation process.

11.2 Template Anatomy — Complete template.yaml

Complete template.yaml for a Django Microservice
  apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/v1beta3
  kind: Template
  metadata:
    name: django-service
    title: Django Microservice
    description: Scaffold a new Django service for the anihpj ecosystem
    tags: [django, python, microservice, recommended]
  spec:
    owner: team-platform
    type: service

    # ── USER INPUTS (the form developers fill in) ──
    parameters:
      - title: Service Details
        required: [name, owner]
        properties:
          name:
            title: Service Name
            type: string
            description: Unique name for your service
            pattern: '^[a-z0-9-]+$'
            ui:autofocus: true
          description:
            title: Description
            type: string
            description: What does this service do?
          owner:
            title: Owner
            type: string
            ui:field: OwnerPicker
            ui:options:
              allowedKinds: [Group]

    # ── ACTIONS TO EXECUTE ──
    steps:
      - id: fetch-base
        name: Fetch Template Skeleton
        action: fetch:template
        input:
          url: ./skeleton
          values:
            name: ${{ parameters.name }}
            description: ${{ parameters.description }}
            owner: ${{ parameters.owner }}

      - id: publish
        name: Publish to GitHub
        action: publish:github
        input:
          allowedHosts: ['github.com']
          description: ${{ parameters.description }}
          repoUrl: github.com?owner=${{ parameters.owner }}&repo=${{ parameters.name }}
          defaultBranch: main

      - id: register
        name: Register in Catalog
        action: catalog:register
        input:
          repoContentsUrl: ${{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }}
          catalogInfoPath: /catalog-info.yaml

11.3 Template Parameters — The User Input Form

Parameter TypeExampleUI Renders AsValidation
stringService nameText input fieldpattern, minLength, maxLength
numberPort numberNumber inputminimum, maximum
booleanEnable monitoringCheckbox toggle—
arrayTags listMulti-select / tag inputitems, minItems, uniqueItems
objectNested configGrouped fieldsproperties, required
💡 UI Fields — Beyond Basic Inputs

Backstage provides special UI fields for common inputs: OwnerPicker (select from Users/Groups), RepoUrlPicker (select GitHub org/repo), EntityPicker (select from Catalog entities). These provide auto-complete and validation out of the box.

11.4 Template Actions — What Happens When You Click "Create"

Template Execution Flow
  👤 User fills form
       │
       ▼
  📝 Parameters collected (name, owner, description)
       │
       ▼
  ⬇️ Step 1: fetch:template → Copy skeleton files, replace placeholders
       │
       ▼
  🔧 Step 2: (optional) Custom actions → Run cookiecutter, process templates
       │
       ▼
  🚀 Step 3: publish:github → Create GitHub repo, push code
       │
       ▼
  📦 Step 4: catalog:register → Register new entity in Catalog
       │
       ▼
  ✅ Done! New service visible in Backstage Catalog!

fetch:template / fetch:plain

fetch:template copies a local ./skeleton directory with placeholder replacement (${{ values.name }}). fetch:plain copies files from a URL without placeholder processing. Use fetch:template when you need customized files; use fetch:plain for static files like license templates.

publish:github / publish:gitlab

Creates a new repository on GitHub/GitLab, pushes the skeleton code, and sets the default branch. Requires the GitHub/GitLab integration token configured in app-config.yaml. The repoUrl parameter uses query string format: github.com?owner=ORG&repo=NAME.

catalog:register

Registers the newly created repository in the Backstage Catalog by reading its catalog-info.yaml. This makes the new service immediately discoverable. The action reads repoContentsUrl from the publish step's output.

Custom Actions

You can build custom actions for organization-specific needs — e.g., creating a Jira ticket, sending Slack notifications, provisioning cloud resources. Custom actions use createTemplateAction() from @backstage/plugin-scaffolder-backend.

11.5 CI/CD Pipeline Integration — From Template to Production

End-to-End Flow: Template → Code → Docker → K8s
  🚀 BACKSTAGE CREATE          📦 GITHUB REPO              🔄 CI/CD PIPELINE
  ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Template executed │───▶│ Repo created     │───▶│ 1. Run tests     │
  │ Skeleton copied   │    │ catalog-info.yaml│    │ 2. Docker build  │
  │ Placeholders done │    │ Dockerfile       │    │ 3. Push image    │
  │ Pushed to GitHub  │    │ CI/CD workflow   │    │ 4. Deploy to K8s │
  └──────────────────┘    │ K8s manifests    │    │ 5. Register Cat. │
                           └──────────────────┘    └────────┬─────────┘
                                                            │
                                                            ▼
  🖥️ BACKSTAGE STATUS                          ☸️ KUBERNETES CLUSTER
  ┌──────────────────┐                    ┌──────────────────┐
  │ K8s tab: 3/3 pods│◀───────────────────│ Deployment: v1.2 │
  │ CI/CD: Build #42 │                    │ Service: :8000   │
  │ TechDocs: live!  │                    │ Ingress: URL     │
  └──────────────────┘                    └──────────────────┘

  ⏱️ Total: ~2 min (template) + ~5 min (CI/CD) = 7 min to production!

11.6 Docker Build Pipeline for anihpj/jobpost — Full End-to-End Flow

Real Project Structure — What Developers Create
  anihpj/
  ├── jobpost/              ← Django application code
  │   ├── models.py         ← Skills, User, JobPost, Location, Author
  │   ├── views.py          ← API endpoints (REST + GraphQL)
  │   └── urls.py           ← /api/jobs/, /api/skills/, /api/authors/
  ├── Dockerfile            ← Container definition (multi-stage)
  ├── requirements.txt      ← Python dependencies (Django, gunicorn, psycopg2)
  └── catalog-info.yaml     ← Backstage entity registration
Complete CI/CD Pipeline — 4 Stages from Git Push to Production
  STAGE 1: TEST (Quality Gate)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ $ pip install -r requirements.txt    │
  │ $ python manage.py test              │
  │ $ flake8 . && black --check .        │
  │ ✅ 42 tests passed, linting clean    │
  └──────────────────┬───────────────────┘
                     │
                     ▼
  STAGE 2: BUILD DOCKER IMAGE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ $ docker build -t anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 .        │
  │ $ docker tag anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 \             │
  │     registry.anihpj.com/jobpost:v1.2.3            │
  │ $ docker push registry.anihpj.com/jobpost:v1.2.3 │
  │ ✅ Image pushed to container registry            │
  └──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘
                     │
                     ▼
  STAGE 3: DEPLOY TO KUBERNETES
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ $ kubectl apply -f k8s/              │
  │ $ kubectl rollout status deploy/jobpost        │
  │ ✅ Deployment "jobpost" rolled out             │
  │ • 3 replicas running on anihpj-prod            │
  │ • Service: jobpost-svc :8000                   │
  │ • Ingress: jobpost.anihpj.com                  │
  └──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘
                     │
                     ▼
  STAGE 4: REGISTER IN BACKSTAGE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ $ curl -X POST :7007/api/catalog/entities        │
  │ ✅ Entity "jobpost" updated in Catalog           │
  │ • K8s tab shows 3/3 pods running                │
  │ • CI/CD card: "Build #142 ✅ 2 min ago"         │
  │ • TechDocs auto-published from /docs/           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 7 Minutes from Git Push to Production

With a well-configured template + CI/CD pipeline, the flow is: 2 min (template execution) + 5 min (CI/CD pipeline) = 7 minutes from clicking "Create" to seeing your service live in Backstage with full K8s deployment status. This is the power of Backstage templates.

Real-Life: Creating a Template — Full 5-Step Workflow

Step-by-Step: From Zero to Template in Production
  # Step 1: Create the template directory structure
  $ mkdir -p templates/django-service/skeleton

  # Step 2: Write template.yaml and skeleton files
  $ vim templates/django-service/template.yaml
  $ vim templates/django-service/skeleton/catalog-info.yaml
  $ vim templates/django-service/skeleton/Dockerfile
  $ vim templates/django-service/skeleton/.github/workflows/ci.yml

  # Step 3: Register template in app-config.yaml
  scaffolder:
    templateLocations:
      - type: file
        target: ./templates/django-service/template.yaml

  # Step 4: Start Backstage and test the template
  $ yarn dev
  → Click CREATE → Choose "Django Microservice"
  → Fill form: Service Name, Owner, Description
  → Click CREATE again

  # Step 5: Verify the output
  Output:
  ✅ Fetch template skeleton... Done
  ✅ Publish to GitHub... Done (repo created: github.com/anihpj/my-service)
  ✅ Register in Catalog... Done (entity: Component:my-service)
  🎉 New service is live in the Catalog!
  🔗 GitHub: https://github.com/anihpj/my-service
  📋 Catalog: http://localhost:3000/catalog/default/component/my-service

📝 Chapter 11 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What problem do Software Templates solve? Describe the before/after.
Show Answer
Answer

Templates solve the manual project scaffolding problem. Before: developers spend ~2 hours copy-pasting from existing repos, renaming files, updating configs, creating repos, and setting up CI/CD. After: 2 minutes — click Create → fill form → everything is automated. Templates standardize project setup across the organization.

Q2
What are the 3 essential parts of a template.yaml?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Parameters — User input form (name, owner, description, etc.)
2. Steps — Ordered list of actions to execute
3. Actions — What each step does (fetch:template, publish:github, catalog:register)

Q3
What is the correct apiVersion for a Software Template?
Show Answer
Answer

apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/v1beta3 — Note: it's scaffolder.backstage.io, NOT backstage.io. The kind is Template. This is different from catalog entities which use backstage.io/v1alpha1.

Q4
What does the fetch:template action do?
Show Answer
Answer

fetch:template copies files from a local ./skeleton directory and replaces placeholders like ${{ values.name }} with actual parameter values. It's the first step in most templates — it creates the working directory with all boilerplate files customized for the new project.

Q5
How does publish:github create a repository?
Show Answer
Answer

publish:github uses the GitHub integration token (configured in app-config.yaml under integrations.github) to call the GitHub API and create a new repository. The repoUrl input uses query string format: github.com?owner=ORG&repo=NAME. It then pushes the scaffolded code to the new repo.

Q6
What does catalog:register do in a template?
Show Answer
Answer

catalog:register reads the catalog-info.yaml from the newly created repository and registers it in the Backstage Catalog. This makes the new service immediately discoverable. It uses ${{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }} to reference the output from a previous publish step.

Q7
What is the OwnerPicker UI field and when would you use it?
Show Answer
Answer

OwnerPicker is a special UI field that provides an auto-complete dropdown for selecting Users or Groups from the Catalog. Use it for the owner parameter: ui:field: OwnerPicker with ui:options: allowedKinds: [Group]. It validates that the selected owner actually exists.

Q8
How do you reference a parameter value inside a template skeleton file?
Show Answer
Answer

Use ${{ values.PARAM_NAME }} syntax. Example: in catalog-info.yaml inside the skeleton: name: ${{ values.name }}. The fetch:template action replaces these placeholders with actual values. You can also reference step outputs: ${{ steps.STEP_ID.output.OUTPUT_NAME }}.

Q9
What are the 4 stages of the CI/CD pipeline for anihpj/jobpost?
Show Answer
Answer

1. TEST: pip install → pytest → linting
2. BUILD: docker build → tag → push to registry
3. DEPLOY: kubectl apply → rollout status
4. REGISTER: POST /api/catalog/entities to update Backstage

Q10
A developer uses your template but the new service doesn't appear in the Catalog. What 3 things could be wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

1. The catalog:register step might be missing or failed — check template execution logs
2. The catalog-info.yaml in the skeleton might have errors — validate with yarn backstage-cli validate-entity
3. The catalog location might not be configured — ensure the repo URL is registered in catalog.locations or the register step completed successfully

📊 Chapter 11 — Visual Summary: Software Templates

🚀2hr → 2minTemplates reduce project setup from hours to minutes. Standardized, automated.
📝template.yamlapiVersion, kind:Template, metadata, spec with parameters + steps.
📋Parametersstring, number, boolean, array, object. Special UI fields: OwnerPicker.
⬇️fetch:templateCopy skeleton files. Replace ${{ values.NAME }} placeholders.
🚀publish:githubCreate GitHub repo via API. Push scaffolded code. Set default branch.
📦catalog:registerRegister new entity from catalog-info.yaml. Discoverable immediately.
🔄CI/CD PipelineTest → Docker Build → K8s Deploy → Register. ~7 min total.
🐳Docker Builddocker build → tag → push to registry. Multi-stage builds for efficiency.
🎯CBA 32%Templates are the HIGHEST weighted CBA domain. Master this!

Chapter 12: Template Actions & Customization

Actions are the engine behind every template — they do the actual work of creating repositories, registering entities, and transforming files. This chapter covers every built-in action, how to structure your template skeleton, and how to build custom actions for your organization's unique needs.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours ⚡ Actions 🎯 CBA 32%

12.1 Built-in Actions — Complete Reference

ActionWhat It DoesKey InputsKey Outputs
fetch:templateCopy skeleton files with placeholder replacementurl, values—
fetch:plainCopy files from URL (no placeholders)url, targetPath—
fetch:cookiecutterUse Cookiecutter templatesurl, values—
publish:githubCreate GitHub repo + push coderepoUrl, defaultBranchrepoContentsUrl, repoUrl
publish:gitlabCreate GitLab repo + push coderepoUrlrepoContentsUrl
publish:bitbucketCreate Bitbucket reporepoUrlrepoContentsUrl
publish:azureCreate Azure DevOps reporepoUrlrepoContentsUrl
catalog:registerRegister entity in CatalogrepoContentsUrl, catalogInfoPathentityRef
catalog:writeWrite catalog entity directlyentities (YAML array)entityRefs
fs:deleteDelete files during template runfiles (array of paths)—
debug:waitPause execution (for testing)seconds—
roadiehq:utils:fs:replaceFind and replace text in filesfiles, find, replace—

12.2 The Template Skeleton — ./skeleton Directory

Skeleton Directory Structure for Django Template
  templates/django-service/
  ├── template.yaml              ← Template definition
  └── skeleton/                  ← Files copied to new project
      ├── catalog-info.yaml      ← Backstage entity (with ${{ values.xxx }})
      ├── Dockerfile             ← Container definition
      ├── requirements.txt       ← Python dependencies
      ├── manage.py              ← Django entry point
      ├── .github/
      │   └── workflows/
      │       └── ci.yml         ← CI/CD pipeline
      ├── k8s/
      │   ├── deployment.yaml    ← K8s deployment manifest
      │   └── service.yaml       ← K8s service manifest
      └── docs/
          ├── index.md           ← TechDocs home page
          └── mkdocs.yml         ← MkDocs configuration

12.3 Custom Template Actions

When built-in actions aren't enough, you can create custom actions using createTemplateAction(). Common use cases: creating Jira tickets, sending Slack notifications, provisioning cloud resources, or running security scans.

Custom Action Example — Create Jira Ticket
  // packages/backend/src/plugins/scaffolder/actions/jira.ts
  import { createTemplateAction } from '@backstage/plugin-scaffolder-backend';

  export const createJiraTicketAction = () => {
    return createTemplateAction({
      id: 'anihpj:jira:create-ticket',
      description: 'Creates a Jira ticket for new service tracking',
      schema: {
        input: {
          type: 'object',
          required: ['project', 'summary'],
          properties: {
            project: { type: 'string', title: 'Jira Project Key' },
            summary: { type: 'string', title: 'Ticket Summary' },
          },
        },
      },
      async handler(ctx) {
        const { project, summary } = ctx.input;
        // Call Jira API...
        ctx.logger.info(`Created Jira ticket in ${project}`);
      },
    });
  };

12.4 Template Testing & Debugging

Debug Commands
  # 1. Validate template syntax
  $ yarn backstage-cli validate-template ./templates/django-service/template.yaml

  # 2. Run template in debug mode (shows detailed logs)
  $ yarn dev  # Then click Create → your template → check terminal output

  # 3. Test individual actions
  $ yarn backstage-cli test-action fetch:template

  # 4. Common errors:
  # ❌ "Step X failed: input validation error" → Check required parameters
  # ❌ "publish:github failed: 401" → Check GITHUB_TOKEN env var
  # ❌ "catalog:register: entity exists" → Entity already registered
  # ❌ "fetch:template: directory not found" → Check ./skeleton path

📝 Chapter 12 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
Name 5 built-in template actions and what each does.
Show Answer
Answer

1. fetch:template — Copy skeleton with placeholders
2. publish:github — Create GitHub repo + push code
3. catalog:register — Register in Catalog
4. fetch:plain — Copy files from URL
5. fs:delete — Delete files during template run

Q2
What goes inside the ./skeleton directory?
Show Answer
Answer

All boilerplate files for the new project: catalog-info.yaml, Dockerfile, CI/CD workflow (.github/workflows/ci.yml), K8s manifests (k8s/deployment.yaml), TechDocs files (docs/index.md, mkdocs.yml), and source code stubs. Files use ${{ values.PARAM }} for customization.

Q3
How do you create a custom template action?
Show Answer
Answer

Use createTemplateAction() from @backstage/plugin-scaffolder-backend. Define: (1) a unique id, (2) input schema with JSON Schema, (3) an async handler function. Register the action in packages/backend/src/plugins/scaffolder.ts.

Q4
How do you validate a template before using it?
Show Answer
Answer

Run yarn backstage-cli validate-template ./path/to/template.yaml. This checks YAML syntax, verifies required fields (apiVersion, kind, spec.parameters, spec.steps), and validates action definitions. Always validate before committing template changes.

Q5
What's the difference between fetch:template and fetch:plain?
Show Answer
Answer

fetch:template copies local skeleton files AND replaces ${{ values.X }} placeholders with parameter values. fetch:plain copies files from a URL WITHOUT any placeholder processing. Use fetch:template for customizable boilerplate; use fetch:plain for static files.

Q6
How do you pass data between template steps?
Show Answer
Answer

Using ${{ steps.STEP_ID.output.OUTPUT_NAME }}. For example, after a publish:github step with id "publish", you can reference its output: ${{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }}. This is how catalog:register knows which repo to register.

Q7
A template fails with "publish:github failed: 401". What's wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

The GitHub token is missing, expired, or lacks permissions. Check: (1) Is ${GITHUB_TOKEN} set as an environment variable? (2) Does the token have repo and workflow scopes? (3) Is the token configured in integrations.github in app-config.yaml?

Q8
Where should custom template actions be registered in the backend?
Show Answer
Answer

In packages/backend/src/plugins/scaffolder.ts. Import your custom action and add it to the scaffolder actions array. Example: scaffolderActions.push(createJiraTicketAction());. Actions must be registered before templates that use them.

Q9
What does publish:github output that catalog:register needs?
Show Answer
Answer

publish:github outputs repoContentsUrl (URL to fetch repo contents) and repoUrl (the clone URL). catalog:register uses repoContentsUrl to read the catalog-info.yaml from the newly created repository. The output is referenced as ${{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }}.

Q10
Your template works locally but fails on a colleague's machine. What are 3 possible causes?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Missing env vars — GITHUB_TOKEN or other secrets not set on their machine
2. Different Backstage version — template uses actions/features from a newer version
3. Skeleton path issue — relative paths in template.yaml may resolve differently depending on working directory. Always use paths relative to template.yaml location.

📊 Chapter 12 — Visual Summary: Actions & Customization

⚡11 Built-in Actionsfetch, publish (4 providers), catalog, fs, debug. All in @backstage/plugin-scaffolder-backend.
📂./skeletonBoilerplate files: catalog-info.yaml, Dockerfile, CI/CD, K8s, TechDocs.
🔧Custom ActionscreateTemplateAction(). Jira, Slack, cloud provisioning, security scans.
🐛Debuggingvalidate-template, yarn dev logs, test-action. Check token, paths, syntax.
🔄Step Outputs${{ steps.ID.output.NAME }} passes data between steps in sequence.
🎯CBA 32%Know all built-in actions. Custom actions are advanced but testable.

Now let's build real templates for the anihpj ecosystem — a Django microservice, a React frontend, a database migration job, and an infrastructure-as-code template. Each template is production-ready with CI/CD, K8s manifests, and Catalog registration built in.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🏗️ Practical 🎯 CBA 32%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Build a complete Django microservice template
  • ✅ Build a React frontend template
  • ✅ Build a database migration job template
  • ✅ Build an infrastructure-as-code template
  • ✅ Register templates and manage their lifecycle

13.1 Template: New Django Microservice

Django Service Template — Key Parameters & Skeleton
  Parameters: name, description, owner, pythonVersion, includeDocker
  Skeleton includes:
    ├── catalog-info.yaml     ← Pre-configured for jobpost-style projects
    ├── Dockerfile            ← Multi-stage Python 3.11 build
    ├── requirements.txt      ← Django, gunicorn, psycopg2
    ├── manage.py             ← Django entry point
    ├── .github/workflows/    ← CI: test → docker build → deploy
    └── k8s/                  ← Deployment + Service + ConfigMap

13.2 Template: New React Frontend

Scaffolds a React app with Vite + Material UI and pre-configured CI/CD pipeline. The template creates: a catalog-info.yaml with frontend-specific annotations, a multi-stage Dockerfile (build with Node, serve with Nginx), GitHub Actions workflow (lint → test → build → deploy), K8s Deployment + Service manifests, and a TechDocs skeleton. Parameters include: app name, description, owner, and whether to include Storybook for component documentation.

💡 Frontend Template Key Difference

Unlike the Django template, the React template uses Nginx as the production server (not Node.js directly). The Dockerfile copies the Vite build output to Nginx's html directory. The K8s Service exposes port 80 (Nginx) instead of 8000 (gunicorn).

13.3 Template: Database Migration Job

Scaffolds a Kubernetes Job for running Django database migrations. Unlike a long-running service, this is a one-time execution that runs to completion. The template includes: a Dockerfile with migration scripts, a K8s Job manifest (with backoffLimit and ttlSecondsAfterFinished), a CronJob variant for scheduled migrations, environment variable configuration for database credentials, and a catalog-info.yaml that registers it as a Component of type "job". This is perfect for CI/CD pipelines that need to run migrations before deploying new versions.

13.4 Template: Infrastructure as Code

Scaffolds Terraform configuration for provisioning cloud resources with best practices built in. The template generates: main.tf (resource definitions for compute, networking, database), variables.tf (parameterized inputs with descriptions and defaults), outputs.tf (connection strings, IPs, endpoints), backend.tf (remote state configuration using S3/GCS/Azure Blob), providers.tf (AWS/GCP/Azure provider configuration), and a catalog-info.yaml registered as a Resource. The CI pipeline includes terraform fmt, terraform validate, and terraform plan steps.

13.5 Registering Templates

Registering Templates in app-config.yaml
  scaffolder:
    templateLocations:
      - type: file
        target: ./templates/django-service/template.yaml
      - type: file
        target: ./templates/react-frontend/template.yaml
      - type: file
        target: ./templates/db-migration/template.yaml
      - type: file
        target: ./templates/infra-as-code/template.yaml

Template Categories

Tags in metadata control how templates appear: recommended (pinned to top), django, python, react (filterable categories).

Recommended Templates

Add recommended to tags to pin templates to the top of the Create page. Best for your most-used, battle-tested templates.

Template Lifecycle

Manage templates like any entity: use lifecycle: production for stable templates, lifecycle: experimental for new ones, and lifecycle: deprecated when retiring old templates.

📝 Chapter 13 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What files should a Django microservice template skeleton include?
Show Answer
Answer

catalog-info.yaml, Dockerfile, requirements.txt, manage.py, .github/workflows/ci.yml, k8s/deployment.yaml, k8s/service.yaml, docs/index.md. Each file uses ${{ values.XXX }} for customization.

Q2
How do templates appear in the Backstage UI?
Show Answer
Answer

Templates appear on the Create page (the + button in the sidebar). They're listed as cards with title, description, and tags. Templates tagged recommended appear first. Users click a template, fill the form, and click Create to execute.

Q3
Where do you configure template locations?
Show Answer
Answer

In app-config.yaml under scaffolder.templateLocations. Each location has a type (file or url) and target (path to template.yaml). Templates can also be registered as Catalog entities of kind Template.

Q4
How does the React frontend template differ from the Django template?
Show Answer
Answer

The React template uses Vite for building and Nginx for serving (not gunicorn). The Dockerfile copies the Vite build output to Nginx's html directory. K8s Service exposes port 80 instead of 8000. The CI pipeline runs npm commands (lint, test, build) instead of pip/pytest.

Q5
What's the purpose of a database migration job template?
Show Answer
Answer

It scaffolds a Kubernetes Job that runs Django database migrations. Unlike a service (which runs continuously), a Job runs once to completion. The template includes a CronJob variant for scheduled migrations. This ensures migrations run reliably in CI/CD pipelines before new versions deploy.

Q6
What Terraform files does the infrastructure-as-code template generate?
Show Answer
Answer

main.tf (resources), variables.tf (inputs with defaults), outputs.tf (connection strings, IPs), backend.tf (remote state config), providers.tf (AWS/GCP/Azure provider). The CI pipeline runs terraform fmt, terraform validate, and terraform plan.

Q7
What does the recommended tag do for templates?
Show Answer
Answer

Templates tagged recommended are pinned to the top of the Create page. They appear first before other templates. Use this tag for your most-used, battle-tested templates that every developer should see first. It's an organizational best practice.

Q8
How do you manage template lifecycle (experimental → production → deprecated)?
Show Answer
Answer

Add lifecycle: experimental to template metadata for new/testing templates. Change to lifecycle: production when stable. Use lifecycle: deprecated when retiring. Deprecated templates still appear but with a warning — they should be removed after all users have migrated.

Q9
You want to add a template that creates a new microservice AND provisions its database. How would you combine actions?
Show Answer
Answer

Use multiple steps in sequence: (1) fetch:template to copy the service skeleton, (2) a custom action to call your cloud API and provision a database, (3) publish:github to create the repo, (4) another custom action to create the database connection string as a K8s Secret, (5) catalog:register. Pass the database connection details between steps via ${{ steps.X.output.Y }}.

Q10
How would you test a new template before making it available to all developers?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Validate with yarn backstage-cli validate-template
2. Tag it experimental (not recommended) initially
3. Run it manually in dev mode and check the terminal logs
4. Verify the output: GitHub repo created, files correct, Catalog entity registered
5. Have a colleague test it on their machine
6. After successful testing, change to lifecycle: production and add recommended tag

📖 Part 5: TechDocs — Documentation-as-Code 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 14: TechDocs — Documentation That Lives With Your Code

TechDocs is Backstage's documentation-as-code engine. Your docs live in the same repo as your code, written in Markdown, and are auto-published to Backstage whenever you push. No more stale wikis, scattered Confluence pages, or out-of-date READMEs — TechDocs makes documentation a first-class citizen of your engineering workflow.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 📖 Docs 🎯 CBA 32%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the TechDocs pipeline — write → build → publish → display
  • ✅ Configure mkdocs.yml for navigation and extensions
  • ✅ Master TechDocs configuration in app-config.yaml (builder, generator, publisher)
  • ✅ Write effective docs with admonitions, code blocks, and Mermaid diagrams
  • ✅ Choose the right storage strategy for production
  • ✅ Follow documentation best practices that keep docs useful and current
🎓 CBA Relevance
Customizing Backstage 32%
TechDocs Is a Core Customizing Topic (CBA 32%)

The CBA exam tests your understanding of the TechDocs pipeline — builder, generator, publisher. You'll need to know: (1) Which annotation links an entity to its docs (backstage.io/techdocs-ref), (2) The difference between local and cloud storage for production, (3) How mkdocs.yml controls navigation and appearance. TechDocs is one of Backstage's 4 pillars — expect multiple exam questions.

14.1 What are TechDocs? — Documentation-as-Code

TechDocs follows the docs-as-code philosophy: documentation lives in the same Git repository as your source code, written in Markdown (.md) or MDX (.mdx), and is automatically built and published every time you push. Under the hood, TechDocs uses MkDocs — a Python-based static site generator that converts Markdown files into beautiful, searchable HTML documentation. MDX support allows embedding React components directly in your docs for interactive examples.

The TechDocs Pipeline — From Markdown to Beautiful Docs
  📝 Developer writes docs in Markdown
       │
       ▼
  📦 Docs live in same repo as code (/docs/)
       │  index.md, architecture.md, api-reference.md
       ▼
  🚀 Git push triggers TechDocs build
       │
       ▼
  ⚙️ TechDocs Generator (MkDocs)
       │  • Converts Markdown → Static HTML
       │  • Applies MkDocs theme & navigation
       │  • Creates search index
       │  • Renders Mermaid diagrams
       ▼
  📤 TechDocs Publisher
       │  • Publishes to storage (local/GCS/S3/Azure)
       │
       ▼
  🖥️ Backstage TechDocs Reader
          • Displays docs on entity page's "Docs" tab
          • Full-text search across all docs
          • Navigation sidebar from mkdocs.yml

14.2 Setting Up TechDocs

Complete mkdocs.yml for jobpost Documentation
  # mkdocs.yml — Place at repo root, next to /docs/ folder
  site_name: 'jobpost Documentation'
  site_description: 'Job posting and recruitment application — Developer Docs'
  repo_url: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost

  nav:                                  # ← Controls the sidebar navigation
    - Home: index.md
    - Architecture: architecture.md
    - 'API Reference': api-reference.md
    - 'Getting Started': getting-started.md
    - Deployment: deployment.md
    - Contributing: contributing.md

  plugins:
    - techdocs-core                     # ← Required TechDocs plugin

  markdown_extensions:
    - admonition                        # ← !!! note, !!! warning, !!! tip
    - codehilite                        # ← Syntax highlighting
    - pymdownx.superfences:             # ← Mermaid diagram support
        custom_fences:
          - name: mermaid
            class: mermaid
            format: !!python/name:pymdownx.superfences.fence_div_format
    - toc:                              # ← Auto-generate table of contents
        permalink: true

14.3 TechDocs Configuration in app-config.yaml

app-config.yaml — TechDocs Section
  techdocs:
    builder: 'local'              # ← 'local' for dev, 'external' for CI/CD
    generator:
      runIn: 'local'              # ← 'local' (needs Python+MkDocs) or 'docker'
      dockerImage: 'spotify/techdocs'  # ← Docker image for MkDocs
    publisher:
      type: 'local'               # ← 'local' | 'googleGcs' | 'awsS3' | 'azureBlobStorage'
      # For GCS:
      # type: 'googleGcs'
      # googleGcs:
      #   bucketName: 'anihpj-techdocs'
      #   credentials: ${GCS_CREDENTIALS}

Builder (local | external)

local: Backstage builds docs on-demand when viewing. Best for development. external: Docs are pre-built by CI/CD pipeline. Best for production — build in CI, publish to cloud storage, Backstage just serves.

Generator (local | docker)

local: Runs MkDocs directly on the Backstage server (requires Python + MkDocs installed). docker: Runs MkDocs in a Docker container using the spotify/techdocs image — isolated, no Python needed on host, consistent builds across environments. Docker is recommended for production because you don't want to manage Python/MkDocs dependencies on your Backstage server. Configure with generator.runIn: 'docker'.

Publisher (local | GCS | S3 | Azure)

local: Stores generated HTML in ./techdocs-output/. Only works for single-instance dev. googleGcs/awsS3/azureBlobStorage: Cloud storage — required for multi-replica production deployments so all pods can access the same docs.

14.4 Writing Effective TechDocs

✅ DO❌ DON'T
Keep docs next to code (/docs/ in each repo)Put docs in a separate wiki/Confluence
Use Mermaid for diagrams (text-based, versionable)Use screenshots that go stale quickly
Structure with clear nav (mkdocs.yml nav section)One giant README with everything
Use admonitions for notes/warnings/tipsBury important warnings in paragraphs
Include code snippets with syntax highlightingDescribe code without showing it
Write for NEW developers (explain acronyms, context)Assume everyone knows the context
Update docs WITH code (same PR as code change)"I'll update docs later" (never happens)

14.5 TechDocs Storage Strategies — Dev vs Production

PublisherDevProductionNotes
local✅ Default❌ Not scalableStores in ./techdocs-output/. Single pod only.
Local (dev)✅ Simple❌No config needed. Best for local development.
googleGcs🟡 Config needed✅ RecommendedAuto-scales, cheap, CDN-ready. Set bucketName.
awsS3🟡 Config needed✅ RecommendedAuto-scales, CDN via CloudFront. Set bucketName + region.
azureBlobStorage🟡 Config needed✅ RecommendedAuto-scales, CDN-ready. Set containerName.
openStackSwift🟡 Config needed✅ For OpenStackOpenStack Swift object storage. For OpenStack-based deployments.

14.6 TechDocs Writing Best Practices

Example: Well-Written TechDocs Page
  # Job Posting API Reference

  !!! note "Prerequisites"
      You need an API key to access these endpoints.
      Contact the platform team to get one.

  ## List Jobs
  Returns a paginated list of job postings.

  **Endpoint:** `GET /api/jobs/`
  **Auth:** API Key required

  ```python
  # Example request
  import requests
  response = requests.get(
      'https://jobpost.anihpj.com/api/jobs/',
      headers={'Authorization': 'Bearer YOUR_API_KEY'}
  )
  ```

  ## System Architecture
  ```mermaid
  graph TD
      A[Browser] --> B[Nginx Ingress]
      B --> C[jobpost Service]
      C --> D[(PostgreSQL)]
      C --> E[(Redis Cache)]
  ```

  !!! warning "Rate Limiting"
      The API is rate-limited to 1000 requests per hour per API key.

📝 Chapter 14 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What does the TechDocs pipeline do? Describe the flow.
Show Answer
Answer

Developer writes Markdown → Git push triggers build → MkDocs converts to static HTML → Publisher stores HTML → Backstage displays on entity page's Docs tab. The pipeline has 3 configurable stages: builder (local/external), generator (local/docker), publisher (local/GCS/S3/Azure).

Q2
What annotation links an entity to its TechDocs?
Show Answer
Answer

backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. — This tells TechDocs to look for mkdocs.yml and /docs/ in the same directory. Alternative: url:https://docs.example.com for externally hosted docs.

Q3
What is mkdocs.yml used for?
Show Answer
Answer

mkdocs.yml configures MkDocs — the static site generator behind TechDocs. It defines: site name, navigation structure (nav section), enabled plugins (must include techdocs-core), and Markdown extensions (admonition, code highlighting, Mermaid diagrams).

Q4
What's the difference between builder: local and builder: external?
Show Answer
Answer

local: Backstage builds docs on-demand when a user views them. Best for dev. external: Docs are pre-built by CI/CD and Backstage only serves them. Best for production — faster page loads and no MkDocs dependency on Backstage server.

Q5
Why use cloud storage (GCS/S3/Azure) instead of local for production TechDocs?
Show Answer
Answer

With multiple Backstage replicas on K8s, local storage only works on one pod — other pods can't access the files. Cloud storage provides shared access for ALL pods, auto-scales, and can use CDN for faster global delivery.

Q6
What Markdown extension enables Mermaid diagrams in TechDocs?
Show Answer
Answer

pymdownx.superfences with custom fences configured for Mermaid. This allows rendering Mermaid diagrams directly from Markdown code blocks marked with ```mermaid. Diagrams are text-based and versionable — they stay in sync with code.

Q7
What are admonitions in TechDocs and how do you use them?
Show Answer
Answer

Admonitions are styled callout boxes: !!! note, !!! warning, !!! tip, !!! danger. They require the admonition Markdown extension in mkdocs.yml. Use them to highlight important information, warnings, or tips that should stand out from regular text.

Q8
Where in app-config.yaml do you configure TechDocs?
Show Answer
Answer

Under the techdocs top-level section with 3 subsections: builder (local|external), generator (runIn: local|docker), and publisher (type: local|googleGcs|awsS3|azureBlobStorage). Each publisher type has its own config (bucketName, credentials, etc).

Q9
Your TechDocs aren't appearing on the entity page. What 3 things do you check?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Annotation: Is backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. in catalog-info.yaml?
2. mkdocs.yml: Does it exist at the repo root with techdocs-core plugin?
3. TechDocs config: Is the techdocs section properly configured in app-config.yaml? Check backend logs for build errors.

Q10
What's the #1 best practice for keeping TechDocs current?
Show Answer
Answer

Update docs in the same PR as code changes. When you change an API endpoint, update the docs in the same Git commit. This ensures docs never fall behind code. Make doc updates part of your definition of done — "code change isn't complete until docs are updated."

15.1 Planning TechDocs for anihpj

anihpj Documentation Plan

15.2 Writing Architecture Documentation

Use Mermaid diagrams to show system architecture — text-based, version-controlled, and render beautifully in TechDocs. Include a system overview diagram showing services (jobpost, frontend), databases (PostgreSQL), caches (Redis), and how they connect.

architecture.md — Mermaid System Diagram
  ```mermaid
  graph TD
      A[Browser] --> B[Nginx Ingress]
      B --> C[jobpost Service :8000]
      B --> D[frontend Service :3000]
      C --> E[(PostgreSQL)]
      C --> F[(Redis Cache)]
      C --> G[External: Email API]
      D --> C
  ```

15.3 Writing API Documentation

api-reference.md — Documenting REST Endpoints
  ## Jobs API
  ### GET /api/jobs/
  Returns paginated list of job postings.

  **Query Parameters:**
  | Param | Type | Description |
  |-------|------|-------------|
  | page | int | Page number (default: 1) |
  | skills | string | Filter by skill name |

  **Response (200):**
  ```json
  {
    "count": 42,
    "results": [{
      "id": 1, "title": "Senior Developer",
      "company": "Tech Corp", "skills": ["Python", "Django"]
    }]
  }
  ```
  **Auth:** Requires API key in Authorization header.

15.4 Writing Deployment Documentation

Document the complete K8s deployment process so any developer can deploy the service. Cover environment variables, K8s manifests, scaling, rollback, and CI/CD integration.

deployment.md — Key Sections to Include
  # Deploying jobpost to Kubernetes

  ## Prerequisites
  - kubectl configured for anihpj-prod cluster
  - Docker image pushed to registry.anihpj.com

  ## Environment Variables
  | Variable | Required | Description | Example |
  |----------|----------|-------------|---------|
  | DATABASE_URL | ✅ | PostgreSQL connection | postgresql://user:pass@host:5432/db |
  | DJANGO_SECRET_KEY | ✅ | Django secret | (use: openssl rand -hex 32) |
  | ALLOWED_HOSTS | ✅ | Comma-separated hosts | jobpost.anihpj.com |

  ## Deploy
  ```bash
  kubectl apply -f k8s/deployment.yaml
  kubectl apply -f k8s/service.yaml
  kubectl apply -f k8s/ingress.yaml
  ```

  ## Scale
  ```bash
  kubectl scale deployment jobpost --replicas=5
  ```

  ## Rollback
  ```bash
  kubectl rollout undo deployment/jobpost
  ```

  • ✅ Create mkdocs.yml with nav structure and techdocs-core plugin
  • ✅ Write docs in /docs/ (index.md, architecture.md, api-reference.md, deployment.md, contributing.md)
  • ✅ Push to GitHub → TechDocs auto-builds and publishes
  • ✅ Visit jobpost entity page → Click "Docs" tab → See documentation!
  • ✅ Update docs in same PR as code changes → auto-rebuild

Part 5 Quick Wins for CBA: TechDocs uses MkDocs under the hood. The pipeline: Write Markdown → Git Push → MkDocs builds HTML → Publisher stores → Backstage displays. The annotation is backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.. For production: use Docker generator + cloud storage publisher (GCS/S3/Azure). Update docs in the SAME PR as code changes. techdocs-core plugin is REQUIRED in mkdocs.yml.

📝 Chapter 15 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What documents should you plan for a production service like jobpost?
Show Answer
Answer

Architecture (system design, data flow diagrams), API Reference (endpoints, auth, examples), Deployment Guide (K8s, env vars, scaling), Contributing Guide (local setup, code style, PR process), and an index.md as the entry point with quick links.

Q2
Why use Mermaid diagrams instead of screenshots in TechDocs?
Show Answer
Answer

Mermaid diagrams are text-based — they're version-controlled in Git, reviewable in PRs, and auto-update when the code changes. Screenshots go stale quickly, can't be diff'd in PRs, and require manual re-creation when the UI changes. Mermaid diagrams stay in sync with your code.

Q3
How do you verify TechDocs are working after setup?
Show Answer
Answer

Visit the entity page in Backstage (e.g., /catalog/default/component/jobpost), click the "Docs" tab. You should see your documentation with navigation sidebar, search bar, and properly rendered Markdown, code blocks, and diagrams. Check backend logs if docs don't appear.

Q4
Where should the mkdocs.yml file be placed relative to the repository?
Show Answer
Answer

At the repository root, next to the /docs/ folder. Backstage looks for mkdocs.yml in the same directory referenced by the backstage.io/techdocs-ref annotation. If the annotation is dir:., mkdocs.yml must be at the repo root.

Q5
What plugin must be included in mkdocs.yml for TechDocs to work?
Show Answer
Answer

techdocs-core — this is the requiredBackstage plugin for MkDocs. Without it, TechDocs won't render properly. Add it under the plugins section: plugins: [techdocs-core]. This plugin handles Backstage-specific features like the TechDocs theme and search integration.

Q6
How do you update TechDocs after making changes to your documentation?
Show Answer
Answer

Push to Git — TechDocs rebuilds automatically. If using builder: local, docs rebuild on the next page view. If using builder: external, your CI/CD pipeline rebuilds and publishes to cloud storage. No manual steps needed — it's fully automated.

Q7
What's the difference between the nav section and the actual /docs/ folder structure?
Show Answer
Answer

The nav section in mkdocs.yml defines the display order and hierarchy of pages in the sidebar. The /docs/ folder is where the actual Markdown files live. You can organize nav differently from folder structure — e.g., put deployment.md before api-reference.md in nav even if they're alphabetically reversed.

Q8
Your TechDocs show a blank page. The annotation is correct and mkdocs.yml exists. What else could be wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Generator issue: Is Python + MkDocs installed (if runIn: local) or Docker available (if runIn: docker)?
2. Publisher issue: For cloud storage, are credentials correct and accessible?
3. Build error: Check the backend logs — MkDocs may have failed to build due to Markdown syntax errors or missing files referenced in nav.

Q9
What is the repo_url setting in mkdocs.yml used for?
Show Answer
Answer

repo_url adds an "Edit on GitHub" (or GitLab/Bitbucket) link to every TechDocs page. Set it to your repository URL: repo_url: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost. This lets readers quickly navigate to the source code or suggest edits to the documentation.

Q10
You're deploying Backstage on K8s with 3 replicas. Which TechDocs publisher should you use and why?
Show Answer
Answer

Use cloud storage (googleGcs, awsS3, or azureBlobStorage). The local publisher stores files on the pod's filesystem — with 3 replicas, each pod has its own copy and they won't be in sync. Cloud storage provides a single shared location accessible by all replicas. Configure the publisher in app-config.yaml with the appropriate bucket/container and credentials.

🔌 Part 6: Plugins — Extending Backstage 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 16: Plugin Architecture — How Backstage Extends

Every feature in Backstage is a plugin — Catalog, TechDocs, Scaffolder, Search, Kubernetes are all plugins. Understanding plugin architecture is critical for the CBA exam (32% of the exam is about customizing Backstage, and plugins are the primary way to customize). With 200+ community plugins available, you can extend Backstage to do almost anything.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔌 Plugins 🎯 CBA 32%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand how plugins work — frontend, backend, and full-stack
  • ✅ Master the plugin framework: createPlugin(), createRoutableExtension(), createApiRef()
  • ✅ Explore the plugin marketplace — 200+ community plugins by category
  • ✅ Know when to use frontend-only vs backend-only vs full-stack plugins
🎓 CBA Relevance
Customizing Backstage 32%
Plugins = Customizing Backstage (32% of CBA)

The CBA exam tests your ability to understand and work with plugins. Know the difference between frontend (UI only), backend (API only), and full-stack (both) plugins. Understand how plugins register routes, access APIs, and communicate via the event bus.

16.1 What are Backstage Plugins?

Plugin Architecture — How Plugins Extend Backstage
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    BACKSTAGE APP                             │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐       │
  │  │ Catalog  │ │Scaffolder│ │ TechDocs │ │  Search  │  ...  │
  │  │ Plugin   │ │ Plugin   │ │ Plugin   │ │ Plugin   │       │
  │  └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘       │
  │       │            │            │            │              │
  │  ┌────┴────────────┴────────────┴────────────┴────┐        │
  │  │              PLUGIN FRAMEWORK                   │        │
  │  │  • Plugin Registry (register/load plugins)     │        │
  │  │  • Route Management (URL routing)              │        │
  │  │  • API Client Proxy (frontend→backend calls)   │        │
  │  │  • Component Mounting (render in UI)           │        │
  │  │  • Event Bus (cross-plugin communication)      │        │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

16.2 Frontend Plugins

Frontend plugins render UI components. They use createPlugin() to define the plugin, createRoutableExtension() for page routing (lazy-loaded), and createComponentExtension() for embedding cards on entity pages. They access backend data via the API client proxy — never directly calling databases or external services.

Frontend Plugin Skeleton
  // plugins/my-plugin/src/plugin.ts
  import { createPlugin, createRoutableExtension } from '@backstage/core-plugin-api';

  export const myPlugin = createPlugin({ id: 'my-plugin' });

  export const MyPage = myPlugin.provide(
    createRoutableExtension({
      name: 'MyPage',
      component: () => import('./components/MyPage').then(m => m.MyPage),
      mountPoint: rootRouteRef,
    }),
  );

16.3 Backend Plugins

Backend plugins provide API endpoints and data processing. They use createRouter() for HTTP routes, access databases via PluginDatabaseManager, read URLs via UrlReader, and communicate with other plugins through the event system. Backend plugins can also schedule recurring tasks (cron-like jobs).

Backend Plugin Skeleton
  // plugins/my-plugin-backend/src/index.ts
  import { createRouter } from '@backstage/backend-defaults';
  import { Router } from 'express';

  export async function createPlugin(env) {
    const router = Router();
    router.get('/health', (_, res) => res.json({ status: 'ok' }));
    router.get('/data', async (_, res) => {
      const db = await env.database.getClient();
      const data = await db('my_table').select('*');
      res.json(data);
    });
    return router;
  }

Core Plugins

Built and maintained by the Backstage team: Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search, Kubernetes, Auth. These ship with Backstage and form the foundation.

CI/CD Plugins

Jenkins, GitHub Actions, ArgoCD, GitLab CI, CircleCI. Show build status, trigger jobs, view deployment history directly on entity pages.

Monitoring Plugins

Grafana (embed dashboards), Prometheus (alert status), Datadog (service health), New Relic (APM data). Bring observability into Backstage.

Incident Management

PagerDuty (on-call schedules, incidents), OpsGenie (alerts). Link services to on-call rotations.

Project Management

Jira (issues, boards), Confluence (docs), SonarQube (code quality), Todoist. Track project progress from Backstage.

16.6 Plugin Types — Frontend vs Backend vs Full-Stack

TypeWhat It DoesExampleFiles In
🖥️ Frontend-OnlyRenders UI. Calls external APIs directly from browser.GitHub README card, Grafana iframepackages/app
⚙️ Backend-OnlyProcesses data, no UI. Provides API endpoints.Auth providers, catalog processorspackages/backend

📝 Chapter 16 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 3 types of Backstage plugins? Give an example of each.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Frontend-only: Renders UI only, calls external APIs from browser. Example: GitHub README card.
2. Backend-only: Provides API endpoints, no UI. Example: Auth providers, catalog processors.
3. Full-stack: Both frontend UI + backend logic. Example: Kubernetes plugin, Scaffolder.

Q2
What does createPlugin() do?
Show Answer
Answer

createPlugin() is the entry point for any Backstage frontend plugin. It creates a plugin instance with a unique ID, registers API factories, and provides the plugin's React components. Every frontend plugin starts with createPlugin({ id: 'my-plugin', ... }).

Q3
How many community plugins are available for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

200+ community plugins are available at backstage.io/plugins. They cover CI/CD (Jenkins, GitHub Actions, ArgoCD), monitoring (Grafana, Prometheus, Datadog), incident management (PagerDuty, OpsGenie), project management (Jira), and communication (Slack, Teams).

Q4
How does a frontend plugin access data from a backend plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

Through the API Client Proxy. The frontend defines an API ref with createApiRef() and uses useApi() to call it. The proxy routes requests from the browser through Backstage's backend, which then calls the actual backend plugin. This avoids CORS issues and centralizes authentication.

Q5
What is the Backstage event bus and how do plugins use it?
Show Answer
Answer

The event bus allows plugins to communicate asynchronously. One plugin publishes events, others subscribe. Example: when the Catalog ingests a new entity, it publishes an event — other plugins (like the Scaffolder or Search) react to it. This keeps plugins loosely coupled.

Q6
What does createRoutableExtension() do?
Show Answer
Answer

createRoutableExtension() creates a lazy-loaded page component for a plugin. It defines the route's mount point and component. The component is only loaded when the user navigates to that route — improving initial page load performance.

Q7
What is the difference between a core plugin and a community plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

Core plugins are built and maintained by the Backstage team and ship with every Backstage installation (Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search, Kubernetes, Auth). Community plugins are contributed by external developers/companies (Jenkins, PagerDuty, Grafana, Sentry). Core plugins follow strict quality standards; community plugins vary in maintenance.

Q8
Where are frontend plugins registered to appear in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

In packages/app/src/App.tsx (for route registration) and packages/app/src/components/Root.tsx (for sidebar navigation). The App.tsx file imports the plugin and defines its route; Root.tsx adds a SidebarItem with an icon and link.

Q9
What is createComponentExtension() used for?
Show Answer
Answer

createComponentExtension() creates a card component that can be embedded on entity pages. Unlike createRoutableExtension (which creates full pages), this creates smaller UI pieces like the "About" card, "CI/CD" card, or "K8s" card that appear on entity overview pages.

Q10
You install a community plugin but it doesn't appear. What are 3 things to check?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Installation: Did you run yarn add in both packages/app AND packages/backend?
2. Registration: Did you import and register the plugin in App.tsx (route) and/or Root.tsx (sidebar)?
3. Configuration: Does the plugin need config in app-config.yaml (e.g., API tokens, URLs)? Check the plugin's documentation for required config.

🖥️FrontendcreatePlugin(), createRoutableExtension(). Renders UI components.
⚙️BackendcreateRouter(), PluginDatabaseManager. API endpoints, data processing.
🔄Full-StackBoth frontend + backend. Most common type. K8s, Scaffolder, PagerDuty.

Chapter 17: Essential Plugins for Daily Use

Now that you understand plugin architecture, let's explore the most important plugins you'll use daily — GitHub integration, CI/CD, monitoring, incident management, error tracking, and communication. Each plugin extends Backstage to display real-time data from your existing tools.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔌 Essential🎯 CBA 32%

17.1 GitHub Integration Plugin

Shows repository details, README, contributors, languages, and GitHub Actions CI/CD status on entity pages. Configured via integrations.github in app-config.yaml. Uses the github.com/project-slug annotation to link entities to repos.

17.2 CI/CD Plugins

Jenkins

Shows build history, triggers jobs, displays test results. Annotation: jenkins.io/job-full-name.

GitHub Actions

Displays workflow runs, status, and logs. Built into the GitHub integration plugin.

ArgoCD

Shows GitOps deployment status, sync state, and application health. Perfect for K8s-native teams.

GitLab CI

Displays pipeline status and job logs from GitLab CI/CD.

17.3 Monitoring Plugins

Grafana

Embeds Grafana dashboards directly in Backstage. Configure dashboard URLs and they render in iframes on entity pages.

Prometheus

Shows alert status and firing alerts for services. Links entities to Prometheus alert rules.

Datadog

Displays service health, APM data, and dashboards from Datadog.

17.4 Incident Management

PagerDuty and OpsGenie plugins show on-call schedules, active incidents, and service health status. Link services via pagerduty.com/service-id annotation.

17.5 Error Tracking

Sentry and Rollbar plugins display recent errors, crash rates, and issue lists. Link projects via sentry.io/project-slug annotation.

17.6 Communication

Slack and Microsoft Teams plugins enable notifications and channel integration. Notify teams when builds fail, deployments complete, or incidents occur — all from Backstage.

📝 Chapter 17 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What annotation links an entity to its GitHub repository?
Show Answer
Answer

github.com/project-slug: owner/repo. Example: github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost. This annotation tells the GitHub plugin which repository to display information from.

Q2
What annotation links an entity to its Jenkins job?
Show Answer
Answer

jenkins.io/job-full-name: folder/job-name. Example: jenkins.io/job-full-name: anihpj/jobpost-build.

Q3
What annotation links an entity to PagerDuty?
Show Answer
Answer

pagerduty.com/service-id: SERVICE_ID. Links the entity to its PagerDuty service for on-call and incident information.

Q4
What does the Grafana plugin show on entity pages?
Show Answer
Answer

The Grafana plugin embeds Grafana dashboards directly in Backstage via iframes. It shows service-level metrics, graphs, and panels configured in Grafana. Configure dashboard URLs and they render on entity pages — giving teams instant access to monitoring data without leaving Backstage.

Q5
How does the ArgoCD plugin integrate with Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

The ArgoCD plugin shows GitOps deployment status on entity pages — sync state, application health, and deployment history. It connects to the ArgoCD API server and displays: which version is deployed, whether the app is synced with Git, and the health status of K8s resources.

Q6
What annotation links an entity to Sentry error tracking?
Show Answer
Answer

sentry.io/project-slug: org/project. Example: sentry.io/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost. This links the entity to its Sentry project, showing recent errors, crash rates, and issue lists on the entity page.

Q7
How do you configure the Jenkins plugin for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Add the Jenkins proxy endpoint in app-config.yaml under proxy.endpoints, install both frontend and backend packages, and add the jenkins.io/job-full-name annotation to entity YAML. The plugin then fetches build history and status from Jenkins and displays it on the entity's CI/CD card.

Q8
What's the difference between the GitHub integration plugin and the GitHub Actions plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

The GitHub integration plugin shows repository metadata (README, contributors, languages, about) and is configured in integrations.github. GitHub Actions is part of the same plugin and shows CI/CD workflow runs and status. They work together — the integration provides repo info; Actions provides build/deploy status.

Q9
You install the PagerDuty plugin but see no data. What 3 things do you check?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Annotation: Is pagerduty.com/service-id in the entity's catalog-info.yaml?
2. API Token: Is the PagerDuty API token configured in app-config.yaml?
3. Service ID: Is the service ID correct? Find it in PagerDuty → Service Directory → your service → URL contains the ID.

Q10
How would you add monitoring visibility for the jobpost service using Backstage plugins?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Install the Grafana plugin and configure a dashboard URL showing jobpost metrics
2. Install the Prometheus plugin and link jobpost's alert rules
3. Add Sentry plugin for error tracking with sentry.io/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
4. Add Kubernetes plugin for pod health and deployment status
All four give complete observability on the jobpost entity page.
