Chapter 18: Building Custom Plugins

When existing plugins don't cover your needs, you build custom plugins. This chapter walks through creating a custom dashboard plugin for anihpj — from scaffolding to testing. You'll learn the complete plugin development workflow.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🛠️ Hands-On🎯 CBA 32%

18.1 When to Build a Custom Plugin

Build a custom plugin when: (1) No existing plugin exists for your tool, (2) You need custom internal workflows, (3) You want organization-specific dashboards aggregating data from multiple sources. Always check the plugin marketplace first — 200+ plugins may already cover your needs.

18.2 Creating a Frontend Plugin — Step by Step

Custom Plugin Directory Structure
  plugins/anihpj-dashboard/
  ├── src/
  │   ├── index.ts              ← Plugin entry, exports
  │   ├── plugin.ts             ← createPlugin() definition
  │   ├── api.ts                ← API client (if backend needed)
  │   ├── routes.ts             ← Route definitions
  │   └── components/
  │       ├── AnihpjPage.tsx    ← Main plugin page
  │       ├── JobStatsCard.tsx  ← Dashboard card
  │       └── ServiceHealth.tsx ← Health status component
  ├── package.json
  └── README.md
Scaffold & Register Steps
  # 1. Scaffold the plugin
  $ yarn backstage-cli create-plugin
  ? Plugin ID: anihpj-dashboard

  # 2. Register in packages/app/src/App.tsx
  import { AnihpjDashboardPage } from '@internal/plugin-anihpj-dashboard';
  <Route path="/anihpj-dashboard" element={<AnihpjDashboardPage />} />

  # 3. Add to sidebar in packages/app/src/components/Root.tsx
  <SidebarItem icon={DashboardIcon} to="anihpj-dashboard" text="anihpj" />

18.3 Creating a Backend Plugin

Backend plugins use createRouter() from Express to define API endpoints. They access the database, read URLs, and communicate with other plugins via the event bus. Scaffold with yarn backstage-cli create-plugin --backend.

18.4 Building the anihpj Dashboard Plugin

Purpose: Custom dashboard showing jobpost metrics — active job listings, applications per day, deployment status, and recent errors. Frontend: React components with Material UI cards and charts. Backend: API endpoints aggregating data from the jobpost database, Kubernetes API, and Sentry.

18.5 Testing Custom Plugins

Test plugins with: (1) Jest unit tests for components and API logic, (2) Integration tests for full plugin workflows, (3) Manual testing with yarn dev — the plugin appears in the sidebar and can be tested interactively.

📝 Chapter 18 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What command scaffolds a new Backstage plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

yarn backstage-cli create-plugin. This scaffolds the plugin with the required structure: plugin.ts, route definitions, and component files. Add --backend for a backend-only plugin.

Q2
After creating a plugin, what 3 files must you update to make it visible?
Show Answer
Answer

1. packages/app/src/App.tsx — Import the plugin and add a route
2. packages/app/src/components/Root.tsx — Add a SidebarItem
3. (If backend) packages/backend/src/index.ts — Register the backend plugin

Q3
When should you build a custom plugin vs using an existing one?
Show Answer
Answer

Build custom when: no existing plugin exists, you need custom internal workflows, or you need organization-specific data aggregation. Always check the marketplace (200+ plugins) first — it may already exist.

Q4
What are the 3 files you must update after creating a frontend plugin to make it visible?
Show Answer
Answer

1. packages/app/src/App.tsx — Import and add a <Route> for the plugin page
2. packages/app/src/components/Root.tsx — Add a <SidebarItem> with icon and link
3. (Optional but common) packages/backend/src/index.ts — Register backend plugin if it has one

Q5
What files would an anihpj dashboard plugin contain?
Show Answer
Answer

plugin.ts (createPlugin definition), routes.ts (URL routing), api.ts (API client for backend data), and components/ with AnihpjPage.tsx (main page), JobStatsCard.tsx (job metrics), ServiceHealth.tsx (K8s health). Standard pattern: plugin → routes → api → components.

Q6
How do you test a custom plugin before deploying?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Jest unit tests: Test individual components and API logic
2. Integration tests: Test the plugin as a whole with mock data
3. Manual testing: Run yarn dev — the plugin appears in the sidebar and can be tested interactively in the browser

Q7
What's the difference between yarn backstage-cli create-plugin and create-plugin --backend?
Show Answer
Answer

create-plugin scaffolds a frontend-only plugin with React components and routing. create-plugin --backend scaffolds a backend-only plugin with Express routes and database access. For a full-stack plugin, you typically create both a frontend and backend plugin separately.

Q8
How does a custom plugin access the Backstage database?
Show Answer
Answer

Through PluginDatabaseManager in the backend plugin. The plugin requests a database client, which provides access to the PostgreSQL database. Plugins should NEVER access the database directly from the frontend — all database access must go through backend API endpoints for security.

Q9
Your custom plugin builds successfully but doesn't appear in the sidebar. What did you miss?
Show Answer
Answer

You likely missed the Root.tsx registration. Creating the plugin and adding a route in App.tsx makes the page accessible, but to show it in the sidebar, you must add a <SidebarItem icon={...} to="plugin-route" text="Plugin Name" /> in packages/app/src/components/Root.tsx.

Q10
You need to build a plugin that shows jobpost metrics from 3 different sources (DB, K8s, Sentry). How would you architect it?
Show Answer
Answer

Build a full-stack plugin: the backend creates 3 API endpoints — /metrics/db (queries jobpost PostgreSQL), /metrics/k8s (calls K8s API for pod stats), /metrics/errors (calls Sentry API). The frontend calls these 3 endpoints via the API proxy and displays the aggregated data in MUI cards and charts. This keeps data access secure and centralized.

🔗RegisterApp.tsx (route) + Root.tsx (sidebar) + backend/index.ts (if backend).

☸️ Part 7: Backstage on Kubernetes 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 19: Deploying Backstage on Kubernetes

Production Backstage runs on Kubernetes — the same platform your services run on. This chapter walks through building Docker images, writing K8s manifests, deploying with Helm, and scaling Backstage for enterprise use. You'll deploy a complete Backstage instance running on K8s with PostgreSQL, Secrets, and Ingress.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours☸️ K8s🎯 CBA 22%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why Kubernetes is the right platform for production Backstage
  • ✅ Build a production Docker image with multi-stage builds
  • ✅ Write complete K8s manifests: Deployment, Service, Ingress, ConfigMap, Secret, PVC
  • ✅ Deploy Backstage with Helm charts
  • ✅ Scale, update, and rollback Backstage on K8s
🎓 CBA Relevance
Infrastructure 22%
Deploying Backstage = CBA Infrastructure (22%)

The CBA tests your ability to deploy Backstage to production. Know: Docker multi-stage builds, K8s Deployment structure (replicas, env vars from Secrets, ConfigMap volumes), PostgreSQL as the required production database, and Helm as the preferred deployment method.

19.1 Why Deploy Backstage on Kubernetes?

🔄
High Availability

Run multiple replicas. If one pod crashes, K8s auto-restarts it. Traffic is load-balanced across healthy pods.

📈
Auto-Scaling

Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) scales Backstage pods based on CPU/memory. Handle traffic spikes automatically.

🏥
Self-Healing

K8s monitors pod health with liveness/readiness probes. Unhealthy pods are restarted or replaced automatically.

📋
GitOps Ready

Everything declared in YAML. Deploy via ArgoCD/Flux. Git is the single source of truth for your Backstage config.

19.3 Docker Image for Backstage

Multi-Stage Dockerfile
  # Stage 1: Build
  FROM node:18-bookworm-slim AS build
  WORKDIR /app
  COPY package.json yarn.lock ./
  RUN yarn install --frozen-lockfile --production=false
  COPY . .
  RUN yarn tsc && yarn build

  # Stage 2: Production (smaller image)
  FROM node:18-bookworm-slim AS runtime
  WORKDIR /app
  COPY --from=build /app/packages/backend/dist ./dist
  COPY --from=build /app/packages/backend/package.json ./
  COPY --from=build /app/node_modules ./node_modules
  ENV NODE_ENV=production
  EXPOSE 7007
  CMD ["node", "packages/backend", "--config", "app-config.yaml"]

Multi-Stage Build

Stage 1 installs ALL dependencies (including devDependencies for TypeScript), compiles, and builds. Stage 2 copies only the compiled output and production dependencies. This keeps the final image small (~300MB vs ~1.5GB).

Image Tagging Strategy

Use semantic versioning: v1.2.3. Tag each build with the Git commit SHA: registry.anihpj.com/backstage:v1.2.3-abc1234. This ensures every deployment is traceable to a specific commit.

19.4 Kubernetes Manifests

Deployment

Complete K8s Deployment YAML for Backstage
  apiVersion: apps/v1
  kind: Deployment
  metadata:
    name: backstage
    namespace: backstage
  spec:
    replicas: 3
    selector:
      matchLabels:
        app: backstage
    template:
      metadata:
        labels:
          app: backstage
      spec:
        containers:
        - name: backstage
          image: registry.anihpj.com/backstage:v1.0
          ports:
            - containerPort: 7007
          env:
            - name: POSTGRES_HOST
              valueFrom:
                secretKeyRef:
                  name: backstage-secrets
                  key: postgres-host
            - name: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
              valueFrom:
                secretKeyRef:
                  name: backstage-secrets
                  key: postgres-password
            - name: GITHUB_TOKEN
              valueFrom:
                secretKeyRef:
                  name: backstage-secrets
                  key: github-token
          volumeMounts:
            - name: app-config
              mountPath: /app/app-config.yaml
              subPath: app-config.yaml
          resources:
            requests:
              memory: "512Mi"
              cpu: "500m"
            limits:
              memory: "2Gi"
              cpu: "2000m"
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /healthcheck
              port: 7007
            initialDelaySeconds: 30
            periodSeconds: 10
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /healthcheck
              port: 7007
            initialDelaySeconds: 10
            periodSeconds: 5
        volumes:
          - name: app-config
            configMap:
              name: backstage-config

Service + Ingress

ClusterIP Service on port 7007. Ingress with TLS termination and host: backstage.anihpj.com. Annotations for cert-manager if using Let's Encrypt.

ConfigMap + Secret

ConfigMap stores app-config.yaml (mounted as file). Secret stores: POSTGRES_HOST, POSTGRES_PASSWORD, GITHUB_TOKEN. Never put secrets in ConfigMap!

PersistentVolumeClaim

Used for TechDocs cache if using local publisher. In production with cloud publisher (GCS/S3/Azure), PVC is not needed for TechDocs — only for PostgreSQL data.

19.5 Helm Chart for Backstage

Deploying with Helm
  # Add Backstage Helm repo
  $ helm repo add backstage https://backstage.github.io/charts
  $ helm repo update

  # Install with custom values
  $ helm install backstage backstage/backstage \
      --namespace backstage --create-namespace \
      --set appConfig.app.title="anihpj Developer Portal" \
      --set database.client=pg \
      --set database.connection.host=postgres.anihpj.com \
      --set ingress.enabled=true \
      --set ingress.host=backstage.anihpj.com

  # Upgrade
  $ helm upgrade backstage backstage/backstage -n backstage

  # Rollback
  $ helm rollback backstage -n backstage

📝 Chapter 19 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
Why should Backstage use PostgreSQL instead of SQLite in production?
Show Answer
Answer

SQLite only supports a single writer — with 3 Backstage replicas on K8s, concurrent writes would cause data corruption. PostgreSQL handles concurrent connections from multiple pods safely and is the only supported production database for Backstage.

Q2
What does a multi-stage Dockerfile achieve for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Stage 1 (build): installs ALL deps, compiles TypeScript, builds the app. Stage 2 (runtime): copies only compiled output + production deps. Result: smaller image (~300MB vs ~1.5GB), faster pulls, smaller attack surface.

Q3
Where should you store app-config.yaml in a K8s deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

In a ConfigMap, mounted as a file in the pod. Create: kubectl create configmap backstage-config --from-file=app-config.yaml. Mount it as a volume. Secrets (DB passwords, tokens) should be in a separate Secret resource, not in the ConfigMap.

Q4
What Kubernetes resources are needed to deploy Backstage in production?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Deployment — 3 replicas, resource limits, env vars from Secrets
2. Service — ClusterIP on port 7007
3. Ingress — TLS termination, host routing
4. ConfigMap — app-config.yaml mounted as volume
5. Secret — PostgreSQL creds, GitHub tokens
6. PVC — For PostgreSQL data (or TechDocs cache if using local publisher)

Q5
How do you pass environment variables from a K8s Secret to a Backstage pod?
Show Answer
Answer

Use env.valueFrom.secretKeyRef in the Deployment spec. Example:
env:
- name: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
valueFrom:
secretKeyRef:
name: backstage-secrets
key: postgres-password

Q6
What image tagging strategy should you use for production Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Use semantic versioning + Git SHA: registry.anihpj.com/backstage:v1.2.3-abc1234. This ensures: (1) every deployment is traceable to a specific commit, (2) you can quickly identify which version is running, (3) rollbacks are precise. Never use :latest in production.

Q7
What Helm commands deploy, upgrade, and rollback Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Install: helm install backstage backstage/backstage -n backstage --create-namespace
Upgrade: helm upgrade backstage backstage/backstage -n backstage
Rollback: helm rollback backstage -n backstage

Q8
Why do you need liveness and readiness probes for Backstage on K8s?
Show Answer
Answer

Liveness probe: Checks if the pod is still running. If it fails, K8s restarts the pod. Endpoint: /healthcheck.
Readiness probe: Checks if the pod is ready to serve traffic. If it fails, K8s removes the pod from the Service load balancer. Use /healthcheck for both.

Q9
You have 3 Backstage pods but only one shows TechDocs. What's wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

You're using the local TechDocs publisher. Each pod has its own ./techdocs-output/ directory — only the pod that built the docs has them. Fix: switch to cloud storage (GCS/S3/Azure) so all pods access the same docs, or use builder: external to pre-build docs in CI/CD.

Q10
Write the kubectl commands to deploy Backstage from scratch.
Show Answer
Answer

kubectl create namespace backstage
kubectl create secret generic backstage-secrets --from-literal=POSTGRES_PASSWORD=xxx --from-literal=GITHUB_TOKEN=xxx -n backstage
kubectl create configmap backstage-config --from-file=app-config.yaml -n backstage
kubectl apply -f k8s/deployment.yaml
kubectl apply -f k8s/service.yaml
kubectl apply -f k8s/ingress.yaml
kubectl scale deploy/backstage --replicas=3 -n backstage

📊 Chapter 19 — Visual Summary

☸️K8s PlatformHA, auto-scaling, self-healing, GitOps. The right platform for production.
🐳DockerMulti-stage build. Build stage → Runtime stage. Smaller, faster, safer.
📋ManifestsDeployment, Service, Ingress, ConfigMap, Secret, PVC. All in YAML.
⛵HelmPackage manager for K8s. Install, upgrade, rollback with one command.
💾PostgreSQLRequired for production. StatefulSet + PVC. NEVER use SQLite on K8s.
🎯CBA 22%Infrastructure domain. Docker, K8s manifests, Helm deployment.

Chapter 20: Kubernetes Plugin Deep Dive

The K8s plugin connects Backstage to your clusters and displays live deployment status on entity pages. This chapter dives into the plugin architecture, multi-cluster setup, custom dashboards, and troubleshooting.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours☸️ Deep Dive🎯 CBA 22%

20.1 K8s Plugin Architecture

Frontend: Kubernetes tab on entity pages renders deployment info, pod lists, and service details. Backend: KubernetesServiceApi communicates with the K8s API server using the configured service account token. Resources are discovered by matching entity annotations (backstage.io/kubernetes-id) to K8s resource names/labels.

20.2 Multi-Cluster Setup

Add multiple clusters to kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods[].clusters[] in app-config.yaml. Each cluster has its own name, URL, auth token, and optional dashboard URL. Backstage queries ALL configured clusters and merges results on entity pages.

20.3 Custom Kubernetes Dashboards

Build custom K8s overview cards showing pod metrics (CPU, memory), deployment history, and cluster health. Use the K8s plugin's API to fetch data and render it in custom MUI components.

20.4 Troubleshooting K8s Plugin

ErrorCauseFix
"403 Forbidden"RBAC: ServiceAccount lacks permissionsAdd get/list/watch on Deployments, Pods, Services
"Connection refused"Wrong API server URL or network issueVerify cluster URL and network connectivity
"TLS certificate"Self-signed cert not trustedSet skipTLSVerify: true (dev only) or add CA cert
No K8s tabMissing annotation or pluginAdd backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation

📝 Chapter 20 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What RBAC permissions does Backstage need for the K8s plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

get, list, watch on Deployments, Pods, Services, Ingresses, ReplicaSets. Backstage is read-only — it never creates or modifies K8s resources. Create a ClusterRole with these permissions and bind it to the ServiceAccount.

Q2
How do you connect Backstage to multiple K8s clusters?
Show Answer
Answer

Add multiple entries to kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods[0].clusters[]. Each has its own name, URL, authProvider, and serviceAccountToken. Backstage queries all clusters and merges results.

Q3
Your K8s plugin shows "403 Forbidden". What's wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

The ServiceAccount token doesn't have sufficient RBAC permissions. Check the ClusterRole and ClusterRoleBinding — the SA needs get, list, watch verbs on Deployments, Pods, Services, Ingresses, and ReplicaSets.

Q4
What is the difference between singleTenant and multiTenant service locator methods?
Show Answer
Answer

singleTenant: Looks for K8s resources in ONE configured namespace. multiTenant: Looks in ALL namespaces matching the entity's annotations. Multi-tenant is the default and most common — it lets each team's services live in their own namespace while Backstage discovers them all.

Q5
How does the K8s plugin discover which resources belong to an entity?
Show Answer
Answer

Through annotations on the Catalog entity: backstage.io/kubernetes-id (match by name), backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace (scope to namespace), or backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selector (match by labels). The plugin queries the K8s API using these filters.

Q6
What cluster locator methods are available besides 'config'?
Show Answer
Answer

Besides config (static list), there are cloud-specific locators: gke (Google Kubernetes Engine), eks (Amazon EKS), aks (Azure AKS). These auto-discover clusters in your cloud account without manual configuration. config is the most commonly used for explicit control.

Q7
How do you add a custom K8s dashboard card to an entity page?
Show Answer
Answer

Create a custom plugin using createComponentExtension() that fetches K8s data via the K8s API and renders it in MUI components. Register it on the entity page. The card can show pod CPU/memory metrics, deployment history, or any custom K8s data.

Q8
Your K8s plugin can't connect because of a self-signed certificate. What do you do?
Show Answer
Answer

Option 1 (dev only): Set skipTLSVerify: true in the cluster config. Option 2 (production): Add the CA certificate to Backstage's trusted certificates and set skipTLSVerify: false. Never use skipTLSVerify in production — it disables TLS verification entirely.

Q9
What K8s permissions does the Backstage ServiceAccount need at minimum?
Show Answer
Answer

get, list, watch on: pods, services, deployments, replicasets, ingresses, configmaps (in all relevant namespaces). Backstage is read-only — it never creates, updates, or deletes K8s resources. Create a ClusterRole with these permissions bound to the Backstage ServiceAccount.

Q10
You add a second K8s cluster to Backstage. How do you verify both are working?
Show Answer
Answer

Visit an entity page with K8s annotations — both clusters' resources should appear in the K8s tab, labeled by cluster name. Check Backstage backend logs for successful connections to both cluster APIs. Verify with: curl :7007/api/kubernetes/services/:entityRef — the response should include resources from both clusters.

📊 Chapter 20 — Visual Summary

🏗️ArchitectureFrontend tab + Backend K8s API client. Discovers by annotations.
🌐Multi-ClusterMultiple clusters in config. Queries all, merges results.
📊DashboardsCustom MUI cards with pod metrics, deployment history.
🔧Troubleshoot403=RBAC, Connection=URL, TLS=cert, No tab=annotation.

Chapter 21: GitOps with Backstage and ArgoCD

GitOps means Git is the single source of truth — all infrastructure and application config lives in Git, and automated tools reconcile the actual state with the desired state. Backstage + ArgoCD together create a complete GitOps workflow visible from a single pane of glass.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour🔄 GitOps🎯 CBA 22%

21.1 What is GitOps?

GitOps principles: (1) Git as source of truth — all config in Git, (2) Declarative — everything defined in YAML, (3) Automated reconciliation — tools like ArgoCD/Flux continuously sync desired state with actual state, (4) Pull-based — the cluster pulls config from Git (not pushed from CI).

21.2 Backstage + ArgoCD Integration

The ArgoCD plugin shows application sync status, deployment history, and health on entity pages. Configure the ArgoCD API endpoint in app-config.yaml, add the plugin, and annotate entities with argocd/app-name. Developers see deployment status without leaving Backstage.

21.3 GitOps Workflow for anihpj

The complete flow: Developer pushes code → GitHub Actions builds Docker image → Updates K8s manifest with new image tag in Git → ArgoCD detects change → Auto-syncs to K8s cluster → Backstage shows deployment status. Rollback: git revert the manifest change → ArgoCD auto-syncs back. Entire pipeline visible in Backstage.

📝 Chapter 21 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 4 principles of GitOps?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Git as single source of truth
2. Declarative configuration (YAML)
3. Automated reconciliation (ArgoCD/Flux)
4. Pull-based deployment (cluster pulls from Git)

Q2
How does the ArgoCD plugin integrate with Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

It shows ArgoCD application sync status, health, and deployment history on entity pages. Configure the ArgoCD API URL in app-config.yaml and add argocd/app-name annotation to entity YAML.

Q3
How do you rollback a deployment in a GitOps workflow?
Show Answer
Answer

git revert the commit that changed the image tag. ArgoCD detects the Git change and auto-syncs the cluster back to the previous version. No manual kubectl commands needed — everything through Git.

Q4
What annotation links an entity to its ArgoCD application?
Show Answer
Answer

argocd/app-name: my-app or argocd/app-selector: app=my-app. This tells the ArgoCD plugin which application to display status for on the entity page. Add it to the entity's metadata.annotations.

Q5
What's the difference between push-based and pull-based deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Push-based: CI/CD pushes changes to the cluster (kubectl apply). The cluster is passive.
Pull-based (GitOps): An agent (ArgoCD) inside the cluster pulls changes from Git and applies them. The cluster is active. GitOps is pull-based — the cluster continuously reconciles with Git.

Q6
How does Backstage show ArgoCD deployment status on entity pages?
Show Answer
Answer

The ArgoCD plugin calls the ArgoCD API server and displays: application sync status (synced/out-of-sync), health status (healthy/degraded), deployment history, and sync duration. Configure the ArgoCD API URL in app-config.yaml and annotate entities with argocd/app-name.

Q7
What are Flux and how does it compare to ArgoCD?
Show Answer
Answer

Both are GitOps tools. ArgoCD has a web UI, is application-focused, and has a native Backstage plugin. Flux is CLI-focused, uses K8s custom resources for configuration, and integrates natively with Helm. Both achieve the same goal: Git as source of truth → auto-sync to cluster.

Q8
Describe the complete GitOps flow for anihpj from code push to Backstage visibility.
Show Answer
Answer

1. Dev pushes code → 2. CI builds Docker image → 3. CI updates image tag in Git (k8s/deployment.yaml) → 4. ArgoCD detects Git change → 5. ArgoCD syncs new image to K8s cluster → 6. Backstage K8s plugin fetches new pod status → 7. ArgoCD plugin shows sync status → Full visibility in Backstage.

Q9
Your ArgoCD app shows "Out of Sync" in Backstage. What does this mean?
Show Answer
Answer

It means the live state in the K8s cluster doesn't match the desired state in Git. Someone may have manually changed a resource (kubectl edit), or a new commit changed the manifests but ArgoCD hasn't synced yet. ArgoCD will auto-sync (if configured) or wait for manual sync.

Q10
How would you set up a complete GitOps pipeline for anihpj using Backstage + GitHub Actions + ArgoCD?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Backstage Template creates the repo with Dockerfile, CI workflow, and K8s manifests
2. GitHub Actions on push: runs tests → builds Docker image → pushes to registry → updates image tag in k8s/deployment.yaml → commits back to repo
3. ArgoCD monitors the repo → detects the manifest change → auto-syncs to K8s
4. Backstage shows: CI/CD status (GitHub Actions card), deployment status (ArgoCD card), pod health (K8s tab). All from one entity page.

Part 7 Quick Wins for CBA: Backstage production = PostgreSQL + K8s + Helm. NEVER use SQLite on K8s. Docker multi-stage builds keep images small (~300MB). K8s manifests: Deployment (3 replicas, probes) + Service (ClusterIP) + Ingress (TLS) + ConfigMap + Secret. The K8s plugin needs get/list/watch RBAC. GitOps = Git is truth + ArgoCD reconciles. Rollback = git revert, not kubectl.

📋GitOpsGit = truth. Declarative YAML. ArgoCD reconciles. Pull-based.
🔄ArgoCDSync status, health, history on entity pages. argocd/app-name annotation.
⏪Rollbackgit revert → ArgoCD auto-syncs. No kubectl needed.

🔐 Part 8: Authentication & RBAC 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 22: Authentication Providers

Authentication is how Backstage knows who you are. This chapter covers the sign-in flow, all major auth providers (GitHub, Okta, Azure AD, Google, Auth0, Keycloak), how to configure them, and how to handle guest access. The CBA exam tests your ability to configure authentication correctly.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~2 hours🔐 Auth🎯 CBA 22%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the Backstage authentication flow
  • ✅ Compare all major auth providers and choose the right one
  • ✅ Configure GitHub OAuth step-by-step
  • ✅ Set up enterprise SSO with Okta/Azure AD
  • ✅ Configure multiple auth providers simultaneously
🎓 CBA Relevance
Infrastructure 22%
Auth = Infrastructure Domain (22% of CBA)

The CBA tests auth configuration: which provider to use, how to configure OAuth/OIDC, sign-in resolvers, and the auth flow. Key concepts: providers (GitHub, Okta, Azure AD), JWT session tokens, group mapping, and NEVER using guest access in production.

22.1 Authentication in Backstage — The Sign-In Flow

Authentication Flow
  👤 User clicks "Sign In"
       │
       ▼
  🔀 Redirect to Auth Provider (GitHub/Okta/Google)
       │
       ▼
  ✅ User authenticates with provider
       │
       ▼
  🎫 Provider returns token to Backstage callback URL
       │
       ▼
  🔑 Backstage creates session (JWT)
       │  • Identity from provider (name, email, avatar)
       │  • Mapped to Backstage User entity
       │  • Group memberships synced
       ▼
  🖥️ User is logged in! Session stored in browser cookie.

22.2 Auth Provider Comparison

ProviderTypeBest ForSetup Effort
GitHubOAuth2Dev teams on GitHub. Auto-maps teams→Groups🟢 Easy
Okta / Azure ADOIDC/SAMLEnterprise SSO. Group sync via claims🟡 Medium
GoogleOAuth2GSuite/Google Workspace orgs🟢 Easy
Auth0 / KeycloakOIDCFlexible identity hub / Self-hosted🟡-🔴 Medium-Complex
Guest (No Auth)None⚠️ Development ONLY — NEVER in production🟢 None

22.3 GitHub Authentication — Step by Step

Setting Up GitHub OAuth
  # 1. Create GitHub OAuth App
  #    GitHub → Settings → Developer settings → OAuth Apps → New
  #    Callback URL: http://localhost:7007/api/auth/github/handler/frame

  # 2. Configure app-config.yaml
  auth:
    environment: development
    providers:
      github:
        development:
          clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}
          clientSecret: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET}

  # 3. Set env vars and start
  $ export GITHUB_CLIENT_ID=abc123
  $ export GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET=xyz789
  $ yarn dev

  # 4. Test: Click Sign In → GitHub → Authorize → Logged in!

22.4 Enterprise SSO (Okta / Azure AD)

For enterprise organizations, Okta and Azure AD provide SAML/OIDC-based SSO. Configure the provider in app-config.yaml with clientId, clientSecret, and the provider's discovery URL. Group claims from the provider are automatically mapped to Backstage Group entities.

22.5 Multiple Auth Providers

Backstage supports multiple providers simultaneously. Configure each in auth.providers and users see all options on the sign-in page. Custom sign-in resolvers determine how identities from different providers map to Backstage Users.

22.6 Guest Access

Guest access allows unauthenticated browsing. Enable with auth.environment: development and no providers. NEVER use guest access in production — it exposes your entire Backstage instance without any authentication. The CBA exam explicitly tests this security boundary.

📝 Chapter 22 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the Backstage auth flow from sign-in to logged-in?
Show Answer
Answer

User clicks Sign In → Redirect to provider (OAuth/OIDC) → User authenticates → Provider returns token to Backstage callback URL → Backstage creates JWT session → User is logged in with identity and group memberships.

Q2
Which auth provider is best for a team already using GitHub?
Show Answer
Answer

GitHub OAuth — it auto-maps GitHub teams to Backstage Groups, uses existing GitHub accounts, and is the easiest to set up (just create an OAuth App). Perfect for dev teams already on GitHub.

Q3
Can you use guest access in production?
Show Answer
Answer

NO — never. Guest access exposes your entire Backstage instance without authentication. Anyone can see all your services, APIs, docs, and potentially sensitive information. Production MUST have auth configured. Guest access is for local development only.

Q4
What is the callback URL for GitHub OAuth in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

http://localhost:7007/api/auth/github/handler/frame for development. For production, replace localhost with your Backstage domain: https://backstage.anihpj.com/api/auth/github/handler/frame. This URL must be registered in the GitHub OAuth App settings.

Q5
How does Backstage store user sessions after authentication?
Show Answer
Answer

As a JWT (JSON Web Token) stored in a browser cookie. The JWT contains the user's identity, group memberships, and expiration time. This token is sent with every request to the Backstage backend for authorization. Sessions expire after a configurable duration.

Q6
What is sign-in resolver and what does it do?
Show Answer
Answer

A sign-in resolver maps the identity from an auth provider to a Backstage User entity. It takes the provider's profile (email, username, display name) and creates or matches a Backstage User. Custom resolvers handle cases where the default mapping doesn't fit your organization's identity model.

Q7
How do GitHub teams map to Backstage Groups?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage automatically reads the user's GitHub team memberships from the OAuth token and creates/matches Backstage Group entities. The sign-in resolver handles this mapping. Team names become Group names, and the hierarchy (parent/child teams) is preserved.

Q8
Can you use multiple auth providers simultaneously in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes. Configure each provider in auth.providers in app-config.yaml. Users see all configured providers on the sign-in page and can choose which one to use. Each provider needs its own sign-in resolver to map identities correctly.

Q9
Where do you configure auth providers in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

In app-config.yaml under auth.providers. Each provider has its own configuration block with clientId, clientSecret, and provider-specific settings. Secrets MUST use ${ENV_VAR} syntax — never hardcode client secrets.

Q10
You configured GitHub auth but sign-in fails with "redirect_uri mismatch". What's wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

The callback URL registered in your GitHub OAuth App doesn't match the one Backstage is using. Check: (1) The OAuth App's callback URL matches YOUR_DOMAIN/api/auth/github/handler/frame, (2) The app.baseUrl in app-config.yaml matches the domain in the callback URL, (3) No trailing slashes in either URL.

📊 Chapter 22 — Visual Summary

🔀OAuth FlowRedirect → Authenticate → Token → JWT Session → Logged In.
🐙GitHubEasiest setup. Auto-maps teams. Best for GitHub-native teams.
🏢EnterpriseOkta/Azure AD for SSO. Group sync via claims. Medium setup.
⚠️GuestDev only. NEVER in production. CBA explicitly tests this.

Chapter 23: Permissions & RBAC

Permissions control what users can do in Backstage. The permission framework uses Allow/Deny rules with conditions to create fine-grained access control. This chapter covers the framework, policy configuration, RBAC roles, and testing.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🛡️ RBAC🎯 CBA 22%

23.1 The Backstage Permission Framework

Permission System
  ┌─────────────────────┐
  │   Permission Policy │ ← Define what actions exist & who can do them
  └────────┬────────────┘
           │
           ▼
  ┌─────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐
  │ Allow Rules │   │ Deny Rules  │
  │ "If owner,  │   │ "If not in  │
  │  allow del" │   │  group, deny"│
  └──────┬──────┘   └──────┬──────┘
         └────────┬────────┘
                  ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │    Permission Evaluator      │
  │ Checks each request against  │
  │ the policy rules             │
  └──────────────────────────────┘

23.2 Configuring Permission Policies

Permission Policy Example
  // packages/backend/src/plugins/permission.ts
  export default createPermissionPolicy({
    allow: [
      { // Anyone can READ entities
        permission: catalogEntityReadPermission,
        resourceType: 'catalog-entity',
        action: 'read',
      },
      { // Only OWNERS can DELETE their entities
        permission: catalogEntityDeletePermission,
        resourceType: 'catalog-entity',
        action: 'delete',
        conditions: {
          rule: 'IS_ENTITY_OWNER',
          resourceType: 'catalog-entity',
        },
      },
    ],
  });

23.3 RBAC Configuration

Map Backstage Groups to roles (admin, developer, viewer). Groups inherit permissions: admins can do everything, developers can create/update their own entities, viewers can only read. Configure in the permission policy file.

23.4 Conditional Permissions

Built-in conditions: IS_ENTITY_OWNER (only the owner), hasAnnotation (check annotation), hasLabel (check label), hasSpec (check spec fields). Combine with AND/OR for complex rules.

23.5 Testing Permissions

Test permissions by: (1) Using the permission evaluator API, (2) Testing as different users with different group memberships, (3) Checking backend logs for permission denial reasons. Always test deny rules before deploying.

📝 Chapter 23 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What's the difference between Allow and Deny rules in the permission framework?
Show Answer
Answer

Allow rules: Grant access when conditions are met. "If owner, allow delete."
Deny rules: Block access when conditions are met. "If not in group X, deny." Deny rules take precedence over Allow rules — if a Deny matches, access is blocked regardless of any Allow rules.

Q2
What does the IS_ENTITY_OWNER condition do?
Show Answer
Answer

IS_ENTITY_OWNER checks if the current user is the owner of the entity being accessed. Used to restrict actions like delete, update, or unregister to only the entity's owner. Combined with Allow rules to grant owner-only permissions.

Q3
Where is the permission policy file located?
Show Answer
Answer

packages/backend/src/plugins/permission.ts. This file defines Allow and Deny rules using createPermissionPolicy(). Any changes require restarting the Backstage backend to take effect.

Q4
Do Deny rules or Allow rules take precedence?
Show Answer
Answer

Deny rules take precedence. If any Deny rule matches a request, access is blocked — even if an Allow rule also matches. This follows the security principle of "deny by default, allow explicitly." Always test Deny rules carefully — they can accidentally block legitimate access.

Q5
What built-in permission conditions are available?
Show Answer
Answer

IS_ENTITY_OWNER — user is the entity's owner
hasAnnotation — entity has a specific annotation
hasLabel — entity has a specific label
hasSpec — check entity spec fields
Conditions can be combined with AND/OR for complex access rules.

Q6
How do you map Backstage Groups to RBAC roles?
Show Answer
Answer

Define roles in the permission policy with group-based conditions. Example: allow catalogEntityDeletePermission only for members of team-platform (admins). Use hasMember or group-based conditions to check the user's group membership against allowed groups.

Q7
What's the difference between catalogEntityReadPermission and catalogEntityDeletePermission?
Show Answer
Answer

catalogEntityReadPermission controls who can VIEW entities. Usually granted to everyone.
catalogEntityDeletePermission controls who can DELETE entities. Usually restricted to owners or admins.
There are also create and update permissions for fine-grained control over entity lifecycle.

Q8
How do you test that permissions are working correctly?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Log in as different users with different group memberships
2. Try actions that should be allowed and denied
3. Check backend logs for permission evaluation results
4. Use the permission evaluator API: POST /api/permission/authorize to test rules programmatically
Always test BOTH allow and deny paths.

Q9
A user reports they can't delete their own entity despite being the owner. What's likely wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

The permission policy likely doesn't have an Allow rule for catalogEntityDeletePermission with IS_ENTITY_OWNER condition. Check: (1) Is the delete permission configured in the policy? (2) Is the IS_ENTITY_OWNER condition applied? (3) Is the user correctly identified as the entity's owner in the catalog?

Q10
How would you restrict the Scaffolder (template creation) to only the platform-team group?
Show Answer
Answer

Add an Allow rule for scaffolderTemplateCreatePermission with a condition checking group membership: conditions: { rule: 'HAS_MEMBER', params: { group: 'team-platform' } }. This grants template creation access only to members of team-platform. All other users are implicitly denied.

📊 Chapter 23 — Visual Summary

🛡️FrameworkAllow/Deny rules → Permission Evaluator → Access decision.
✅AllowGrant access. "If condition met, allow." Owner-based, role-based.
🚫DenyBlock access. Takes precedence over Allow. Group-based exclusion.
🔍ConditionsIS_ENTITY_OWNER, hasAnnotation, hasLabel, hasSpec. AND/OR combos.

Chapter 24: Integrating External Identity Providers

Beyond authentication, you can sync users and groups from external identity sources like LDAP, Active Directory, Okta, and Azure AD. This keeps your Backstage org chart automatically in sync with your actual organization.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1 hour🆔 Identity🎯 CBA 22%

24.1 LDAP / Active Directory Integration

Backstage can ingest Users and Groups from LDAP/AD using the @backstage/plugin-catalog-backend-module-ldap module. Configure the LDAP server URL, bind credentials, and search filters in app-config.yaml. Entities are synced on a schedule — changes in AD automatically appear in Backstage.

24.2 Okta / Azure AD Group Sync

Sync groups from Okta or Azure AD by configuring the provider to include group claims in the auth token. Backstage auto-creates Group entities from these claims. Members are added/removed automatically when group membership changes in the provider.

24.3 Custom Sign-In Resolvers

When built-in resolvers aren't enough, build a custom sign-in resolver to map external identities to Backstage Users. The resolver receives the provider's identity payload and returns a Backstage User reference. Use this when you need custom mapping logic beyond what built-in providers offer.

📝 Chapter 24 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
How does Backstage sync users from LDAP?
Show Answer
Answer

Using the @backstage/plugin-catalog-backend-module-ldap module. Configure LDAP server URL, bind DN, and search filters in app-config.yaml. Users and Groups are ingested as Catalog entities on a configurable schedule.

Q2
What is a sign-in resolver?
Show Answer
Answer

A sign-in resolver maps the identity from an auth provider (GitHub, Okta, etc.) to a Backstage User entity. It takes the provider's profile data and determines which Backstage user this corresponds to. Custom resolvers are used when the built-in mapping doesn't fit your needs.

Q3
How do group memberships stay in sync with Okta/Azure AD?
Show Answer
Answer

Group claims are included in the auth token from the provider. Backstage reads these claims on every sign-in and updates the user's Group memberships automatically. Changes in Okta/Azure AD are reflected the next time the user signs in.

Q4
What module is needed for LDAP integration?
Show Answer
Answer

@backstage/plugin-catalog-backend-module-ldap. Install it and configure the LDAP server URL, bind DN, bind credentials, and search filters in app-config.yaml. The module runs on a schedule to sync Users and Groups from LDAP into the Backstage Catalog.

Q5
How often does Backstage sync users from LDAP?
Show Answer
Answer

The sync schedule is configurable in app-config.yaml. Default is typically every 30-60 minutes. You can configure the frequency, timeout, and initial delay. Changes in LDAP appear in Backstage after the next sync cycle — not instantly.

Q6
What's the difference between LDAP sync and Okta group claims?
Show Answer
Answer

LDAP sync: Scheduled bulk import. Backstage pulls users/groups from LDAP on a timer. Changes take effect after the next sync cycle.
Okta group claims: Real-time. Group info is in the auth token received at sign-in. Changes take effect on the user's next sign-in (immediate for new sessions).

Q7
When would you build a custom sign-in resolver?
Show Answer
Answer

When built-in resolvers don't fit your needs: (1) Your identity provider uses non-standard claims, (2) You need to transform usernames/emails before mapping, (3) You have multiple identity sources that need custom merging logic, (4) You need to call external APIs to determine user attributes during sign-in.

Q8
How do you configure the LDAP search filter for users?
Show Answer
Answer

In app-config.yaml under the LDAP provider config: users.options.filter: "(objectClass=person)". Customize to filter by department, group membership, or any LDAP attribute. Example: "(&(objectClass=person)(department=Engineering))" for only engineering users.

Q9
What happens when a user is removed from LDAP but still exists in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

The user entity remains in the Backstage Catalog but is marked as orphaned. On the next LDAP sync, Backstage can be configured to either: (1) Remove the user (clean sync), or (2) Keep the user but flag them (preserve history). Choose based on your compliance requirements.

Q10
You need to sync users from both LDAP and Okta into backstage. How would you architect this?
Show Answer
Answer

Configure BOTH: (1) LDAP catalog module for bulk sync of existing users/groups, (2) Okta as an auth provider for sign-in with group claims. LDAP handles initial import and periodic sync; Okta handles real-time auth and group updates. Use custom sign-in resolvers to merge identities from both sources into the same Backstage User entity.

Part 8 Quick Wins for CBA: Auth = who you are (OAuth/OIDC). Permissions = what you can do (Allow/Deny rules). NEVER use guest access in production. GitHub is easiest for dev teams — auto-maps teams to Groups. Okta/Azure AD for enterprise SSO. Sign-in resolvers map provider identities to Backstage Users. Deny rules ALWAYS take precedence over Allow rules. IS_ENTITY_OWNER is the most common permission condition. LDAP syncs on schedule; Okta claims update on sign-in.

📊 Chapter 24 — Visual Summary

📂LDAP/ADBulk import users/groups. Scheduled sync. LDAP module required.
🔄Group SyncOkta/Azure AD group claims auto-sync on every sign-in.
🔧Custom ResolverMap external identities when built-in mapping isn't enough.

🏭 Part 9: Production & CI/CD 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 25: CI/CD Integration with Backstage

Backstage brings CI/CD visibility into one place — build status, deploy history, and pipeline results appear directly on entity pages. This chapter covers integrating GitHub Actions, Jenkins, GitLab CI, and ArgoCD so developers see CI/CD status without leaving Backstage.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔄 CI/CD🎯 CBA 22%

25.1 CI/CD Visibility in Backstage

Entity pages show CI/CD status cards with: build status (passing/failing/running), recent builds, success rate, duration, and deep links to the CI/CD tool. Plugins fetch this data from your CI/CD system's API and render it on the entity page.

25.2 GitHub Actions Integration

Workflow Configuration

Install @backstage/plugin-github-actions. Add github.com/project-slug annotation to entity YAML. Backstage auto-discovers workflow runs and displays status, logs, and duration on the entity's CI/CD card.

Viewing Runs in Backstage

The GitHub Actions tab shows: recent workflow runs, status (✅/❌/🔄), trigger (push/PR/schedule), duration, and links to detailed logs on GitHub. Developers see build status at a glance without opening GitHub.

25.3 Jenkins Integration

Install @backstage/plugin-jenkins. Configure the Jenkins proxy endpoint in app-config.yaml. Add jenkins.io/job-full-name annotation. Shows build history, triggers jobs, displays test results.

25.4 GitLab CI Integration

Install @backstage/plugin-gitlab. Displays pipeline status, job logs, and merge request overview from GitLab CI/CD. Add gitlab.com/project-id annotation to link entities.

25.5 ArgoCD / Flux (GitOps CD)

The ArgoCD plugin shows application sync status, deployment history, and health. Flux integration provides similar GitOps visibility. Both show whether the live state matches the desired state in Git.

25.6 anihpj CI/CD Dashboard

Combining the above: jobpost entity page shows GitHub Actions build status, ArgoCD deployment sync state, and K8s pod health — all on one page. Developers get complete CI/CD visibility from a single pane of glass.

📝 Chapter 25 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
What annotation links an entity to GitHub Actions?
Show Answer
Answer

github.com/project-slug: owner/repo. The GitHub Actions plugin uses this to find workflow runs for the entity's repository.

Q2
How does Backstage display Jenkins build status?
Show Answer
Answer

Via the Jenkins plugin using jenkins.io/job-full-name annotation. The plugin calls the Jenkins API through the Backstage proxy and displays build history, status, and allows triggering builds.

Q3
What CI/CD tools can Backstage integrate with?
Show Answer
Answer

GitHub Actions, Jenkins, GitLab CI, ArgoCD, CircleCI, and more. Each has its own plugin and annotation. All display build/deploy status on entity pages.

Q4
How do you configure the Jenkins proxy endpoint in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

In app-config.yaml under proxy.endpoints: '/jenkins': { target: 'https://jenkins.anihpj.com', headers: { Authorization: 'Bearer ${JENKINS_TOKEN}' } }. This routes frontend requests through Backstage to Jenkins securely.

Q5
What does the ArgoCD plugin show on entity pages?
Show Answer
Answer

Application sync status (synced/out-of-sync), health status (healthy/degraded), deployment history, and sync duration. Configure with argocd/app-name annotation and ArgoCD API URL in app-config.yaml.

Q6
How would you set up CI/CD visibility for the anihpj/jobpost service?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Add github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost for GitHub Actions status
2. Add argocd/app-name: jobpost for deployment status
3. Install both plugins (frontend + backend)
4. Configure ArgoCD API URL in app-config.yaml
Result: Build status AND deploy status visible on jobpost entity page.

Q7
What's the difference between the GitHub integration plugin and the GitHub Actions plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

GitHub integration: Shows repo metadata — README, contributors, languages. Configured in integrations.github.
GitHub Actions: Shows CI/CD workflow runs — build status, logs, duration. Part of the same plugin family but focuses on CI/CD specifically.

Q8
How does Backstage proxy CI/CD API calls securely?
Show Answer
Answer

Through the Backstage proxy — the frontend calls /api/proxy/jenkins/..., and the Backstage backend forwards the request to Jenkins with the configured auth token. The token never reaches the browser. Configured in app-config.yaml under proxy.endpoints.

Q9
Your GitHub Actions plugin shows no workflow runs. What 3 things do you check?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Annotation: Is github.com/project-slug correct? (must be owner/repo format)
2. Token: Is ${GITHUB_TOKEN} set with repo and workflow scopes?
3. Plugin installed: Is @backstage/plugin-github-actions in both packages/app and packages/backend?

Q10
How would you add a custom CI/CD card showing data from a tool that has no Backstage plugin?
Show Answer
Answer

Build a custom plugin: (1) Create a frontend plugin with createPlugin() and createComponentExtension(), (2) Create a backend plugin that calls your tool's API, (3) Register both in App.tsx/Root.tsx and backend/index.ts, (4) Use the proxy to route API calls securely. The custom card renders on entity pages alongside built-in CI/CD cards.

Chapter 26: Production Operations

Running Backstage in production requires proper planning — PostgreSQL, TechDocs storage, search engine, upgrades, and monitoring. This chapter covers the essential production checklist and operational procedures.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🏭 Ops🎯 CBA 22%

26.1 Production Checklist

☑ PostgreSQL (not SQLite) ☑ HTTPS/TLS ☑ Auth configured (no guest) ☑ Cloud storage for TechDocs ☑ Search engine (Elasticsearch/PostgreSQL) ☑ Monitoring (Prometheus + Grafana) ☑ Logging ☑ Backups ☑ 3+ replicas ☑ CI/CD pipeline for Backstage itself.

26.2 PostgreSQL Setup

Use a managed PostgreSQL service (RDS, Cloud SQL) or StatefulSet on K8s with PVC for data persistence. Configure backups, connection pooling, and monitoring. Critical: NEVER use SQLite in production — it can't handle concurrent writes from multiple pods.

26.3 TechDocs in Production

Use cloud storage publisher (GCS/S3/Azure) so all K8s replicas share the same docs. Set builder to external — build docs in CI/CD, not on the Backstage server. This keeps Backstage pods stateless and fast.

26.4 Search in Production

SQLite-based search works for dev but not production. Use Elasticsearch or PostgreSQL-based search for production. Configure in app-config.yaml under search.elasticsearch. Elasticsearch provides fast full-text search across all entities and TechDocs.

26.5 Backstage Upgrades

Upgrade process: (1) Read the changelog and migration guide, (2) Update package.json versions, (3) Run yarn backstage-cli migrate for DB schema changes, (4) Test in staging, (5) Deploy with Helm upgrade. Always backup PostgreSQL before major upgrades.

📝 Chapter 26 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
Why must you use PostgreSQL (not SQLite) in production?
Show Answer
Answer

SQLite can't handle concurrent writes — with 3+ Backstage replicas, you'd get data corruption. PostgreSQL supports concurrent connections and is the only supported production database for Backstage.

Q2
What should the TechDocs builder be set to in production?
Show Answer
Answer

builder: external — docs are pre-built by CI/CD and published to cloud storage. Backstage only serves them. This keeps pods stateless and fast.

Q3
What's the upgrade process for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Read changelog → Update versions → Run yarn backstage-cli migrate → Test in staging → Deploy with Helm upgrade → Backup PostgreSQL before major upgrades.

Q4
What 5 items are on the production checklist for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. PostgreSQL (not SQLite)
2. HTTPS/TLS enabled
3. Auth configured (no guest access)
4. Cloud storage for TechDocs
5. Monitoring (Prometheus + Grafana)
Plus: search engine, logging, backups, 3+ replicas, CI/CD pipeline.

Q5
Why should you use Elasticsearch for production search?
Show Answer
Answer

SQLite-based search (default) doesn't scale and only works on a single pod. Elasticsearch provides: distributed full-text search, high availability with multiple nodes, fast queries across millions of entities/docs, and shared access for all Backstage replicas.

Q6
What does yarn backstage-cli migrate do?
Show Answer
Answer

It applies database schema migrations that come with new Backstage versions. When you upgrade Backstage, the database schema may change — migrate updates your PostgreSQL tables to match. Always backup before running this command.

Q7
How do you configure a managed PostgreSQL service for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

In app-config.yaml: backend.database.client: pg, connection.host: ${POSTGRES_HOST} (RDS/Cloud SQL endpoint), connection.port: 5432, connection.user/password via env vars. Use SSL connection. Configure the managed service with automated backups and monitoring.

Q8
Your Backstage upgrade failed and the app won't start. How do you recover?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Rollback the deployment: helm rollback backstage to previous version
2. Restore PostgreSQL from backup if migrations corrupted data
3. Check logs for the specific error
4. Fix the issue in staging before retrying
5. Never upgrade production without testing in staging first.

Q9
What cloud storage options exist for TechDocs in production?
Show Answer
Answer

Google Cloud Storage (GCS), AWS S3, Azure Blob Storage, or OpenStack Swift. Configure the publisher in app-config.yaml: techdocs.publisher.type: 'googleGcs' with bucket name and credentials. Cloud storage = shared access for all K8s replicas.

Q10
You need to migrate Backstage from SQLite to PostgreSQL. What are the steps?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Set up PostgreSQL (managed service or K8s StatefulSet)
2. Export SQLite data (if needed)
3. Update app-config.yaml: client: pg with connection details
4. Set PostgreSQL env vars (POSTGRES_HOST, POSTGRES_PASSWORD)
5. Run yarn backstage-cli migrate to create schema
6. Test in staging
7. Deploy to production
Note: SQLite → PostgreSQL migration may require a fresh start if schema differs.

📊 Chapter 26 — Visual Summary

💾PostgreSQLRequired for production. Managed service or StatefulSet. Backup before upgrades.
📖TechDocsCloud storage publisher. External builder. Stateless pods.
🔍SearchElasticsearch or PostgreSQL. Not SQLite for production search.
⬆️UpgradesChangelog → migrate → test → Helm upgrade. Always backup.

Chapter 27: Monitoring & Observability

Production Backstage needs monitoring — metrics, logs, alerts, and analytics. This chapter covers setting up a complete observability stack with Grafana, Prometheus, Loki, and Alertmanager.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1 hour📊 O11y🎯 CBA 22%

27.1 Full Observability Stack

Grafana Dashboards

Create Backstage-specific dashboards showing: request rate, error rate, latency percentiles, catalog entity count, active users. Embed via the Grafana plugin.

Prometheus Metrics

Backstage exposes metrics at /metrics. Scrape with Prometheus for: HTTP request duration, error counts, catalog operations, scaffolder task status.

Loki Log Aggregation

Ship Backstage logs to Loki for centralized log search. Query logs by service, error level, or trace ID. Combine with Grafana for correlated metrics+logs views.

Alertmanager Alerts

Configure alerts for: high error rate, pod restarts, database connection failures, certificate expiry. Route to PagerDuty/Slack for immediate notification.

27.2 Logging

Backstage uses Winston for structured logging. Configure log level via LOG_LEVEL env var. In production, ship logs to Loki/ELK/Splunk. Include request IDs for tracing. Set log level to info (not debug) in production.

27.3 Alerting

Key alerts: Backstage is down (>1 min), error rate >5%, database connection failures, certificate expiring (<30 days), disk space >80%. Route critical alerts to PagerDuty, warnings to Slack.

27.4 Backstage Analytics

Track usage metrics: most viewed entities, most used templates, search queries, active users. Use Backstage's built-in analytics API or integrate with Google Analytics/Matomo. Helps justify Backstage ROI and guide improvements.

📝 Chapter 27 — CBA Practice Questions

Q1
Where does Backstage expose Prometheus metrics?
Show Answer
Answer

At the /metrics endpoint on the Backstage backend (port 7007). Configure Prometheus to scrape this endpoint for HTTP metrics, catalog operations, and scaffolder tasks.

Q2
What logging library does Backstage use?
Show Answer
Answer

Winston — a structured logging library for Node.js. Configure log level via environment variable. Ship logs to Loki, ELK, or Splunk in production.

Q3
What are 3 critical alerts for Backstage in production?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Backstage is down (>1 min) — PagerDuty
2. Error rate >5% — PagerDuty
3. Database connection failures — PagerDuty
Warnings: certificate expiry, high disk usage → Slack.

Q4
What metrics does Backstage expose at /metrics?
Show Answer
Answer

HTTP request duration and count, error rates (by status code), catalog operations (entity reads/writes), scaffolder task status, and Node.js process metrics (CPU, memory, event loop). All in Prometheus format for scraping.

Q5
How do you correlate Backstage logs with metrics in Grafana?
Show Answer
Answer

Use Loki for logs and Prometheus for metrics — both data sources in Grafana. Include request IDs in logs. In Grafana, you can click a spike in a Prometheus graph and jump to the corresponding Loki logs filtered by that time range and request ID.

Q6
What Winston log levels should you use in dev vs production?
Show Answer
Answer

Development: debug — shows detailed information for debugging.
Production: info — shows important operational events. Never use debug in production — it generates excessive log volume and may expose sensitive data. Set via LOG_LEVEL env var.

Q7
How would you set up a Grafana dashboard for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Point Prometheus to scrape Backstage /metrics
2. Add Prometheus as a data source in Grafana
3. Create panels for: request rate, error rate, p95 latency, catalog entity count
4. Embed the dashboard in Backstage via the Grafana plugin
5. Set up alerts in Grafana/Alertmanager for threshold breaches.

Q8
What analytics should you track for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Most viewed entities, most used templates, top search queries, daily active users, time to create new service (via templates). These metrics help justify Backstage ROI and guide improvements. Use Backstage's analytics API or Google Analytics.

Q9
Your Backstage /metrics endpoint returns 404. What happened?
Show Answer
Answer

The metrics endpoint may not be enabled by default in all Backstage setups. Check: (1) Is the @backstage/backend-plugin-metrics package installed? (2) Is it registered in packages/backend/src/index.ts? (3) Try :7007/metrics (not :3000 — metrics are on the backend port).

Q10
How would you set up complete observability for Backstage in production?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Metrics: Prometheus scrapes /metrics → Grafana dashboards
2. Logs: Winston → Loki → searchable in Grafana
3. Alerts: Alertmanager → PagerDuty (critical) + Slack (warnings)
4. Traces: OpenTelemetry → Jaeger/Tempo (optional)
5. Analytics: Built-in analytics API → usage dashboards
All four pillars (metrics, logs, alerts, traces) give complete observability.

Part 9 Quick Wins for CBA: CI/CD plugins use annotations to link entities. GitHub Actions = github.com/project-slug. Jenkins = jenkins.io/job-full-name. ArgoCD = argocd/app-name. Production checklist: PostgreSQL, HTTPS, Auth, Cloud storage, Monitoring. NEVER SQLite in prod. TechDocs builder = external in production. Search = Elasticsearch (not SQLite). Upgrade process: changelog → migrate → test → deploy. /metrics endpoint for Prometheus. Winston logger: debug in dev, info in prod.
