🎓 Part 10: Advanced & CBA Exam 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 28: Backstage APIs — Automating Everything

Backstage exposes powerful REST APIs for the Catalog, Scaffolder, Search, TechDocs, and Auth — enabling automation, CI/CD integration, Slack bots, and custom tooling. Every operation you can do in the UI, you can do programmatically.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔌 API🎯 CBA 32%

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • ✅ Understand all 5 Backstage REST API categories (Catalog, Scaffolder, Search, TechDocs, Auth)
  • ✅ Perform CRUD operations on catalog entities via the API
  • ✅ Execute software templates programmatically and track task status
  • ✅ Build automation scripts for CI/CD pipeline integration
  • ✅ Know the authentication model (Bearer tokens, service-to-service)

🎯 CBA Exam Relevance — Customizing Backstage 32%

The CBA exam tests your ability to automate Backstage programmatically. You'll see questions about which API endpoint to use for specific operations, how to authenticate API calls, and how to integrate Backstage APIs into CI/CD pipelines. Know the Catalog and Scaffolder APIs well — they're the most tested.

💡 CBA TIP: Memorize the 5 main API base paths: /api/catalog/, /api/scaffolder/v2/, /api/search/, /api/techdocs/, /api/auth/. The exam expects you to know which API to call for which task.

28.1 Backstage REST API Overview

Base URL: http://localhost:7007/api/ (dev) or https://backstage.YOURCOMPANY.com/api/ (prod). All API calls require authentication via a Bearer token in the Authorization header.

📋 Backstage API Reference Table

API CategoryBase PathPurposeKey Endpoints
Catalog/api/catalogCRUD on entities/entities, /locations, /entity-facets
Scaffolder/api/scaffolder/v2Execute templates/tasks, /tasks/:id, /tasks/:id/eventstream
Search/api/searchQuery search index/query — accepts ?term= and filter params
TechDocs/api/techdocsAccess documentation/metadata/:kind/:ns/:name, /docs/:kind/:ns/:name/*
Auth/api/authToken management/:provider/refresh, /:provider/logout

Authentication: All API calls require Authorization: Bearer <token>. Tokens come from the auth provider (e.g., GitHub OAuth). For service-to-service automation, use a Backstage-generated service token configured in app-config.yaml under backend.auth.keys.

28.2 Catalog API — CRUD Operations

List & Get Entities

GET /api/catalog/entities — returns JSON array of all entities. Supports filtering: ?filter=kind=Component,spec.lifecycle=production. GET /api/catalog/entities/by-name/:kind/:namespace/:name — get a single entity by its triplet identifier.

Create & Update Entities

POST /api/catalog/entities — create a new entity from a JSON body (the YAML converted to JSON). PUT /api/catalog/entities/by-uid/:uid — update an existing entity. POST /api/catalog/locations — register a new location (URL or file path) that Backstage will ingest.

Delete & Refresh

DELETE /api/catalog/entities/by-uid/:uid — remove an entity (requires delete permission). POST /api/catalog/refresh — force immediate re-reading of ALL catalog locations. Useful after adding/updating locations. POST /api/catalog/locations/:id — refresh a single location.

Entity Facets & Stats

GET /api/catalog/entity-facets — get aggregate counts by kind, lifecycle, owner, namespace. Perfect for building catalog dashboards. Returns JSON like: {"kind":[{"value":"Component","count":42},...]}.

28.3 Scaffolder REST API — Templates as Code

The Scaffolder API lets you trigger software templates from any system — CI/CD pipelines, Slack bots, internal CLIs, or even scheduled jobs.

Execute a Template

POST /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks — the main endpoint. Body must include templateRef (e.g., "template:default/django-service") and values (the template parameters). Returns a taskId for tracking.

Track Task Status

GET /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId — get the current status of a template execution. Statuses: open, processing, completed, failed. GET /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId/eventstream — SSE stream for real-time log output.

28.4 Automation Examples — CI/CD & Beyond

Here are real-world examples you can use in your pipelines:

Auto-Register a Service in CI/CD

# Register a new entity via CI/CD pipeline
curl -X POST \
  https://backstage.anihpj.com/api/catalog/entities \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $BACKSTAGE_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "apiVersion": "backstage.io/v1alpha1",
    "kind": "Component",
    "metadata": {
      "name": "new-service",
      "description": "Auto-registered from CI/CD"
    },
    "spec": {
      "type": "service",
      "lifecycle": "production",
      "owner": "team-platform"
    }
  }'

Trigger a Template from CI/CD

# Trigger a Django service template from CI/CD
curl -X POST \
  https://backstage.anihpj.com/api/scaffolder/v2/tasks \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $BACKSTAGE_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "templateRef": "template:default/django-service",
    "values": {
      "name": "new-service",
      "owner": "team-platform",
      "description": "Created via CI pipeline"
    }
  }'

Sync External Data into Catalog

Use the API to push data from external sources (CMDB, asset management, spreadsheets) into Backstage's catalog. This is how large orgs migrate from legacy systems: write a script that transforms external data → entity JSON → POST to catalog API. Run as a cron job for ongoing sync.

⚡ SPEED TIP: The Catalog API is the most-tested API on the CBA. Know: (1) POST /entities creates, (2) POST /locations registers, (3) POST /refresh refreshes all. The Scaffolder API uses /scaffolder/v2/tasks (not v1!). For automation, always use Bearer tokens from env vars — never hardcode.

📝 Chapter 28 — CBA Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
How do you list all entities via the Catalog API?
Show Answer
Answer

GET /api/catalog/entities. Returns JSON array of all entities. Supports filtering: ?filter=kind=Component,spec.lifecycle=production.

Explanation

The Catalog API is Read-only by default for GET requests. The filter query parameter uses Backstage's entity filter syntax: kind=X,spec.lifecycle=Y. This is the most common API call for building automation scripts — always check /entities first before creating duplicates.

Q2
How do you create an entity via the API? Which two approaches exist?
Show Answer
Answer

Approach 1: POST /api/catalog/entities with a JSON body containing the full entity definition. Approach 2: POST /api/catalog/locations to register a URL/file path pointing to a catalog-info.yaml — Backstage then ingests and processes it.

Explanation

Approach 1 (POST /entities) is "push-based" — you push the entity directly. Approach 2 (POST /locations) is "pull-based" — you register a source and Backstage pulls from it. The CBA favors Approach 2 because it follows GitOps: the catalog-info.yaml lives in Git, and Backstage auto-discovers it. Approach 1 is useful for programmatic/automated entity creation where no Git repo exists.

Q3
What's the difference between POST /entities and POST /locations?
Show Answer
Answer

POST /entities directly creates the entity in the catalog. POST /locations registers a source (URL or file) that Backstage will read and process — entities are created as a result of ingestion. Locations are the preferred approach for GitOps workflows.

Explanation

Think of it like this: POST /entities is like handing a book directly to a librarian. POST /locations is like telling the librarian "new books arrive at this URL every day — go check it." The location approach scales better because one location URL can produce many entities (e.g., a GitHub org with 100 repos, each with catalog-info.yaml).

Q4
What endpoint refreshes the entire catalog? When should you use it?
Show Answer
Answer

POST /api/catalog/refresh. Use after adding/updating catalog locations or after changes to catalog-info.yaml files in Git. Forces immediate re-processing. In production, Backstage auto-refreshes periodically (configurable via catalog.processingInterval), but you can trigger this for urgent updates.

Explanation

By default, Backstage refreshes the catalog every few minutes. But when you push a new catalog-info.yaml to GitHub and need it visible NOW (e.g., during a demo or incident), POST /refresh forces immediate ingestion. Note: this refreshes ALL locations, which can be expensive on large catalogs. For a single location, use POST /api/catalog/locations/:id instead.

Q5
How do you execute a software template via the Scaffolder API?
Show Answer
Answer

POST /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks with a JSON body containing templateRef (e.g., "template:default/django-service") and values (the template input parameters). Returns a taskId. Track progress with GET /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId.

Explanation

The templateRef format is template:<namespace>/<template-name>. The values object must match the template's defined parameters in its template.yaml. The Scaffolder API is asynchronous — it returns immediately with a taskId, and the template runs in the background. Always check the task status before assuming success: completed = success, failed = check the event stream for errors.

Q6
How do you authenticate Backstage API calls?
Show Answer
Answer

All API calls require Authorization: Bearer <token> header. Tokens come from the auth provider (user tokens via OAuth). For service-to-service automation, configure backend.auth.keys in app-config.yaml to generate service tokens.

Explanation

There are two token types: User tokens (obtained via OAuth login — used for user-driven API calls) and Service tokens (static tokens configured in app-config.yaml under backend.auth.keys — used for CI/CD automation). Service tokens bypass the OAuth flow and are ideal for pipelines. Never share user tokens in scripts — always use service tokens for automation.

Q7
What are the 5 main API categories in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Catalog API (/api/catalog) — CRUD on entities. 2. Scaffolder API (/api/scaffolder/v2) — execute templates. 3. Search API (/api/search) — query search index. 4. TechDocs API (/api/techdocs) — access documentation. 5. Auth API (/api/auth) — token management.

Explanation

Memorize these 5 for the CBA exam. The most-tested are Catalog and Scaffolder. Remember: the Scaffolder API uses /v2/ (not v1!), and the Search API is /query (not /search — that's the UI route). Each API category is backed by a separate backend plugin that you can extend or replace.

Q8
How do you get entity counts grouped by kind (facets)?
Show Answer
Answer

GET /api/catalog/entity-facets. Returns JSON with aggregate counts: "kind":[{"value":"Component","count":42},{"value":"API","count":15},...]. Also supports faceting by spec.lifecycle, spec.owner, and metadata.namespace.

Explanation

Facets are pre-computed aggregate counts — they're fast even on catalogs with 10,000+ entities. Use them for building dashboards (e.g., "42 Components, 15 APIs, 8 Systems"). The facet endpoint doesn't return entity details, just counts. This is a CBA favorite because it tests whether you understand the difference between listing entities (heavy) and faceting (lightweight).

Q9
How do you track the status of a Scaffolder task?
Show Answer
Answer

GET /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId returns the current status: open → processing → completed or failed. For real-time streaming, use GET /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId/eventstream (SSE — Server-Sent Events).

Explanation

The task lifecycle is: open (queued) → processing (running template actions) → completed (success) or failed (error). The /eventstream endpoint uses SSE (Server-Sent Events) — a one-way stream from server to client — which is ideal for CI/CD pipelines that want real-time log output. Poll /tasks/:taskId every 2-3 seconds for a simple status check; use /eventstream for live debugging.

Q10
What's the difference between Scaffolder API v1 and v2?
Show Answer
Answer

v2 (/api/scaffolder/v2/) is the current version with task-based execution and event streaming. v1 is deprecated. The CBA exam tests on v2. Always use /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks, never /api/scaffolder/v1/.

Explanation

v1 used a synchronous "job" model — you submitted a template and waited for the full response. v2 introduced task-based async execution: submit a task, get a taskId immediately, and poll/stream for results. This was a critical architectural improvement because templates can take minutes to run (cloning repos, running CI, etc.). The CBA exam explicitly tests on v2 — any answer mentioning v1 is a distractor.

📊 Ch 28 — Visual Summary

📋Catalog APICRUD on entities. GET/POST/PUT/DELETE. Locations, refresh, facets.
🚀Scaffolder v2POST /tasks, GET status, SSE event stream. templateRef + values.
🔍Search APIQuery /query?term=X. Filters by kind, lifecycle, namespace.
🤖AutomationCI/CD auto-registration. Slack bots. Cron sync. Bearer tokens.

Chapter 29: Performance, Scaling & High Availability

As your Backstage adoption grows from 10 to 10,000 developers, you need to scale. This chapter covers performance optimization at every layer, horizontal scaling on Kubernetes, high availability architectures, and caching strategies for enterprise deployments.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours📈 Scale🎯 CBA 32%

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • ✅ Identify performance bottlenecks across frontend, backend, catalog, and search layers
  • ✅ Configure horizontal scaling with HPA on Kubernetes
  • ✅ Design a highly available Backstage architecture (multi-AZ, DB failover)
  • ✅ Implement caching at multiple levels (Redis, CDN, DB query cache)
  • ✅ Monitor performance using Backstage's built-in /metrics endpoint

🎯 CBA Exam Relevance — Customizing Backstage 32%

The CBA tests your understanding of Backstage's stateless architecture and scaling principles. Expect questions about why multiple replicas work (stateless design), what external dependencies are needed (PostgreSQL, cloud storage), and how caching fits into the architecture.

💡 CBA TIP: The key exam concept is that Backstage pods are stateless — any pod can handle any request. State lives in PostgreSQL (user data, catalog) and cloud storage (TechDocs). This is WHY horizontal scaling works.

29.1 Performance Optimization

Backstage performance must be tuned at every layer. Here's a systematic breakdown:

📈 Performance Optimization — Layer by Layer

LayerBottleneckSolutionImpact
FrontendLarge JS bundle, slow page loadsCode splitting, lazy loading, tree shaking🔽 40-60% load time
Backend APISlow database queriesQuery optimization, connection pooling, indexing🔽 50-80% query time
Catalog IngestionProcessing 1000s of entitiesBatch processing, incremental ingestion, parallel workers🔽 60% processing time
SearchSlow full-text queriesElasticsearch tuning, index optimization, sharding🔽 70% search latency
TechDocsRebuilding all docs on every changeExternal build storage, CDN, incremental builds🔽 50% build time

Monitoring: Backstage exposes a /metrics endpoint (Prometheus format) with request latency, error rates, and resource usage. Use Grafana dashboards to track trends and identify bottlenecks before they impact users.

29.2 Horizontal Scaling — Many Pods, One Backstage

    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                    LOAD BALANCER (Ingress)                   │
    │               backstage.anihpj.com:443 → TLS                 │
    └───────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────┘
            │                  │                  │
            ▼                  ▼                  ▼
    ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
    │ Backstage    │  │ Backstage    │  │ Backstage    │
    │ Pod 1        │  │ Pod 2        │  │ Pod 3        │
    │ (stateless)  │  │ (stateless)  │  │ (stateless)  │
    └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘
           │                 │                 │
           └─────────┬───────┴─────────┬───────┘
                     │                 │
                     ▼                 ▼
            ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
            │ PostgreSQL   │  │ Cloud Storage │
            │ (HA Cluster) │  │ (S3/GCS/Azure)│
            │              │  │ TechDocs      │
            └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘

Key points: Run 3+ replicas on K8s with HPA. Each pod is stateless — all state is in PostgreSQL (catalog, users, sessions) and cloud storage (TechDocs). Use kubectl autoscale deployment backstage --cpu-percent=70 --min=3 --max=10. All pods share the same database, so any pod can handle any user request.

29.3 High Availability — Zero Downtime

    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │              HIGH AVAILABILITY ARCHITECTURE                  │
    │                                                             │
    │  Zone A (us-east-1a)     Zone B (us-east-1b)   Zone C (...)│
    │  ┌─────────────┐        ┌─────────────┐       ┌──────────┐ │
    │  │ Backstage   │        │ Backstage   │       │Backstage │ │
    │  │ Pod A1      │        │ Pod B1      │       │ Pod C1   │ │
    │  │ Pod A2      │        │ Pod B2      │       │          │ │
    │  └──────┬──────┘        └──────┬──────┘       └────┬─────┘ │
    │         │                      │                   │       │
    │         └──────────────────────┼───────────────────┘       │
    │                                │                           │
    │              ┌─────────────────┴─────────────────┐         │
    │              ▼                                   ▼         │
    │    ┌──────────────────┐              ┌──────────────────┐  │
    │    │ PostgreSQL       │              │ Cloud Storage    │  │
    │    │ Primary + Replica│              │ Multi-Region     │  │
    │    │ (auto-failover)  │              │ (S3/GCS cross-reg)│  │
    │    └──────────────────┘              └──────────────────┘  │
    └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

HA Checklist: Multi-AZ K8s cluster (≥3 zones), PostgreSQL with streaming replication + automatic failover (Patroni/PgBouncer), cloud storage with cross-region replication for TechDocs, Elasticsearch cluster (≥3 nodes), load balancer with health checks on /healthcheck endpoint. DR Plan: Git contains all config (GitOps) — redeploy in new region in <30 min. Backup PostgreSQL daily, retain 30 days.

29.4 Caching Strategies — Speed at Every Layer

⚡ Multi-Layer Caching Architecture

Cache LayerTechnologyWhat It CachesTTL
CDNCloudFront / Cloud CDNStatic assets, TechDocs HTML1 hour — 1 day
Service WorkerBrowser SWJS bundles, CSS, iconsUntil new deploy
Backend CacheRedisSessions, auth tokens, API responses5–15 minutes
DatabasePostgreSQL shared_buffersFrequently queried entitiesAutomatic (LRU)
Catalog ReadsIn-memory (plugin)Entity definitionsConfigurable

Configuration example in app-config.yaml:

# app-config.yaml — Cache configuration
backend:
  cache:
    store: redis
    connection: redis://redis-service:6379
    defaultTtl: 300  # 5 minutes in seconds
  reading:
    allow:
      - host: "backstage.anihpj.com"

catalog:
  processingInterval: { minutes: 5 }  # How often to check for changes
  readCache:
    enabled: true
    maxAge: { minutes: 60 }  # Cache entity reads for 60 minutes

Pro tip: Redis is the most impactful cache — it reduces database load by 60-80% for repeated queries. For TechDocs, a CDN in front of your cloud storage bucket provides near-instant page loads globally.

⚡ SPEED TIP: Remember: Backstage is stateless → horizontal scaling works. The 3 external dependencies for HA are: PostgreSQL (data), Cloud Storage (TechDocs), Elasticsearch (search). If any of these is a single point of failure, Backstage isn't truly HA.

📝 Chapter 29 — CBA Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
Why can Backstage be horizontally scaled across multiple replicas?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage pods are stateless. All state lives in external services: PostgreSQL (catalog, users, sessions) and cloud storage (TechDocs). Any pod can handle any request since they all connect to the same database and storage.

Explanation

This is the single most important architectural concept for the CBA. Unlike monolithic apps that store session data in memory (requiring sticky sessions), Backstage was designed cloud-native from day one. Every pod is interchangeable — if Pod A handles your login and Pod B handles your catalog search, it doesn't matter. This is why Kubernetes HPA works seamlessly with Backstage.

Q2
What Kubernetes resource enables automatic scaling of Backstage pods?
Show Answer
Answer

Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA). Configure with: kubectl autoscale deployment backstage --cpu-percent=70 --min=3 --max=10. HPA monitors CPU/memory metrics and automatically adds/removes pods based on load.

Explanation

HPA works specifically because Backstage is stateless. If Backstage required sticky sessions, HPA would cause problems — a new pod wouldn't have the user's session data. The minimum 3 replicas ensures high availability (survive 1 pod failure). The max (10+) depends on your PostgreSQL connection pool size — too many pods can overwhelm the database.

Q3
What are the 3 external dependencies needed for a highly available Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. PostgreSQL (with replication/failover) — stores catalog, users, sessions. 2. Cloud Storage (S3/GCS/Azure Blob, multi-region) — stores TechDocs. 3. Elasticsearch (cluster) — powers search. All must be HA for Backstage to be HA.

Explanation

If any of these 3 is a single point of failure, Backstage is NOT truly HA. PostgreSQL failing means no catalog access. Cloud storage failing means no TechDocs. Elasticsearch failing means search is broken. This is a common CBA "trick question" — they'll describe a setup with multiple Backstage pods but a single PostgreSQL instance and ask "Is this HA?" Answer: No.

Q4
What caching options are available for Backstage? Which is most impactful?
Show Answer
Answer

Redis (backend cache — most impactful, 60-80% DB load reduction), CDN (static assets + TechDocs), Service Worker (frontend JS/CSS), PostgreSQL shared_buffers (database level), Catalog in-memory cache (entity reads). Redis is the most impactful for API performance.

Explanation

Redis sits between the Backstage backend and PostgreSQL. When a user requests an entity, Backstage checks Redis first — if found (cache hit), it returns immediately without touching the database. This is critical because database connections are expensive. A well-configured Redis cache can reduce PostgreSQL load by 60-80%, enabling fewer database resources to serve more users.

Q5
How do you configure Redis caching in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

In app-config.yaml, set backend.cache.store: redis and backend.cache.connection: redis://redis-service:6379. Optional: backend.cache.defaultTtl: 300 (seconds = 5 minutes). Requires the Redis plugin to be installed.

Explanation

The store field accepts memory (default, per-pod, not shared) or redis (shared across all pods). Always use redis in multi-pod deployments — memory caching means each pod has its own cache, leading to inconsistency and wasted memory. The defaultTtl (Time To Live) controls how long cached data lives before being refreshed from the database.

Q6
What makes Backstage highly available? List the key components.
Show Answer
Answer

Multi-AZ K8s cluster (≥3 zones), multiple Backstage replicas, PostgreSQL with streaming replication + auto-failover (Patroni/PgBouncer), cloud storage with cross-region replication, Elasticsearch cluster (≥3 nodes), load balancer with health checks, GitOps config for DR.

Explanation

The key insight: HA is NOT just about multiple Backstage pods. It's about ensuring EVERY dependency is HA. The "weakest link" principle applies — if PostgreSQL is a single instance, your entire Backstage is a single point of failure regardless of how many pods you run. The CBA will test this concept by presenting scenarios with 5 Backstage pods but a single DB — the correct answer is "NOT highly available."

Q7
What are the 5 performance optimization layers in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Frontend: Code splitting, lazy loading. 2. Backend API: Query optimization, connection pooling. 3. Catalog Ingestion: Batch processing, parallel workers. 4. Search: Elasticsearch tuning, index optimization. 5. TechDocs: External build storage, CDN, incremental builds.

Explanation

Performance optimization is never one-size-fits-all. The frontend bottleneck (slow page loads) requires different solutions than the catalog bottleneck (slow entity processing). The CBA expects you to match the problem to the solution: "Users complain about slow page loads" → Frontend optimization. "New entities take 10 minutes to appear" → Catalog ingestion optimization. "Search returns results slowly" → Elasticsearch tuning.

Q8
Where does Backstage store its state? Why does this matter for scaling?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage pods are stateless — state lives in external services: PostgreSQL (catalog data, user sessions) and cloud storage (TechDocs files). This matters because it means ANY pod can serve ANY request, enabling true horizontal scaling without sticky sessions.

Explanation

This is the architectural foundation that everything else builds on. Compare with a stateful app like Jenkins (where each agent has local state): you can't just add more Jenkins masters. Backstage's stateless design means scaling is as simple as kubectl scale deployment backstage --replicas=5. The load balancer can use round-robin — no session affinity needed.

Q9
What's the disaster recovery plan for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Since all config is in Git (GitOps), you can redeploy Backstage to a new region in <30 minutes. Backup PostgreSQL daily (retain 30 days). Cloud storage (TechDocs) should use cross-region replication. Elasticsearch snapshots for search index recovery.

Explanation

The beauty of GitOps: your app-config.yaml, catalog-info.yaml files, and template definitions are all in Git. If your K8s cluster burns down, you: (1) create a new K8s cluster, (2) restore PostgreSQL from backup, (3) run kubectl apply on your Backstage manifests, (4) Backstage is back. Cloud storage cross-region replication means TechDocs data is already in the backup region — no restore needed.

Q10
How do you monitor Backstage performance in production?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage exposes a /metrics endpoint in Prometheus format. Use Prometheus + Grafana dashboards to track request latency, error rates, CPU/memory, and catalog processing times. Set up alerts for high latency (>2s p95) and error rate spikes.

Explanation

The Prometheus /metrics endpoint is built into Backstage's backend — no extra plugin needed. Key metrics to watch: http_request_duration_seconds (p95 latency), http_requests_total (request rate), catalog_processing_duration_seconds (catalog ingestion time). Alert when p95 latency exceeds 2 seconds or error rate exceeds 1%. This is an often-overlooked CBA topic — they want to know you understand observability.

🏥HA ArchitectureMulti-AZ, PostgreSQL failover, cross-region storage, ES cluster.
⚡5 Cache LayersCDN, SW, Redis, DB buffers, Catalog. Redis = most impactful.
📊MonitoringPrometheus /metrics endpoint. Grafana dashboards. P95 latency alerts.

Security is non-negotiable for production Backstage. This chapter covers the complete security checklist, secret management strategies, network security layers, vulnerability scanning, and audit logging — everything you need to run Backstage securely in production.

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • ✅ Complete a production security checklist for Backstage
  • ✅ Manage secrets securely using K8s Secrets, Vault, or cloud secret managers
  • ✅ Configure network security: TLS, NetworkPolicies, WAF, CORS, rate limiting
  • ✅ Run vulnerability scans on dependencies and container images
  • ✅ Enable audit logging and set up security monitoring

🎯 CBA Exam Relevance — Customizing Backstage 32%

The CBA exam tests security fundamentals: where to store secrets (NOT in Git!), how to configure CORS, which auth providers to use, and the principle of least privilege. Expect at least 2-3 security-specific questions.

💡 CBA TIP: The #1 security question on the exam: "Where should secrets be stored?" Answer: Never in app-config.yaml or Git. Use ${ENV_VAR} references, K8s Secrets, or external vaults. This is almost guaranteed to appear.

30.1 Production Security Checklist

🔒 Backstage Production Security Checklist

#CheckRequirementPriority
1HTTPS EverywhereTLS 1.2+ on all endpoints. Redirect HTTP→HTTPS.🔴 Critical
2Auth EnforcedSSO required. No guest access in production.🔴 Critical
3Secrets ExternalNo secrets in app-config.yaml or Git. Use K8s Secrets/Vault.🔴 Critical
4RBAC — Least PrivilegeUsers get minimum permissions needed. Default deny.🟡 High
5Image UpdatesRegularly update Backstage images. Scan for CVEs.🟡 High
6WAF + Rate LimitingWeb Application Firewall on Ingress. Rate limit API calls.🟡 High
7Audit LoggingEnable and ship audit logs to SIEM (Splunk/Datadog).🟢 Medium

9CORS ConfiguredRestrict API origins. Never use * in production.🟢 Medium

30.2 Secret Management — Never in Git

⚠️ The Golden Rule

NEVER put secrets (tokens, passwords, keys) in app-config.yaml or any file committed to Git. Always use environment variable substitution: ${GITHUB_TOKEN}. The CBA exam WILL ask about this.

Environment Variables (K8s)

# Kubernetes Secret — store sensitive values
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: backstage-secrets
type: Opaque
stringData:
  GITHUB_TOKEN: "ghp_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
  POSTGRES_PASSWORD: "supersecretpassword"
  BACKSTAGE_SECRET: "random-generated-secret"
---
# Deployment — reference secrets as env vars
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: backstage
        env:
        - name: GITHUB_TOKEN
          valueFrom:
            secretKeyRef:
              name: backstage-secrets
              key: GITHUB_TOKEN

External Vaults (Advanced)

For enterprise: HashiCorp Vault (dynamic secrets, auto-rotation), AWS Secrets Manager (auto-rotation, IAM integration), or Azure Key Vault. Use External Secrets Operator (ESO) to sync vault secrets into K8s Secrets automatically. Token rotation: Use fine-grained GitHub PATs (scoped to specific repos) and rotate every 90 days. Automate rotation via CI pipeline.

30.3 Network Security — Defense in Depth

TLS & Ingress

# Ingress with TLS + WAF annotations (NGINX example)
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: backstage-ingress
  annotations:
    cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: "letsencrypt-prod"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/ssl-redirect: "true"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/rate-limit: "100r/s"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/whitelist-source-range: "10.0.0.0/8"
spec:
  tls:
  - hosts: [backstage.anihpj.com]
    secretName: backstage-tls
  rules:
  - host: backstage.anihpj.com
    http:
      paths:
      - path: /
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
            name: backstage
            port:
              number: 7007

NetworkPolicies & CORS

NetworkPolicies: Restrict Backstage pods to only talk to PostgreSQL, Redis, and Elasticsearch. Block all other egress. Deny all ingress except from the load balancer.

CORS Configuration in app-config.yaml:

# app-config.yaml — CORS + CSP
backend:
  cors:
    origin: "https://backstage.anihpj.com"  # NEVER use "*" in prod!
    methods: [GET, POST, PUT, DELETE]
    credentials: true
  csp:
    connect-src: ["'self'", "https://api.anihpj.com"]
    img-src: ["'self'", "data:", "https://avatars.githubusercontent.com"]

30.4 Vulnerability Management — Scan Everything

🛡️ Security Scanning Tools

ToolWhat It ScansIntegrationFrequency
yarn auditNode.js dependenciesCI pipeline (GitHub Actions)Every commit
SnykDependencies + base imageCI + GitHub integrationEvery PR
Dependabotnpm/yarn packagesGitHub native (auto-PRs)Daily
TrivyContainer imagesCI pipeline (image build)Every build
GrypeContainer images + SBOMCI pipelineEvery build
Backstage UpdatesPlatform itselfManual upgrade cycleMonthly

Workflow: 1) CI builds Backstage image → 2) Trivy/Grype scans it → 3) If critical CVEs found, fail the build → 4) Dependabot auto-opens PRs for vulnerable npm packages → 5) Monthly Backstage version upgrades for platform-level fixes. Subscribe to GitHub Security Advisories for Backstage.

⚡ SPEED TIP: The 3 things the CBA will ask about secrets: (1) Use ${ENV_VAR} in config, never hardcode. (2) K8s Secrets + External Vaults for production. (3) Rotate tokens regularly. Also remember: CORS origin must be specific — "*" is a security fail in production.

📝 Chapter 30 — CBA Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
Where should secrets NEVER be stored? What's the correct approach?
Show Answer
Answer

Never in app-config.yaml or any file committed to Git. Use environment variable substitution (${GITHUB_TOKEN}), Kubernetes Secrets mapped to env vars, or external vaults (HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager).

Explanation

This is THE most important security question on the CBA. The reason: Backstage's app-config.yaml is typically stored in Git, and Git history is forever. A secret committed to Git is compromised permanently. Even if you "remove" it later, it's still in Git history. Always use ${ENV_VAR} substitution — the value is injected at runtime from K8s Secrets.

Q2
How do you reference a secret in app-config.yaml without exposing it?
Show Answer
Answer

Use ${ENV_VAR_NAME} syntax. Example: integrations.github[0].token: ${GITHUB_TOKEN}. The actual token value is injected at runtime via environment variables (from K8s Secrets or vault).

Explanation

The ${...} syntax tells Backstage's config reader to substitute the value at runtime from the process environment. This means your app-config.yaml is safe to commit to Git — it only contains references, not actual secrets. At runtime, the K8s Secret is mounted as an environment variable, and Backstage reads it. This is the standard pattern for all 12-factor apps.

Q3
What tools can scan Backstage for vulnerabilities?
Show Answer
Answer

Dependencies: yarn audit (built-in), Snyk (commercial), Dependabot (GitHub native). Container images: Trivy (open source, fast), Grype (open source, SBOM support). Platform: Regular Backstage version upgrades + GitHub Security Advisories.

Explanation

Scan at 3 levels: (1) Dependencies — yarn audit in CI fails builds on critical CVEs. (2) Container images — Trivy scans the Docker image before pushing to registry. (3) Platform — Backstage releases monthly patches; GitHub Advisories for zero-days. The CBA expects automated CI scanning, not manual checks.

Q4
How do you restrict which origins can call Backstage's API?
Show Answer
Answer

Configure CORS in app-config.yaml: backend.cors.origin: "https://backstage.anihpj.com". This restricts API access to your Backstage domain only. Never use "*" in production — it allows any website to call your API.

Explanation

CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) is a browser security mechanism. Setting origin: "*" means ANY website can make API calls to your Backstage instance — including malicious sites. A CBA trick: they might show you a config with "*" and ask "Is this production-ready?" Answer: No, it's a security vulnerability.

Q5
What are the top 5 items on a Backstage security checklist?
Show Answer
Answer

1. HTTPS everywhere (TLS 1.2+). 2. Auth enforced (SSO, no guest access). 3. Secrets external (K8s Secrets/Vault, never in Git). 4. RBAC least privilege. 5. Regular image updates + vulnerability scanning.

Explanation

These 5 items are ordered by priority. HTTPS and Auth are table stakes — without them, you have no security. Secrets management prevents credential leaks. RBAC limits blast radius if an account is compromised. Regular updates close known vulnerabilities. The CBA may ask you to prioritize: "If you can only implement 2 security measures, which should they be?" Answer: HTTPS + Auth.

Q6
How do you manage GitHub tokens securely for Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Use fine-grained Personal Access Tokens (PATs) scoped to specific repositories and permissions (read-only for catalog, read-write for scaffolder). Store in K8s Secrets. Rotate every 90 days. Automate rotation via CI pipeline or use GitHub App installation tokens.

Explanation

Fine-grained PATs are superior to classic PATs because they're scoped to specific repos + specific permissions. A catalog token only needs contents: read on your org's repos. A scaffolder token needs contents: write + administration: write. If a token leaks, the blast radius is limited. GitHub App tokens auto-expire after 1 hour — even more secure for CI.

Q7
What NetworkPolicies should you apply to Backstage pods?
Show Answer
Answer

Allow egress only to: PostgreSQL (port 5432), Redis (port 6379), Elasticsearch (port 9200), cloud storage APIs (443), and GitHub API (443). Allow ingress only from the Ingress controller. Default deny all other traffic.

Explanation

NetworkPolicies implement "least privilege" at the network layer. If a Backstage pod is compromised, the attacker can only reach the 5 allowed services — they can't scan your internal network or call out to command-and-control servers. The "default deny" is critical: start by blocking everything, then explicitly allow only what Backstage needs.

Q8
How do you enable TLS for Backstage on Kubernetes?
Show Answer
Answer

Use cert-manager with Let's Encrypt to auto-provision TLS certificates. Configure the Ingress resource with TLS section pointing to the cert-manager generated secret. Add the annotation cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: "letsencrypt-prod".

Explanation

cert-manager automates TLS certificate lifecycle: issuance, renewal (every 90 days), and revocation. The Let's Encrypt production issuer provides free, trusted certificates. The Ingress TLS section tells NGINX/Traefik to terminate TLS at the load balancer. Backstage itself doesn't handle TLS — it runs HTTP internally, and the Ingress handles HTTPS externally.

Q9
What's the difference between Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, and K8s Secrets?
Show Answer
Answer

K8s Secrets: Base64-encoded, cluster-scoped, no auto-rotation. Good for simple cases. Vault: Dynamic secrets, auto-rotation, audit logging, multi-cloud. Best for enterprise. AWS/Azure Secrets Manager: Cloud-native, auto-rotation, IAM-integrated. Use External Secrets Operator to sync to K8s.

Explanation

K8s Secrets are NOT encrypted at rest by default (only base64-encoded) — anyone with kubectl get secret -o yaml can decode them. Vault and cloud secret managers provide true encryption, dynamic secret generation (temporary credentials), and audit trails. For CBA: know that K8s Secrets are "good enough" for small deployments but enterprises should use Vault or cloud secret managers.

Q10
How do you enable audit logging in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Configure in app-config.yaml: backend.auditLogger section. Ship logs to stdout/stderr (collected by Fluentd/Filebeat) and forward to SIEM (Splunk, Datadog, ELK). Audit events include: entity creation/deletion, template execution, user login/logout, and permission changes.

Explanation

Audit logging is often overlooked but critical for SOC2/ISO27001 compliance. Backstage writes audit logs to stdout as structured JSON. Your log collector (Fluentd/Filebeat) ships them to your SIEM. Key events to audit: who created/deleted entities, who executed templates, who changed permissions, and who logged in/out. These logs are essential for security investigations.

🔒Secrets Rule${ENV_VAR} only. K8s Secrets → env vars. Vault for enterprise. Never Git.
🌐NetworkTLS + Ingress, NetworkPolicies, WAF, rate limiting, CORS restrict.

Customizing Backstage (32%) — THE BIGGEST DOMAIN

Frontend & backend plugins, React components, Material UI theming, Software Templates (template.yaml actions), TechDocs customization (mkdocs.yml), Scaffolder actions (fetch, publish, register), Catalog processors, and Backstage APIs. If you master this domain, you're 1/3 of the way to passing!

Development Workflow (24%)

Setting up Backstage dev environment (npx, create-app), TypeScript basics, Yarn/NPM workspace management, Docker image building, local development with hot reload, CI/CD pipeline integration, and Backstage CLI commands.

Backstage Infrastructure (22%)

Backstage architecture (frontend/backend split), app-config.yaml structure, Kubernetes deployment, auth providers (GitHub, Okta, Azure AD), permission framework (Allow/Deny rules), scaling considerations, and monitoring.

Backstage Catalog (22%)

Entity kinds (Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group, Template, Location), catalog-info.yaml structure, annotations (backstage.io/*), catalog ingestion (locations, providers, processors), entity relationships, and K8s plugin integration.

31.3 What You'll Actually See on the CBA Exam

📝 Real CBA Question Types

Here are the question patterns you'll encounter, based on the official CBA curriculum:

  • 🔹 "Which entity kind should you use for X?" — Tests your knowledge of the 9 entity kinds
  • 🔹 "What annotation links to Y?" — Tests backstage.io/* annotations
  • 🔹 "Which app-config.yaml section configures Z?" — Tests configuration knowledge
  • 🔹 "What action would you use to publish to GitHub?" — Tests template actions
  • 🔹 "How do you register a new entity location?" — Tests catalog operations
  • 🔹 "What's the correct YAML for a Component entity?" — Tests YAML structure
  • 🔹 "Frontend vs backend plugin — which for this scenario?" — Tests plugin model
  • 🔹 "Where should secrets be stored?" — Tests security knowledge
  • 🔹 "Which auth provider supports SSO for X?" — Tests auth configuration
  • 🔹 "How do you scale Backstage horizontally?" — Tests architecture

🎯 CBA EXAM — Top 10 Strategies

  1. KNOW YOUR YAML — catalog-info.yaml, template.yaml, and app-config.yaml are the heart of the exam. You'll see them in many questions.
  2. MASTER ENTITY KINDS — Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group, Template, Location. Know when to use each.
  3. UNDERSTAND THE PLUGIN MODEL — createPlugin(), routes, API refs, frontend vs backend plugins.
  4. MEMORIZE TEMPLATE ACTIONS — fetch:template, publish:github, catalog:register are the most common.
  5. KNOW TECHDOCS PIPELINE — mkdocs.yml, generators, publishers, cloud storage integration.
  6. AUTH PROVIDERS — GitHub, Okta, Azure AD, sign-in resolvers. Know the config structure.
  7. PERMISSION FRAMEWORK — Allow/Deny rules, conditions, resource types.
  8. ANNOTATIONS — backstage.io/* annotations, what each one does.
  9. K8s PLUGIN — How it discovers resources, required annotations on K8s objects.
  10. PRACTICE! — Create real catalog-info.yaml files, real templates, real mkdocs.yml. Hands-on is the best prep.

31.4 CBA Exam Day — Complete Checklist

📅 1 Week Before Exam

  • ☐ Take a full-length timed practice exam (90 minutes, ~60 questions)
  • ☐ Review ALL wrong answers — understand WHY you got them wrong
  • ☐ Memorize catalog-info.yaml structure (apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec)
  • ☐ Memorize template.yaml structure (parameters, steps, actions)
  • ☐ Review all app-config.yaml top-level sections
  • ☐ Bookmark backstage.io/docs in your exam browser
  • ☐ Test your exam workspace: camera, microphone, desk cleared
  • ☐ Read exam rules & ID requirements (passport/driver's license)

📅 1 Day Before Exam

  • ☐ Light review only — NO heavy studying!
  • ☐ Quick review of 9 entity kinds (Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group, Template, Location)
  • ☐ Quick review of template actions (fetch:template, publish:github, catalog:register)
  • ☐ Prepare workspace: clean desk, good lighting, quiet room
  • ☐ Charge laptop fully, test internet connection (wired preferred)
  • ☐ Sleep 8 hours! 😴 Sleep is more important than cramming

📅 Exam Day — You've Got This! 💪

  • ☐ Light breakfast (protein + carbs), stay hydrated 💧
  • ☐ Bathroom break BEFORE exam starts 🚽
  • ☐ Log in 15 minutes early for proctor check-in
  • ☐ Have water in a clear bottle (no labels)
  • ☐ Open backstage.io/docs in a browser tab
  • ☐ Close all other apps and browser tabs
  • ☐ Deep breath — you've prepared for this! 🎉

31.7 Practice Exam — 30 Questions (Timed: 45 Minutes)

Take this practice exam under timed conditions. Cover all 4 domains proportionally: 10 Customizing (32%), 7 Dev Workflow (24%), 7 Infrastructure (22%), 6 Catalog (22%). Focus areas: YAML structure, annotations, template actions, entity kinds, auth configuration, permissions, K8s plugin, TechDocs pipeline, and security best practices.

📝 Practice Exam Tips

  • ⏱️ Set a timer for 45 minutes (half the real exam)
  • 📖 Keep backstage.io/docs open — practice finding answers quickly
  • ❌ Don't Google — only use docs.backstage.io
  • 📊 Score yourself: 21/30 = 70% (passing). Aim for 25+ (83%+)
  • 🔍 After scoring, review every wrong answer and re-study the topic
  • 🔄 Take the practice exam again 3 days later — aim for improvement
⚡ SPEED TIP — CBA Exam Day: The open-book policy is your secret weapon. Don't waste time memorizing exact YAML syntax — instead, practice navigating backstage.io/docs FAST. Know where to find: entity YAML examples, template action docs, app-config reference, and annotation list. If you can find the answer in <30 seconds in the docs, you'll have time to spare.

📝 Chapter 31 — CBA Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
What is the CBA exam duration, format, and passing score?
Show Answer
Answer

90 minutes, multiple choice (~60 questions), 70% passing score. Online proctored by Linux Foundation. Open book — backstage.io/docs is allowed! Includes 1 free retake. Valid for 3 years.

Q2
What are the 4 CBA domains and their weights?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Customizing Backstage: 32% (plugins, React/MUI, templates, TechDocs, APIs). 2. Development Workflow: 24% (setup, CLI, TypeScript, Yarn/NPM, Docker). 3. Backstage Infrastructure: 22% (architecture, config, K8s, auth, permissions). 4. Backstage Catalog: 22% (entities, YAML, annotations, ingestion).

Explanation

Customizing Backstage (32%) is the largest domain — if you master plugins, templates, and TechDocs, you're 1/3 of the way to passing. The other 3 domains are nearly equal at ~22-24% each. Study strategy: allocate 32% of your study time to Customizing, 24% to Dev Workflow, 22% each to Infrastructure and Catalog.

Q3
What's the recommended time management strategy for the 90-minute CBA exam?
Show Answer
Answer

Pass 1 (0-30min): Easy questions (~30). Pass 2 (30-60min): Medium (~20). Pass 3 (60-80min): Hard + flagged (~10). Review (80-90min): Check flagged. Golden rule: never spend >2 min on one question.

Q4
What does the open-book policy mean for the CBA exam?
Show Answer
Answer

You can access backstage.io/docs during the exam. This means you don't need to memorize exact YAML syntax — you need to know WHERE to find answers quickly. Practice navigating docs efficiently before exam day.

Explanation

The open-book policy is a game-changer but also a trap. If you don't know the docs structure, you'll waste time searching. Practice: open backstage.io/docs → find "Software Catalog" → find "Entity YAML" → find "Component" example. This should take <15 seconds with practice. Don't try to read docs from scratch during the exam — search specific keywords.

Q5
What are the 9 Backstage entity kinds? Why does the CBA test them?
Show Answer
Answer

Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User, Group, Template, Location. The CBA tests them because knowing which entity kind to use for a given scenario is fundamental to using Backstage. Expect questions like "You're modeling a microservice — which entity kind?" (Answer: Component, type: service).

Explanation

Memorize all 9. They're NOT interchangeable: a Component represents a piece of software, an API represents an API endpoint, a System groups Components, a Domain groups Systems. Common CBA trick: "You need to model a REST endpoint — use Component?" Wrong, use API kind. "You need to group 5 microservices — use Domain?" Wrong, use System (Domain is higher-level).

Q6
What are the 3 most common template actions on the CBA exam?
Show Answer
Answer

1. fetch:template — fetches template skeleton from a URL/directory. 2. publish:github — creates a GitHub repository and pushes code. 3. catalog:register — registers the new component in Backstage's catalog. These 3 form the core scaffold→publish→register pipeline.

Explanation

These 3 actions appear in almost EVERY template.yaml. The flow is always: (1) fetch the template files, (2) publish the generated code to a Git repo, (3) register the new entity in Backstage's catalog. The CBA will ask "What action publishes to GitHub?" — it's always publish:github. "What action registers in the catalog?" — catalog:register.

Q7
What should you do 1 week before the CBA exam?
Show Answer
Answer

Take a full-length timed practice exam (90 min), review all wrong answers, memorize catalog-info.yaml and template.yaml structures, review app-config.yaml sections, bookmark backstage.io/docs, test your exam workspace (camera, mic, desk), read exam rules and ID requirements.

Explanation

The week-before routine is critical: the practice exam reveals your weak areas, and you have exactly 1 week to fix them. The workspace test matters — many candidates fail the proctor check due to messy desks, poor lighting, or mic issues. Do a dry run: sit at your desk, start a timer, and simulate 90 minutes of exam conditions.

Q8
What is the 8-week CBA study plan structure?
Show Answer
Answer

Weeks 1-2: Foundations (Ch 1-6). Weeks 3-4: Catalog (Ch 7-10). Week 5: Templates (Ch 11-13). Week 6: TechDocs + Plugins (Ch 14-18). Week 7: Auth + Production + K8s (Ch 19-27). Week 8: Review + 2 full practice exams (Ch 28-32). Target: 85%+ on practice exams.

Explanation

The plan is front-loaded with foundations because everything else builds on them. Week 7 is the heaviest (9 chapters!) because Auth + Production + K8s are dense topics. Week 8 is pure review — no new content, just practice exams and filling gaps. The 85% target on practice exams gives you a 15% buffer for exam-day nerves.

Q9
What's the #1 mistake CBA candidates make, and how do you avoid it?
Show Answer
Answer

Spending too long on hard questions. If you spend 5 minutes on one hard question, you lose 3 easy ones. Avoid by: flagging immediately when stuck (>2 min), moving to Pass 3, and making your best guess if still unsure. 70% passing means you can miss 18 questions — don't sacrifice easy points for hard ones.

Explanation

This is a psychological trap: your brain wants to solve the hard problem in front of you. But the exam rewards breadth, not depth. A hard question is worth the same 1 point as an easy one. Flag it and move — you can miss 18 questions and still pass. The 2-minute rule is not a suggestion; it's your most important exam strategy.

Q10
How should you use backstage.io/docs during the exam?
Show Answer
Answer

Use it as a reference, not a crutch. Know the docs structure: /docs/features/software-catalog/ for entities, /docs/features/software-templates/ for templates, /docs/reference/config/ for app-config. Practice finding answers in <30 seconds. Don't read docs from scratch — search for specific keywords.

Explanation

The docs are your ally if used correctly. Before exam day: memorize the URL structure so you can jump directly to sections. backstage.io/docs/features/software-catalog/descriptor-format has the exact YAML format for every entity kind. backstage.io/docs/reference/config/ has every app-config option. If you can find answers in 30 seconds, the open-book policy gives you a huge advantage.

32.1 Custom Catalog Processors

Extend Backstage's catalog ingestion pipeline to pull data from ANY source — legacy CMDBs, asset management systems, spreadsheets, or custom APIs.

// Custom Entity Provider — ingest from external API
import { EntityProvider, EntityProviderConnection } from '@backstage/plugin-catalog-backend';

export class CustomCMDBProvider implements EntityProvider {
  getProviderName(): string { return 'custom-cmdb'; }

  async connect(connection: EntityProviderConnection): Promise<void> {
    // Fetch data from external CMDB
    const services = await fetch('https://cmdb.anihpj.com/api/services');
    const data = await services.json();

    // Transform CMDB data → Backstage entities
    const entities = data.map((svc: any) => ({
      entity: {
        apiVersion: 'backstage.io/v1alpha1',
        kind: 'Component',
        metadata: {
          name: svc.name,
          annotations: {
            'cmdb.anihpj.com/id': svc.id,
            'backstage.io/managed-by': 'custom-cmdb',
          },
        },
        spec: {
          type: 'service',
          lifecycle: svc.env === 'prod' ? 'production' : 'experimental',
          owner: svc.team,
          system: svc.platform,
        },
      },
    }));

    // Apply to catalog
    await connection.applyMutation({
      type: 'full',
      entities,
    });
  }
}

Use cases: Migrate from legacy developer portals, sync data from CMDB/ServiceNow, ingest from custom APIs, enrich entities with data from external systems. The catalog ingestion pipeline is fully extensible — you can add custom processors that transform, validate, or enrich entities at any stage.

32.2 Dynamic Plugins (Beta) — The Future

Dynamic plugins allow installing plugins at runtime — no Backstage rebuild required. This is the future of Backstage extensibility, enabling plugin marketplaces and faster iteration.

# app-config.yaml — Dynamic Plugin Configuration
dynamicPlugins:
  rootDirectory: /dynamic-plugins
  plugins:
    - package: '@backstage-community/plugin-github-actions'
      version: '0.6.0'
      integrity: sha512-xxx...
    - package: '@backstage-community/plugin-jenkins'
      version: '0.5.0'
      integrity: sha512-yyy...
    - package: '@internal/plugin-anihpj-dashboard'
      version: '1.0.0'
      integrity: sha512-zzz...

# Plugins are loaded at runtime — no rebuild!
# Just restart Backstage to pick up new plugins.

⚡ Why Dynamic Plugins Matter

  • 🔌 Install without rebuilding — saves 10-15 minutes per plugin install
  • 📦 Plugin marketplace — browse and install plugins like an app store
  • 🔄 Independent versioning — update plugins without updating Backstage core
  • 🏢 Multi-team — different teams can manage their own plugins independently
  • ⚠️ Currently Beta — production-ready for most plugins, but test before deploying

32.3 Backstage as a Service — Self-Hosted vs Managed

🏢 Backstage Deployment Options Comparison

OptionControlSetup TimeCostBest For
Self-Hosted (K8s)🟢 Full control🔴 1-4 weeks💰 Ops cost onlyLarge teams with K8s expertise
Spotify Portal (Managed)🟡 Limited🟢 1-2 days💰💰 Per-seatTeams wanting fast setup
Roadie🟡 Moderate🟢 1-2 days💰💰 Per-seatMid-size teams, SaaS preferers
Cortex🟡 Moderate🟢 Hours💰💰💰 EnterpriseService catalog focus
Port🟡 Moderate🟢 Hours💰💰💰 EnterpriseDeveloper portal platform

Decision matrix: Choose self-hosted if you need full customization, have K8s expertise, and want to avoid per-seat costs. Choose managed if you want to focus on building plugins, not maintaining infrastructure. Many organizations start managed and migrate to self-hosted as they scale.

32.4 Multi-Tenancy — One Backstage, Many Teams

Large organizations can run a single Backstage instance serving hundreds of teams through namespace-based isolation.

# Entity with namespace-based multi-tenancy
apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
kind: Component
metadata:
  name: payment-service
  namespace: team-payments  # ← Team isolation!
  annotations:
    backstage.io/view-url: https://github.com/anihpj/payment-service
spec:
  type: service
  lifecycle: production
  owner: team-payments

---
# Permission policy — team-scoped access
# Team A can only see namespace: team-payments
# Team B can only see namespace: team-orders

🏢 Multi-Tenancy Architecture

  • 🔑 Namespace isolation: Each team's entities live in their own namespace
  • 👁️ Permission-scoped views: Teams only see entities they have access to
  • 🎨 Per-team customization: Different sidebar, homepage, and plugins per team
  • 📊 Shared infrastructure: One Backstage instance, one database, one deployment
  • 💰 Cost efficient: No per-team Backstage instances needed

📝 Chapter 32 — CBA Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
What are custom catalog processors used for? Give a real example.
Show Answer
Answer

Extending the catalog ingestion pipeline to pull data from custom sources. Example: a CMDB processor that fetches services from a legacy CMDB API, transforms them into Backstage Component entities, and applies them to the catalog. This enables migration from legacy portals to Backstage.

Q2
What are dynamic plugins and why are they important?
Show Answer
Answer

Plugins installed at runtime without rebuilding Backstage. Important because: (1) saves 10-15 min per install, (2) enables plugin marketplaces, (3) independent versioning, (4) multi-team plugin management. Currently in beta — the future of Backstage extensibility.

Explanation

Traditional plugins: add npm package → rebuild → redeploy (15+ min). Dynamic plugins: drop in /dynamic-plugins dir → restart (30 sec). This enables plugin marketplaces, independent team plugin management, and vendor-shipped plugin updates without Backstage upgrades. The #1 most anticipated Backstage feature.

Q3
Compare self-hosted vs managed Backstage. When would you choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

Self-hosted: Full control, 1-4 weeks setup, ops cost only. Best for large teams with K8s expertise who need full customization. Managed (Roadie/Spotify/Cortex/Port): Limited/moderate control, hours-days setup, per-seat pricing. Best for teams wanting fast setup without infrastructure management.

Explanation

The decision comes down to: control vs convenience. Self-hosted gives you unlimited customization (any plugin, any theme, any integration) but requires K8s ops skills. Managed handles infra for you but limits customization. Many orgs follow a maturity path: start managed for speed → migrate to self-hosted when customization needs exceed managed capabilities.

Q4
How does namespace-based multi-tenancy work in Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

Entities are assigned to namespaces (e.g., namespace: team-payments). Permission policies restrict which users can see which namespaces. Each team sees only their own entities while sharing the same Backstage instance, database, and deployment — enabling cost-efficient multi-team portals.

Explanation

Namespaces act like folders for entities. Without multi-tenancy, all 500 teams see all 10,000 entities — overwhelming. With namespaces + permissions, Team A sees only Team A's services. The platform team manages one Backstage deployment instead of 500. This is the enterprise pattern for orgs with 50+ teams.

Q5
What is an EntityProvider in Backstage's catalog?
Show Answer
Answer

An EntityProvider is a class that implements the catalog ingestion interface. It connects to an external data source, fetches data, transforms it into Backstage entities, and applies mutations to the catalog via applyMutation. It's the foundation for custom catalog integrations.

Explanation

EntityProvider is the TypeScript interface you implement to build custom catalog connectors. The two key methods: getProviderName() (unique identifier) and connect(connection) (fetches data and applies it). The connection.applyMutation({type: 'full', entities}) call replaces all entities from this provider — existing ones are updated, new ones are added, removed ones are deleted. This is how Backstage keeps catalog in sync with external systems.

Q6
What are the benefits of multi-tenancy over running separate Backstage instances per team?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Cost: One instance instead of N. 2. Maintenance: One deployment to manage. 3. Consistency: Same plugins, theme, and configuration for all teams. 4. Cross-team visibility: Teams can discover each other's services when permitted. 5. Centralized governance: Platform team manages one Backstage.

Explanation

Running N separate Backstage instances means N databases, N deployments, N upgrade cycles. Multi-tenancy with namespaces means 1 instance serving all teams. The trade-off: less team autonomy (they share the same Backstage version and plugin set) vs massive operational savings. Most orgs with <200 teams choose multi-tenancy.

Q7
What is Backstage's status in CNCF and why does it matter?
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage is a CNCF Graduated project (2024), the highest maturity level. This means: vendor-neutral governance, strong community (27k+ GitHub stars, 200+ contributors), enterprise adoption (Spotify, Netflix, American Airlines), and long-term sustainability. Graduated status gives enterprises confidence to adopt.

Explanation

CNCF maturity levels: Sandbox (experimental) → Incubating (growing) → Graduated (production-ready, widely adopted). Graduated projects (Kubernetes, Prometheus, Envoy, Backstage) have proven governance, security practices, and community health. For enterprises, "CNCF Graduated" means it's safe to bet on Backstage for the next 10+ years.

Q8
How do dynamic plugins differ from traditional Backstage plugins?
Show Answer
Answer

Traditional: Plugins are npm packages installed at build time. Requires rebuilding and redeploying Backstage. Dynamic: Plugins are loaded at runtime from a configured directory. No rebuild needed — just restart Backstage. Dynamic plugins enable independent versioning and plugin marketplaces.

Explanation

The key difference is the deployment boundary. Traditional plugins are compiled INTO Backstage — they're part of the build artifact. Dynamic plugins are external packages loaded at runtime — they're separate artifacts. This separation means: (1) plugins can be updated independently, (2) different teams can manage different plugins, (3) plugin failures don't crash Backstage core.

Q9
What are the key considerations when migrating from a legacy developer portal to Backstage?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Data migration: Build custom catalog processors to ingest from legacy system. 2. Phased rollout: Migrate team by team, not big bang. 3. Feature parity: Identify which plugins replace legacy features. 4. Training: Teams need to learn Backstage concepts. 5. Run both in parallel during transition.

Explanation

The #1 migration mistake: "big bang" cutover. Teams lose access to the old portal while Backstage is half-configured, causing frustration. Better approach: run both portals simultaneously for 3-6 months. Migrate one team at a time, gather feedback, iterate. Build custom catalog processors that sync data FROM the legacy system INTO Backstage so both portals show the same data.

Q10
How can you get involved in the Backstage community?
Show Answer
Answer

1. GitHub: backstage/backstage — start with "good first issue" tags. 2. Discord: Active community with channels for plugins, catalog, scaffolder. 3. Community Calls: Bi-weekly, open to all. 4. BackstageCon: Annual conference. 5. Contribute plugins: Publish to backstage-community plugins. 6. Documentation: Improve docs based on your experience.

Explanation

The Backstage community is uniquely welcoming to newcomers. The "good first issue" label on GitHub is actively maintained and reserved for new contributors. Contributing to Backstage (even fixing a typo) is a great way to deepen your understanding — and it looks great on your resume. Many Backstage maintainers started as first-time contributors.

🌅 Morning Routine — Start Your Day in Backstage

Every developer at anihpj starts their day at backstage.anihpj.com. Here's what the first 5 minutes look like:

    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                  🌅 MORNING ROUTINE (5 MINUTES)                   │
    │                                                                  │
    │  1. OPEN BACKSTAGE (30 sec)                                      │
    │     https://backstage.anihpj.com → SSO login with GitHub         │
    │                                                                  │
    │  2. CHECK "MY SERVICES" DASHBOARD CARDS (1 min)                  │
    │     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
    │     │  📦 jobpost                    ✅ All pods healthy (3/3) │  │
    │     │  🔧 CI/CD Pipeline             ✅ Last build: 2 min ago  │  │
    │     │  🐛 Sentry Errors              🟡 2 new warnings         │  │
    │     │  📊 Grafana Dashboard          ✅ P95 latency: 45ms      │  │
    │     └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
    │                                                                  │
    │  3. CHECK ON-CALL STATUS (30 sec)                                │
    │     PagerDuty card → "Not on-call today ✅"                      │
    │                                                                  │

    │     Jira card → "3 tickets assigned, 1 in review"                │
    │                                                                  │
    │  5. START WORKING! 🚀                                             │
    │     Everything you need is ONE click away                        │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔑 Why This Matters

Before Backstage, this same check-in required opening 6 different browser tabs (GitHub, Jenkins, Sentry, Grafana, PagerDuty, Jira) — about 10-15 minutes of context switching. Backstage consolidates it into ONE interface, ONE login, 5 minutes. That's 10 minutes saved per developer, per day. For a team of 20 developers, that's 200 minutes (3+ hours) saved daily.

📦

🔧

🛠️ Creating a New Service — The 2-Minute Workflow

Need a new microservice? Here's the complete flow using anihpj's Django Microservice template:

    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │              🛠️ CREATING A NEW SERVICE IN 2 MINUTES               │
    │                                                                  │
    │  STEP 1: Click "CREATE" in the sidebar                           │
    │          ↓                                                       │
    │  STEP 2: Select template: "Django Microservice"                  │
    │          ↓                                                       │
    │  STEP 3: Fill the form (30 seconds)                              │
    │          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
    │          │  Name:        notification-service                  │  │
    │          │  Owner:       team-platform                        │  │
    │          │  Description: Email/SMS notification service        │  │
    │          │  Port:        8000                                  │  │
    │          │  Database:    PostgreSQL ✅                           │  │
    │          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
    │          ↓                                                       │
    │  STEP 4: Click "CREATE" → Wait 2 minutes                         │
    │          ↓                                                       │
    │  STEP 5: ✅ WHAT YOU GET (automatically!)                        │
    │          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
    │          │  📁 GitHub repo: anihpj/notification-service       │  │
    │          │  🔧 CI/CD: GitHub Actions (test → build → deploy)  │  │
    │          │  ☸️  K8s: Deployment + Service + Ingress in /k8s/  │  │
    │          │  📖 TechDocs: mkdocs scaffolded in /docs/          │  │
    │          │  🎭 Catalog: Auto-registered in Backstage!         │  │
    │          │  🔒 Security: Trivy scan + Snyk in pipeline        │  │
    │          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 Pro Tip: Template Customization

The Django Microservice template at anihpj is pre-configured with: PostgreSQL connection pooling, Prometheus metrics endpoint, structured JSON logging, health check endpoints (/healthz, /readyz), and K8s resource limits. This means every new service is production-ready from day one — no ops team involvement needed.

What happens behind the scenes: The Scaffolder executes the template's actions in sequence: (1) fetch:template pulls the Django boilerplate, (2) replaces placeholders with your input values, (3) publish:github creates the repo and pushes code, (4) catalog:register registers the new Component entity. Total automation time: ~120 seconds.

🔍 Investigating an Issue — The 15-Minute Debug Flow

Something broke in production. Here's how Backstage turns a 2-hour investigation into 15 minutes:

😰 WITHOUT Backstage — The 2-Hour Debug
  • 🔴 See error alert in Slack → "Sentry shows spike in jobpost"
  • 🔴 Open Sentry → Find error → Stack trace shows API change
  • 🔴 Open GitHub → Find repo → Check recent commits → 5 commits today
  • 🔴 Open Jenkins → Find pipeline → Check recent deploys → 10 min ago
  • 🔴 Open kubectl → Check pods → No restarts → But what changed?
  • 🔴 Ask in Slack → "Who changed the API?" → Wait 20 min for reply
  • 🔴 Find docs → Confluence → Outdated! → Check code directly
  • 📊 TOTAL: ~2 HOURS to find the root cause
🎉 WITH Backstage — The 15-Minute Debug
  • ✅ Sentry card on jobpost entity page shows error spike — click through
  • ✅ Check K8s tab: Pod status? → All healthy. Restarts? → No.
  • ✅ Check CI/CD tab: Recent deploy? → YES, 10 minutes ago!
  • ✅ Check TechDocs tab: API changed? → Breaking change in v2.1!
  • ✅ Check Ownership card: Who made the change? → team-platform
  • ✅ Rollback: Click ArgoCD card → "Rollback to v2.0" → Done!
  • ✅ Update TechDocs with migration guide → Push to repo → Auto-published
  • 📊 TOTAL: ~15 MINUTES — found AND fixed!
🔗
Connected Insights

Backstage connects Sentry errors → CI/CD deploys → K8s status → TechDocs changes — all on ONE page. The correlation that took 2 hours of manual searching is now one click away.

⏪
Instant Rollback

ArgoCD plugin lets you rollback directly from the entity page. No kubectl, no SSH — click and confirm. The deployment reverts in seconds.

📝 Daily Operations — CBA Practice Questions (5 Questions)

Q1
A new developer asks "What services does my team own?" Which Backstage feature answers this?
Show Answer
Answer

The Software Catalog — it lists all services owned by the developer's team with descriptions, owners, and links to docs, CI/CD, and K8s status. The catalog auto-groups entities by spec.owner.

Explanation

The Catalog is the foundation of Backstage. Without it, developers have no way to discover what exists. The CBA expects you to map scenarios to the correct pillar: discovery → Catalog, creation → Templates, documentation → TechDocs, extensibility → Plugins.

Q2
What happens when a developer clicks "CREATE" and runs a Software Template?
Show Answer
Answer

The Scaffolder executes template actions in sequence: (1) fetch:template pulls boilerplate code, (2) replaces placeholders with input values, (3) publish:github creates a repo and pushes code, (4) catalog:register registers the new component in the catalog. Result: production-ready repo with CI/CD, K8s manifests, and docs.

Explanation

This is the "golden path" — every new service follows the same template, ensuring consistency across the organization. Template actions are the building blocks: fetch gets the skeleton, publish deploys to Git, register adds to the catalog. Custom actions can extend this pipeline for org-specific needs.

Q3
How does Backstage reduce debugging time from 2 hours to 15 minutes?
Show Answer
Answer

By consolidating all tools on one entity page: Sentry errors, CI/CD deploy history, K8s pod status, and TechDocs all visible together. The correlation that required opening 6 different tools is now one page. The ArgoCD plugin enables instant rollback from the same interface.

Explanation

The key concept is connected insights. Before Backstage, each tool was isolated — you had to manually correlate "Sentry error at 10:05" with "Jenkins deploy at 10:00." Backstage shows them side by side on the entity page. This is the single-pane-of-glass value proposition.

Q4
A new developer ships their first commit in 2 hours with Backstage vs 2 weeks without. What enables this?
Show Answer
Answer

Three Backstage features work together: (1) Software Catalog — discover what your team owns and how services connect, (2) TechDocs — read up-to-date architecture docs alongside the code, (3) Software Templates — scaffold a test service to understand the dev workflow. No access tickets, no meeting scheduling, no wiki hunting.

Explanation

Onboarding is the highest-ROI use case for Backstage. Traditional onboarding requires: requesting access to 10+ tools (3 days), finding documentation across wikis (2 days), setting up dev environment (3 days), understanding architecture via meetings (3 days). Backstage collapses this into SSO + catalog discovery + template scaffolding = 2 hours.

A developer starts their day. Which Backstage plugins would they interact with in their first 5 minutes?

(1) K8s plugin — check pod health and replica counts, (2) CI/CD plugin (Jenkins/GitHub Actions) — verify last build passed, (3) Sentry/DataDog plugin — check for new errors, (4) PagerDuty plugin — verify on-call status, (5) Jira plugin — check assigned tickets. Q1
What metric best indicates Backstage adoption in your organization?
Show Answer
Answer

Weekly Active Users (WAU) — measures how many developers actually log in and use Backstage each week. Target: >80% of the engineering org by month 6. If developers aren't logging in, Backstage isn't delivering value regardless of how many entities are registered.

Explanation

WAU is the North Star metric for adoption because it measures actual behavior, not potential. You can register 1000 entities, but if only 10 developers visit Backstage weekly, it's failing. Track WAU alongside entity registration to ensure your catalog is driving usage.

Q2
How do you measure whether Backstage is making developers faster?
Show Answer
Answer

Track efficiency KPIs: (1) Time to 10th PR (new developer) — should drop from 2-3 weeks to 2-3 days. (2) Templates used per week — more = more self-service. (3) Services created via self-service — target >80%. (4) Documentation discovery time — from 30+ min to <30 seconds.

Explanation

Efficiency isn't just about speed — it's about eliminating friction. The CBA expects you to understand that Backstage's value isn't just "faster coding" but "less time spent finding information." The time-to-10th-PR metric is particularly powerful because it captures the entire onboarding experience.

Q3
What are quality metrics in the context of Backstage, and why do they matter?
Show Answer
Answer

Quality metrics measure how well-maintained your Backstage instance is: (1) % services with TechDocs (>90%), (2) % services with owners (100%), (3) % services with CI/CD visible (>80%), (4) catalog freshness (<24 hours). These indicate whether Backstage is a reliable source of truth.

Explanation

Quality metrics prevent the "garbage in, garbage out" problem. If only 40% of services have owners, a developer searching for "who owns X" gets incomplete results → they stop trusting Backstage. The CBA may ask about maintaining catalog quality: stale entities, missing owners, and undocumented services all degrade the developer experience.

Q4
How do you calculate the ROI of Backstage to justify the investment to leadership?
Show Answer
Answer

Calculate savings from: (1) Developer hours saved (time not spent searching/context-switching), (2) Onboarding acceleration (fewer weeks to productivity), (3) Ticket elimination (self-service replaces manual requests), (4) Faster incident resolution. For 100 developers: ~$2M/year savings vs ~$250K/year platform cost = 8x ROI.

Explanation

The CBA may present a business case scenario. Key formula: (hours saved per developer per day) × (number of developers) × (hourly cost) × (working days per year). Even 30 minutes saved per developer per day at $75/hr for 100 developers = $1.125M/year. Backstage's cost is a fraction of the savings.

Q5
What's the relationship between adoption metrics and ROI?
Show Answer
Answer

Adoption metrics are leading indicators of ROI. High WAU + high entity registration → developers are discovering services → less time searching → efficiency gains. High template usage → self-service adoption → fewer ops tickets → cost savings. Without adoption, ROI is zero regardless of how well Backstage is configured.

Explanation

This is a common CBA scenario: "You've deployed Backstage with 50 plugins and 200 entities, but WAU is 15%. Is it successful?" Answer: No — adoption drives ROI. A simpler Backstage with fewer plugins but 90% WAU delivers more value than a feature-rich instance nobody uses. Focus on adoption first, features second.
